Preface

Welcome to "Unofficial Red Hat RHCSA 8 & 9 Exam (EX200) Complete Reference," a comprehensive guide to help you prepare for and pass the Red Hat Certified System Administrator (RHCSA) certification exam. This book is designed for system administrators who want to enhance their skills and validate their knowledge of Red Hat Enterprise Linux (RHEL) 8 and 9.

The RHCSA exam measures your ability to perform essential system administration tasks in a RHEL environment, such as installing and configuring software, managing users and permissions, troubleshooting common issues, and automating tasks with scripting. It's a valuable credential for IT professionals who work with RHEL or want to advance their careers in Linux administration.

This book covers all the exam objectives in detail and provides hands-on exercises, practical tips, and real-world scenarios to reinforce your learning. Whether you're a beginner or an experienced system administrator, this book will help you master the essential skills and concepts needed to pass the RHCSA exam with confidence.

Please note that this is an unofficial reference guide and is not affiliated with Red Hat or its certification program. However, the content has been carefully crafted to align with the exam objectives and best practices in RHEL administration. You should also refer to the official Red Hat documentation, training, and support resources to supplement your learning.

I hope you find this book valuable and wish you the best of luck in your RHCSA certification journey.

Important

The RHCSA EX200 exam is a valuable certification for IT professionals who want to demonstrate their skills and knowledge in Linux system administration. Certification can help candidates stand out in a competitive job market and can lead to career advancement opportunities.

About the RHCSA exam (EX200)

The RHCSA (Red Hat Certified System Administrator) EX200 exam is a performance-based exam that measures the knowledge and skills required to configure, manage, and troubleshoot Red Hat Enterprise Linux (RHEL) systems. The exam focuses on practical skills and requires the test taker to perform real-world tasks in a live environment. The exam is administered by Red Hat, a leading provider of open-source software solutions.

The RHCSA EX200 exam consists of about 15 hands-on, practical tasks that require the test-taker to perform specific actions on a live system within a given time limit (2.5 hours) and with a passing score of 210 out of 300\. The tasks are designed to assess the candidate's ability to perform system administration tasks, such as managing users and groups, configuring file systems, installing and configuring software, and managing processes.

Note that

In the RHCSA exam, you cannot get any outside help. Only documentation provided with the product during testing is available (for example, man and info pages).

The RHCSAv8 exam is still available to be scheduled if you have a voucher or have taken the courses and prepared with an RHLS subscription. RHEL 8 testing has been officially terminated and will not be available after May 2023.

EX200 is the purchase code for the exam when you take it in a classroom. EX200K is the purchase code for individual exams you take at testing centers. But in terms of content and exam objectives, the same for both. Red hat decided not to pursue taking the exam at home because of concerns about exam security gaps.

Prologue

Imagine you're a system administrator responsible for managing a fleet of servers running Red Hat Enterprise Linux (RHEL). You have a wide range of responsibilities, from setting up new systems to troubleshooting complex issues, ensuring high availability, and automating routine tasks. You've spent countless hours mastering the intricacies of RHEL, learning how to configure services, deploy applications, secure the network, and optimize performance.

One day, you learn about the Red Hat Certified System Administrator (RHCSA) certification and realize that it's the perfect way to validate your skills and differentiate yourself from other administrators. You decide to take the challenge and sign up for the exam, confident that you'll ace it.

However, as you start preparing for the exam, you realize that it's not as easy as you thought. The exam objectives cover a vast array of topics, from basic system administration tasks to advanced concepts like shell scripting, virtualization, and storage management. You also need to know how to use various command-line tools, configure network settings, and troubleshoot issues under pressure.

That's where this book comes in. I've created "Unofficial Red Hat RHCSA 8 & 9 Exam (EX200) Complete Reference" to help you master the essential skills and knowledge needed to pass the RHCSA exam. This book covers all the exam objectives in detail and provides you with step-by-step instructions, real-world scenarios, and hands-on exercises to reinforce your learning.

I've also included practical tips, best practices, and expert advice from experienced system administrators who have passed the RHCSA exam and are now Red Hat Certified Professionals (RHCPs). They know what it takes to succeed and have shared their insights to help you avoid common pitfalls and maximize your chances of success.

I believe that this book will be an invaluable resource for anyone preparing for the RHCSA exam or seeking to enhance their RHEL administration skills. I hope you find it useful and wish you the best of luck on your certification journey.

Sincerely,

Ghada Atef

I.I Linux Administration Introduction

Linux is an operating system or kernel created by Linus Torvalds with other contributors, released on September 17, 1991, and distributed under an open-source license means programmers can use the Linux Kernel to design their own custom operating systems. 

Linux is the preferred operating system for production servers and critical systems. It has over 300 popular distributions that are being used all around the globe.

The most popular choices among Linux distributions are Red Hat, CentOS, Fedora, Ubuntu, and Debian. Ubuntu and Debian distros are used by the majority of home or desktop users. Red Hat Enterprise Linux and CentOS are two popular choices for server operating-system. 

Most web servers, mobile phones, personal computers, supercomputers, and cloud servers are powered by Linux due to their high stability, high security, and open-source environment. 

The job of a Linux systems administrator is to manage computer system operations such as maintenance, optimization, user account creation or reporting, and taking backups using Linux tools and command line interface tools.

System administration has become a powerful standard for any organization and institute that requires a solid IT foundation. Hence, the need for competent Linux administrators is a requirement of time. The job profile may change from each organization as there may be additional responsibilities and duties for the role.

Linux Administrator's main tasks

● Maintain all internet requests including DNS, RADIUS, Apache, MySQL, and PHP.

● Take regular backups, create new stored procedures, and list backups.

● Monitors systems performance.

● Analyze all errors and fix logs along with providing excellent customer support for web hosting service, ISP, and LAN customers to troubleshoot growing support issues.

● Communicating with employees, vendors, and customers professionally at all times should be one of his characteristics.

● Enhance, maintain, and create the tools for the Linux environment and its users.

● Detect and solve service problems ranging from disaster recovery to login problems.

● Install the necessary systems and security tools.

● Ensures compliance with security standards.

● Work with the Data Network Engineer and other personnel or departments to analyze hardware requirements and makes recommendations.

● Troubleshoot when a problem occurs.

◆◆◆

I.II RHEL vs CentOS

RHEL

Red Hat Enterprise Linux (RHEL) is Red Hat’s official distribution. Red Hat support services, Service Level Agreements (SLAs), and certifications are based on RHEL. 

RHEL aims to be an enterprise-level, stable, and secure operating system. RHEL major versions have a support cycle length of seven years with an option to extend up to ten years, thus being a more stable and secure operating system. Although the source code of RHEL is freely available, Red Hat restricts the redistribution of their officially supported versions of RHEL. 

To deploy RHEL on a server or workstation, you have to purchase a subscription from Red Hat and renew it every year. This subscription entitles you to additional items, such as support and patches.

CentOS

is a community-developed and supported alternative to RHEL. It is similar to Red Hat Enterprise Linux but lacks enterprise-level support. As such, it is downstream from RHEL. CentOS is more or less a free replacement for RHEL with a few minor configuration differences. You can download it directly from the official website. CentOS Linux version numbers reflect the release date of the RHEL on which it is based. 

Although you get CentOS for free, for companies that think in terms of the lifespan of their servers and software for several years, the difference between CentOS and RHEL can be significant. An important difference between RHEL and CentOS is the access to repositories. A repository is the installation source used for installing software. If you are using free software such as CentOS, the correct repositories are set up automatically. But if you are using Red Hat Enterprise Linux with a subscription, you’ll get access to the Red Hat repositories on the Red Hat Network (RHN).

CentOS Stream

Year (2020) RedHat announced that it was changing how CentOS work. These updates shifted CentOS Linux from a stable distribution that comes from a rebuild of Red Hat Enterprise Linux (RHEL) to a rolling release Linux CentOS Stream distribution.

That makes CentOS Stream the upstream, public development branch for RHEL, which you can consider as a midstream between Fedora and RHEL as it contains content planned for upcoming RHEL minor releases. The updates are not batched up into minor releases, but rather are released as they are ready.

◆◆◆

I.III VirtualBox Install

3.1 VirtualBox Introduction

Oracle Virtual Machine VirtualBox is a free and open-source hypervisor for x86 computers, currently being developed by Oracle Corporation, you can install VirtualBox on several host operating systems, including Linux, Mac OS, Windows, Solaris, and OpenSolaris.

It supports the creation and management of virtual machines running versions and derivations of Windows, Linux, BSD, Solaris, and the limited virtualization of macOS guests on Apple hardware. 

For some guest operating systems, a guest addition package of device drivers and system applications is available, which typically improves performance, especially for graphics.

3.2 VirtualBox Install

1\. Go to the VirtualBox Website.   

2\. Go to the download section. There are versions available for Windows, Mac, and Linux.  

3\. Download the Windows version at the top by clicking Windows hosts. If your host operating system is macOS X, you will click on macOS X hosts to download the .dmg file. After the download finish, open the file and follow the steps you will see.   

4\. Back to Windows users, after finishing, start the installation.  

5\. Click Run to run the installation file.  

6\. Click Yes to allow this app to make changes to your device.  

7\. Leave the default selected features and press Next to continue the setup wizard. 

Note that

● You can choose not to install USB Support, Networking, or Python support by clicking the grey icon near each option and selecting the red X, or, the Entire feature would be unavailable. 

● If this is your first time dealing with virtual machines, this will eliminate the need to install custom drivers, which makes installing and uninstalling VirtualBox easier. 

8\. Uncheck the default settings if you don't wish VirtualBox icons to appear on the desktop or the quick launch bar. However, keep the register file association box checked, this will ensure that files associated with VirtualBox open only with VirtualBox. Then press Next. 

9\. Click Yes to proceed with the installation.

10\. Click Install to install VirtualBox. 

11\. Check the Start Oracle VM VirtualBox Checkbox, then click Finish to open VirtualBox.

12\. If prompted to download the latest version of the Oracle VM VirtualBox Extension Pack, agree to the download, then install it.

◆◆◆

I.IV Creating an RHEL Virtual Machine

To create an RHEL VM, use the following procedure:

1\. Start VirtualBox  

2\. From the Menu bar, click Machine > New, Or Press Ctrl+N, Or from the Toolbar, click Tools > New to start the virtual machine wizard.  

3\. Give your virtual machine a name. If you give it RHEL, the version will automatically default Type to Linux and Version to Red Hat (64-bit), then click Next.  

4\. Select the amount of memory your Virtual Machine will use. When we choose the operating system in the previous step, VirtualBox automatically recommends the proper amount of memory to use. Note that using RAM lower than 4 GB is not recommended. At least choose 4 GB, then press Next.  

5\. Select Create a new virtual hard disk, then click Create. This opens a second wizard to create a new virtual hard disk, choose VDI.

6\. Click Next and select Dynamically allocate Memory. Selecting a memory size of 20 GB or more is recommended. Select either Fixed-Size Storage or Dynamically Expanding Storage depending upon your needs. Fixed-size storage is going to be the size of the virtual hard disk on the host Operating System (a virtual disk of 20 GB will be 20 GB on the host Operating System's hard disk). A dynamically expanding storage will be only the size of RHEL on your hard disk but will grow in size as files are added to it until it reaches its limit.  

7\. Click Next, select the size of the virtual hard disk to be 20GB.

8\. Click Create, and you should see your RHEL virtual machine in the list. 

◆◆◆

I.V RHEL 9 Install

Installing RHEL using the graphical user interface

RHEL 9 Download

You can create a Red Hat account to join the Red Hat Developer program to get the no-cost offering Red Hat Developer Subscription for Individuals, which gives developers access to all versions of Red Hat Enterprise Linux.

RHEL 9 Install

After downloading the RHEL 9 ISO file, you can install RHEL 9 on physical as well as virtual hardware.

First, install RHEL physically, this can be done using an installation DVD or a Bootable RHEL USB.

Creating a bootable RHEL USB stick from Microsoft Windows is very simple and we're going to cover the process in the next few steps.

Requirements:

● An 8GB or larger USB stick.

● Microsoft Windows 10 or later.

● Rufus, a free and open-source USB stick writing tool.

To install Rufus, go to Google search, search for Rufus, go to Rufus's website, then click the download button. After downloading Rufus, perform these steps to configure your USB device in Rufus:

1\. Start Rufus.  

2\. Insert your USB stick.

3\. Rufus will update to set the device within the Device field.  

4\. If the device is incorrect, select the correct one from the device field's drop-down menu.

5\. For best compatibility with newer hardware, keep the Partition scheme and target system type set as Master Boot Record (MBR) partition scheme for Unified Extensible Firmware Interface (UEFI). However, if you need to use the USB stick with older hardware, change this to MBR Partition Scheme for BIOS or UEFI.

6\. To select the RHEL 9 ISO file, click the optical drive icon alongside the enabled Create a bootable disk using ISO Image. This will open a file requester from which you can navigate and select the ISO file.  

7\. Leave all other parameters with their default values and click Start to initiate the writing process.

8\. If prompted that there are files to download, agree to the download. Click Yes.

9\. If you receive an alert that the download failed or the attempt to connect to the server failed. Click OK. Press the log button to show the log. From log, copy the downloading address and paste it in the Internet explorer, then press Enter. As you will see, to access the address, you need to turn on TLS 1.0, TLS 1.1, and TLS 1.2. Click on the Change Settings button, then search for TLS. Click the three TLS checkboxes. Then click OK. Close internet explorer and try again. Click the Start button. Then click Yes.

10\. You will then be alerted that Rufus has detected that the RHEL ISO is an isohybrid image which means the same image file can be used as the source for both a DVD and a USB stick without requiring conversion. Keep Write in ISO Image mode selected and click on OK to continue.

11.  Rufus will warn you that all data on your selected USB device is about to be destroyed. This is a good moment to double-check you've selected the correct device before clicking OK. After checking, click the Ok button.

The ISO will now be written to your USB stick, and the progress bar in Rufus will give you some indication of how long this will take. With a reasonably modern machine, this should take around 3 minutes. Rufus will complete the writing process and silently drop back to its default window.

Congratulations! You now have RHEL 9 on a USB stick, bootable, and ready to go.

Once you set up your USB drive to be bootable with RHEL, you can then reboot your system (catch the boot menu and boot from USB).

If your computer doesn't automatically boot from USB, try holding F12 when your computer first starts. With most machines, this will allow you to select the USB device from a system-specific boot menu. 

Second, install RHEL 9 using VirtualBox

You can install RHEL 9 using VirtualBox, I recommend you do this to keep your old Operating System as it is, so you can switch back to your old Operating System any time you want without any difficulties, and if you want to install RHEL 9 directly to your machine, there is no problem you can follow the same steps.

RHEL 9 Installation Requirements

A system with a capacity of at least 20 GB of hard disk, 4 GB of RAM and a good Internet connection.

RHEL 9 Install

1\. Start VirtualBox.  

2\. Select your RHEL virtual machine. Once you've done this, click the “Settings” button.  

3\. Click the “Storage” tab.    

4\. Click on Controller: IDE, Adds optical drive, then press the choose disk button to choose RHEL 9 ISO file, then press Open.  

5\. RHEL ISO will be mounted under the Controller device.  

6\. Click on the System tab on the left. Choose boot order and keep Optical on the top as the priority.  

7\. Press OK to return to the main window. Your RHEL machine is ready to boot now.  

8\. Select your virtual machine. Click the Start button.

9\. RHEL Virtual machine will start in a separate window and you should see the GRUB boot menu. From this menu, you can choose from different options:

a) Install RHEL 9: Choose this for a normal installation.

b) Test This Media & Install RHEL 9: Select this if you want to test the installation media before installing. Notice that this will take a significant amount of time.

c) Troubleshooting: Select this option for some troubleshooting options. This option is useful if you cannot normally boot from your computer’s hard drive anymore.

10\. Select Install RHEL 9 from the GRUB menu and press <Enter>.  

11\. Choose your preferred language and click on Continue.  

12\. Set Keyboard, Installation Source, Software Selection, Time & Date, Kdump, and Installation Destination.   

13\. Click on the Installation Destination. Select the virtual hard drive, for new Linux users Click on the chosen local hard disk, then select Automatic from the Storage Configuration Section and click on Done. For more advanced users you can select Custom from the Storage Configuration Section and click on Done. Then, make sure that the disk you want to use is selected and click Done to proceed. Choose the LVM Partition scheme then click the + sign to add a new Mount Point. LVM is short for "Logical Volume Management". You can think of Logical Volumes as "dynamic partitions", meaning that you can create/resize/delete LVM "partitions" from the command line while your Linux system is running without needing to reboot the system to make the kernel aware of the newly-created or resized partitions.

14\. For setting up the environment required in this book, I recommend using the following disk layout (based on a 40GB hard disk). You will need to configure (Recommended partitioning scheme):

● A /boot partition, recommended size at least 1 GiB, then click add mount point button. Under device type choose Standard, and Under File system choose XFS File system. Then click on Update settings. 

Note that, unlike other mount points, using an LVM volume for /boot is not possible. /boot must be located on a separate disk partition.

The XFS file system is a highly scalable, high-performance file system that supports file systems up to 16 EiB (exbibyte - approximately 16 billion GiB), files up to 8 EiB (approximately 8 billion GiB), and directory structures containing tens of millions of entries. XFS supports metadata journaling, which facilitates quicker crash recovery. The XFS file system can also be defragmented and resized while mounted and active. This file system is selected by default and is highly recommended.

● Click the + sign to add a new Mount Point / for the root partition, recommended size of 10 GiB, then click add mount point button. Under device type choose LVM, and Under File system choose XFS formatted logical volume. 

● Click the + sign to add a new Mount Point swap, with a size of 4GB logical volume that is used as swap space, recommended size of at least 2 GiB. Then click add mount point. Under device type choose LVM, and Under file system choose swap.

● Click the + sign to add a new Mount Point /home for the user home partition, here I will leave the desired capacity blank to take all the remaining sizes from the 20GB, recommended size at least 1 GiB, then click add mount point button. Under device type choose LVM, and Under File system choose XFS formatted logical volume.

● Click on the done button.

● You will see a summary of the changes we have made. Check the summary then click the accept changes button to write your configuration.

15\. Click on Network & Host Name, which is off by default, switch it on. Type in a hostname and click on Apply. Then, click on Done. 

16\. To install RHEL 9 server with a graphical user interface, don’t do anything else. But, if you want to install RHEL 9 headless server, click on Software Selection, then select Server and click on Done. Here, I will leave it with the graphical user interface. 

17\. If you want to set up your time zone, click on Time & Date, select your Region and City and click on Done.

18\. Click on Kdump to configure Kdump. Kdump is used to set up a process that is started with your server to allow you create a memory core dump in case the kernel crashes. If your server does have enough RAM available, that will result in Kdump being active automatically. If your server is low on memory, you won’t have it running by default. As Linux kernel crashes don’t occur very frequently, and setting up Kdump is not a part of the RHCSA or RHCE objectives, you can disable kdump and click the Done button. 

19\. Click on Begin Installation, and the installation should start.

20\. To enter the root password. Click on the Root Password button. Type your root password, confirm it, then click on Done. 

21\. To create a new login user, click on User Creation. Type in the new user's personal information. You can check the Make this user administrator checkbox to make this user administrator. Here, I will leave it as a normal user. Type the new user password, confirm it, then click on Done. Note that logging in with administrator privileges has a lot of risks.

22\. Once the installation is complete, click on Reboot.

Note that

● VirtualBox VM may boot from the RHEL 9 Installation DVD or ISO image again. To avoid that, click on Devices > Optical Drives > Remove disk from virtual drive. Click on Force Umount, then click on Machine > Reset to reset the VM. Click on Reset  to confirm the action, and the VM should boot from the virtual hard drive.

Adjust the Resolution to view a full-resolution RHEL VM on your computer.

1\. Click the system menu in the top-right corner to manage your system settings.

2\. Click on the system settings icon.  

3\. Click on the search icon.  

4\. Find the Displays icon and click on it.  

5\. Choose a suitable resolution for your screen.  

6\. Click the apply button.  

7\. Click the Keep Changes button.  

8\. Now, you are viewing a full-resolution RHEL/CentOS VM on your computer.

◆◆◆

I.VI VirtualBox Guest Additions Install

Installing VirtualBox Guest Additions procedure:

1\. Install kernel header packages required for VirtualBox guest Additions installation, execute the following command:   

# dnf install tar bzip2 kernel-devel-$(uname -r) kernel-headers perl gcc make elfutils-libelf-devel

2\. Insert VirtualBox guest additions ISO image by clicking Devices > Insert Guest addition CD image.  Note that, it is better to use the latest VirtualBox Guest addition CD image if possible. The ISO supplied with your VirtualBox may be outdated which might result in compilation errors.

3\. Once you insert the VirtualBox Guest addition ISO image the window will pop up. You can press the Run button to begin the installation.

Steps to start the installation from the command line:

This method may be needed to get a more verbose output from the installation process. This way, it is easier to troubleshoot any potential issues you encounter.

To do so run the following command:

$ cd /run/media/`whoami`/VB*

Change to the root user and begin the installation:

$ su

# ./VBoxLinuxAdditions.run

If successful you should see the following output:

Installing additional modules ...

Note that

● Building the VirtualBox Guest Additions kernel modules may take a while.

● Running kernel modules will not be replaced until you restart the system.

4\. After finishing successfully, reboot your system to enable the VirtualBox guest additions.  

5\. To confirm that VirtualBox guest additions are installed and loaded, use:  

# lsmod | grep vbox

◆◆◆

I.VII RHEL 9 Graphical User Interface

To work on a Linux system directly, you will need to provide a username and password. Linux systems have two basic modes for a system to run in: either quick and sober in text console mode, which looks like DOS with a mouse, multitasking, and multi-user features, or in graphical mode, which looks better but eats more system resources.

The default graphical mode of the RHEL 8 desktop environment is GNOME 3.28, and the default graphical mode of the RHEL 9 desktop environment is GNOME 40.

GNOME 40 includes a new and improved Activities Overview design that gives the overview a great new look and provides an improved experience for navigating the system and launching apps.

The graphical mode is the default nowadays on most desktop computers. You know you will connect to the system using graphical mode when you are asked for your username and password. To log in, make sure the mouse pointer is in the login window, click on your username and enter your password to the system and click OK or press Enter. It can take a little while before the graphical environment is started, depending on the CPU speed of your computer, the software you use, and your personal settings.

It is generally considered a bad idea to connect (graphically) using the root username, which is the system administrator’s account, since the use of graphics includes running a lot of extra programs, in the root's case with a lot of extra permissions, so to keep all risks as low as possible, use a normal user account to connect graphically. But there are enough risks to keep this in mind as general advice, for all use of the root account: only log in as root when extra privileges are required. 

Visual overview of GNOME 3

features a completely reimagined user interface designed to stay out of your way, minimize distractions, and help you get things done. When you first log in, you will see an empty desktop and the top bar.

The top bar provides access to your windows and applications, your calendar and appointments, and system properties like sound, networking, and power. In the system menu in the top bar, you can change the volume or screen brightness, edit your Wi-Fi connection details, check your battery status, log out or switch users, and turn off your computer.

Activities overview

To access your windows and applications, click the Activities button, or just move your mouse pointer to the top-left corner. You can also press the Super key on your keyboard. You can see your windows and applications in the overview. You can also just start typing to search your applications, files, folders, and the web.

On the left of the overview, you will find the dash. The dash shows you your favorite and running applications. Click any icon in the dash to open that application; if the application is already running, it will be highlighted. Clicking its icon will bring up the most recently used window. You can also drag the icon to the overview, or onto any workspace on the right.

Right-clicking the icon displays a menu that allows you to pick any window in a running application, or to open a new window. You can also click the icon while holding down Ctrl to open a new window.

When you enter the overview, you will initially be in the windows overview. This shows you live thumbnails of all the windows on the current workspace. Click the grid button at the bottom of the dash to display the applications overview. This shows you all the applications installed on your computer. Click any application to run it, or drag an application to the overview or onto a workspace thumbnail. You can also drag an application onto the dash to make it a favorite. Your favorite applications stay in the dash even when they’re not running, so you can access them quickly.

Start applications

There are several ways of opening an application once you’re in the Activities overview:

Start typing the name of an application — searching begins instantly. (If this doesn’t happen, click the search bar at the top of the screen and start typing.) If you don’t know the exact name of an application, try to type a related term. Click the application’s icon to start it.

Click the grid button at the bottom of the dash. You will see the frequently used applications if the Frequent view is enabled. If you want to run a new application, press the All button at the bottom to view all the applications. Press on the application to start it. 

Workspaces

are separate desktop screens that have different application windows. These can be used to organize the working environment by grouping open application windows by task. For example, windows being used to perform a particular system maintenance activity (such as setting up a new remote server) can be grouped in one workspace, while email and other communication applications can be grouped in another workspace.

There are three methods for switching between workspaces. One method is to click the indicator in the right corner of the window list and select the desired workspace. Another, perhaps the fastest, is to type Ctrl+Alt+UpArrow or Ctrl+Alt+DownArrow to switch between workspaces sequentially. A third is to switch to the Activities Overview and click the desired workspace.

You can launch an application in a separate workspace by dragging its icon from the dash, and dropping it onto one of the workspaces on the right-hand side of the screen. The application will open in the chosen workspace. You can launch an application in a new workspace by dragging its icon to the empty workspace at the bottom of the workspace switcher, or to the small gap between two workspaces.

Quickly running a command

Another way of launching an application is to press Alt+F2, enter its command name, and then press the Enter key. For example, to launch Rhythmbox, press Alt+F2 and type ‘rhythmbox’. The name of the app is the command to launch the program. Use the arrow keys to quickly access previously run commands.

Application menu

The application menu, located beside the Activities button, shows the name of the active application alongside its icon and provides quick access to windows and details of the application, as well as a quit item.

Clock, calendar & appointments

Click the clock on the top bar to see the current date, a month-by-month calendar, a list of your upcoming appointments, and new notifications. You can also open the calendar by pressing Super+M. You can access the date and time settings and open your full Evolution calendar directly from the menu.

New Boxes Features

Boxes, the GNOME application for using remote and virtual machines has several new features and enhancements for GNOME 3.28\. One major new feature is the automatic downloading of operating systems, straight from the new box assistant. To create a virtual machine, all you have to do is pick the operating system you want to use, and Boxes will do the rest.

The new version of Boxes also makes it easy to transfer files between your computer and virtual machines. To transfer files, you can either drag and drop them onto the Boxes window or select the Send Files option and select files to be transferred using a file chooser. In both cases, Boxes provides feedback about the progress of file transfers, so you know how long they are going to take, as well as when they have finished.

System menu

Click the system menu in the top-right corner to manage your system settings and your computer. When you leave your computer, you can lock your screen to prevent other people from using it. You can also quickly switch users without logging out completely to give somebody else access to the computer, or you can suspend or power off the computer from the menu.

If you have a screen that supports vertical or horizontal rotation, you can quickly rotate the screen from the system menu. If your screen does not support rotation, you will not see the button.

Lock Screen

When you lock your screen, or it locks automatically, the lock screen is displayed. In addition to protecting your desktop while you’re away from your computer, the lock screen displays the date and time. It also shows information about your battery and network status.

Window List

GNOME features a different approach to switching windows than a permanently visible window list found in other desktop environments. This lets you focus on the task at hand without distractions.

Switch between windows

You can see all the running applications that have a graphical user interface in the window switcher. This makes switching between tasks a single-step process and provides a full picture of which applications are running.

From a workspace:

Press Super +Tab to bring up the window switcher.

Release Super to select the next (highlighted) window in the switcher. Otherwise, still holding down the Super key, press Tab to cycle through the list of open windows, or Shift+Tab to cycle backward.

You can also move between the application icons in the window switcher with the → or ← keys, or select one by clicking it with the mouse. Previews of applications with a single window can be displayed with the ↓ key. From the Activities overview, click on a window to switch to it and leave the overview. If you have multiple workspaces open, you can click on each workspace to view the open windows on each workspace. 

GNOME 40 New Features

The most important improvements that have been added to GNOME 40 include:

● New look with rounded windows.

● Redesigned Settings app section.

● Improve performance and resource usage.

● Added a suspend option in the Power Off menu.

● A Do Not Disturb button is included in the notification

popup to stop notifications from appearing on the screen.

● New workspace gestures.

● Horizontal workspaces are positioned across the middle of the general view screen.

● The Favorites bar at the bottom of the screen.

● The app launcher slides up from the bottom.

● New improvements to the GNOME 40 weather widget include a new design that conveys forecast information more clearly than before.

◆◆◆

I.VIII Creating Linux Lab on VirtualBox (Server & Client)

After starting the VirtualBox, instead of creating a new RHEL 9 virtual machine, I will clone the one I have. But first, let me tell you what is cloning in Oracle VM VirtualBox Manager.

What is cloning?

Cloning is creating a full copy or a linked copy of an existing Virtual Machine. This copy is called a clone. You might use a cloned Virtual Machine to experiment with a Virtual Machine configuration, to test different guest Operating System levels, or to back up a Virtual Machine.

Clones can be full or linked depending on the amount of data copied from the source to the destination machine.

A full clone is an independent copy of a virtual machine that shares nothing with the parent virtual machine after the cloning operation. The ongoing operation of a full clone is separate from the parent virtual machine.

It does not share virtual disks with the parent virtual machine, so it generally performs better than linked clones, but it takes longer to create than linked clones. Creating a full clone can take several hours or even days if the files included are large.

A linked clone is a copy of a virtual machine that shares virtual disks with the parent virtual machine in an ongoing manner. It is a fast way to convert and run a new virtual machine. You can create a linked clone from the current state or a snapshot of a powered-off Virtual machine. This method saves disk space and lets multiple virtual machines use the same software installation.

All files available on the source machine at the moment of the snapshot continue to remain available to the linked clone. Ongoing changes to the virtual disk of the parent do not affect the linked clone, and changes to the disk of the linked clone do not affect the source machine, but a linked clone must have access to the source, and without access to the source, you cannot use a linked clone.

Here, I will make the clone linked to reduce the needed storage.

The Clone Virtual Machine Wizard

Start the wizard by selecting a virtual machine, then from the menu bar click on "Machine", then select “Clone” from the drop-down menu, or in the "Snapshots" view of the selected VM select “Clone”, or directly select “Clone” from the toolbar menu.

Specify a new Name for the clone. I suggest making the first a "server" and the clone a client. You can choose a Path for the cloned virtual machine; otherwise, Oracle VM VirtualBox uses the default machines folder.

The Clone Type option specifies whether to create a clone linked to the source VM or to create a fully independent clone:

● Full Clone: Copies all dependent disk images to the new VM folder. A full clone can operate fully without the source VM. The Snapshots option specifies whether to create a clone of the current machine state only or of everything.

*   Everything: Clones the current machine state and all its snapshots.

*   Current Machine State: Clones a VM snapshot and all its child snapshots. Notice, if you select the current state of the source VM as the clone point, Oracle VM VirtualBox creates a new snapshot.

●        Linked Clone: Creates new differencing disk images based on the source VM disk images.

In the “Additional options” section, the available clone options are:

●        MAC Address Policy: Specifies how to keep network card MAC addresses when cloning the VM.

For example, the Generate new MAC addresses for all network adapters value assigns a new MAC address to each network card during cloning. This is the default setting. This is the best option when both the source VM and the cloned VM must operate on the same network as in our case. Other values enable you to keep the existing MAC addresses in the cloned VM.

● Keep Disk Names: Keep the disk image names when cloning the VM. Typically, do not check as I want new names for the clone.

● Keep Hardware UUIDs: keeps the hardware universally unique identifiers (UUIDs) when cloning the VM. Typically, do not check as I want new UUIDs for the clone.

Notice, the Clone menu item is disabled while a machine is running.

After finishing, you can follow these steps to group the Linux virtual machines to work together.

There are two available options for making groups:

● Drag one VM on top of another VM.  

● Or select the VMs you want to make as a group by pressing Ctrl and clicking on the mouse Left-Click, then select Group from the right-click menu.

Now, Let’s configure the virtual machine networks to work as if they are on the same network with the host operating system:

1\. Click on your virtual machine.

2\. Click on Settings.

3\. Choose Network.

4\. Leave network 1 as it is (Nat).

5\. Choose advanced: and generate a new random mac address by clicking on the circle arrow button.

6\. Go to network 2, Change the type of the network to Bridged.

The difference between the three options is:

● Host-only only permits network operations with the Host OS.  

● NAT mode will mask all network activity as if it came from your Host OS, although the VM can access external resources.  

● Bridged mode replicates another node on the physical network and your VM will receive its own IP address if The Dynamic Host Configuration Protocol (DHCP) is enabled in the network.

7\. Choose advanced: here also generate a new random mac address.

The promiscuous mode makes it possible for a physical network adapter to have multiple MAC addresses, allowing all incoming traffic to pass the physical network adapter of the host machine and reach the virtual network adapter of the VM which has its own MAC address that is represented on the host adapter, even if that traffic is not addressed to the virtual network adapter of that particular VM. The promiscuous mode is useful for network testing and security audits. 

There are three options for using the promiscuous mode:

● Deny: Any traffic that is not intended for the virtual network adapter of the VM is hidden from the VM. This option is set by default and is what I will use.

● Allow VMs: All traffic is hidden from the VM network adapter except the traffic transmitted to and from other VMs.

● Allow All: There are no restrictions in this mode. A VM network adapter can see all incoming and outgoing traffic.

Note that

● The Promiscuous mode can be used not only for the Bridged network mode but also for NAT Network, Internal Network, and Host-Only Adapter modes.

8\. Save and close.

9\. Select the other virtual machine, then click on “Settings.”

10\. Repeat the same steps, we have done on the other virtual machine

11\. Save and close.

12\. Start the group of virtual machines.

13\. After logging in, enable both of the available network connections. 

14\. You can check the network interface information using either of these commands the old “ifconfig”, “ip addr show”, or “nmcli dev show”. 

nmcli stands for Network Management Command-Line Interface and is a tool that allows controlling NetworkManager and reporting its status.

16\. Let’s check the connection with other systems. We can do this using the ping command.

17\. First, try pinging the host Operating system.

18\. Then, ping the other virtual machine on the same network.

19\. Also, you can try pinging the internet, for example, ping google.com

20\. OK.

Hostname Changing

To change your hostname, you can modify the “/etc/hostname” file, or you can use the “hostnamectl” command. 

Before changing the hostname, let’s, first view it by using the command:

# cat /etc/hostname

Or the command:

$ echo $HOSTNAME

Or the command:

$ hostnamectl status

Which also shows the machine ID, the boot ID, the Operating system, and the Kernel Version.

Now, to set a new hostname on your system, you can enter this command as root:

# hostnamectl set-hostname name

To check:

$ hostnamectl status

Now, we finished creating our lab successfully.

Chapter II: Understand & Use Essential Tools

Chapter II Contents

● II.I Access a shell prompt and issue commands with the correct syntax

● II.II Use input-output redirection (>, >>, |, 2>, etc.)

● II.III Use grep and regular expressions to analyze text

● II.IV Access remote systems using SSH

● II.V System targets

● II.VI Archive, compress, unpack, and uncompress files using tar, star, gzip, and bzip2

● II.VII Create and edit text files

● II.VIII Create, delete, copy, and move files and directories  

● II.IX Create hard and soft links

● II.X List, set, and change standard ugo/rwx permissions   

● II.XI Locate, read, and use system documentation including man, info, and files in /usr/share/doc

◆◆◆

II.I Access a shell prompt and issue commands with the correct syntax

1.1 Terminal vs Shell 

Before starting, let me tell you the difference between a terminal and a shell. 

A terminal refers to the program that runs a shell. While a shell is a program that processes commands and returns the output.

The shell is much more than just a command interpreter, it is also a programming language of its own with complete programming language constructs such as conditional execution, loops, variables, functions, and many more. That is why the GNU Linux shell is more powerful compared to the Windows shell.

Note that

● Different shells for Linux are available, but bash is the common shell. So, when I am talking about “the shell” in this book, I will be talking about the bash shell.

If you are managing a Linux system, terminal access is essential. If your system defaults to the multi-user.target, you will get a login prompt and you will directly access the text mode or the command-line interface (CLI). To get a prompt enter your username and password. 

Notice, that Linux doesn't display any password placeholder characters.

In the case of the system defaults to the graphical.target, you will get a GNOME login window. Select your username to be prompted for your password, then press the Sign In button to get to the desktop. To get to the command prompt Click on the Activities menu in the upper-left, then click on the Terminal icon in the side menu in the GNOME shell. This will launch the GNOME Terminal, and you will get a command prompt. Or Click on Activities, click on Show Applications, then click on All, and from Utilities click on Terminal.

The terminal window is your control panel for the system. Almost everything that follows is done using this simple but powerful text tool. A terminal window should always show a command prompt when you open it. Once you launch the Terminal, you would find something like “Sam@rhel.server:~$” written on it.

Sam@rhel.server:~$

● Sam: is the name of the user.

● rhel.server: is the computer name or the hostname which we added during the installation.

Important

● The hostname helps identify a computer over the network. In a server environment, the hostname is essential. You can use the command ‘echo $HOSTNAME’ to check your hostname.

The telda ~ sign

The telda ~ sign shows that the user is working in the home directory, you can check the current path using the pwd command. 

Notice, that if you change the directory this sign will vanish. For example, if we moved from /home directory to /bin directory using the 'cd' command. The ‘~’ sign will be changed with the current directory name. It will appear again when moving back to the home directory either by using the command cd /home/username or by typing the command cd only, or by typing the command cd ~.

The $ sign

The $ sign suggests that you are working as a standard user. But if you switched to the root user by using the ‘su‘ command a # will be displayed and the username will be changed to root.

1.2 Commands entered at the shell prompt have three basic parts:

● Command to run.

● Options to adjust the behavior of the command.

● Arguments that are typically targets of the command.

The command is the name of the program to run. It may be followed by one or more options, which adjust the behavior of the command or what it will do. Options normally start with one or two dashes (e.g., - a or - - all) to distinguish them from arguments. Commands may also be followed by one or more arguments, which often indicate a target that the command should operate on.

1.3 Kinds of Commands:

●        Aliases

●        Internal commands

●        External commands

Alias

A shell alias is a shortcut to reference a command. It can be used to avoid typing long commands. For common patterns, it can reduce keystrokes and improve efficiency. A simple example is setting default options on commands to avoid having to type them each time a command is run. Some aliases are provided by default; You can type alias on the command line to get an overview. 

To define an alias, use:

alias newcommand=’oldcommand’, as in the default alias ll=’ls -l --color=auto’.

Notice, aliases are executed before anything else.

Internal Commands vs External commands

Bash built-in commands (also known as "internal commands") are part of the shell itself. Each built-in command is executed directly in the shell itself, instead of an external program that the bash would load and run. You can show the list of built-in commands using the help command.

An example of an internal or built-in command is the alias command, while the top command is an example of an external command.

To find out whether a given command is an internal built-in or external, you can use the type command. For example: 

$ type -a cd

$ type -a top

The PATH Environment Variable 

The $PATH is an environmental variable that tells the shell which directories the executable programs are located in when a user enters a command. To show the PATH variable content type:

$ echo $PATH

The which command

To find out which exact command the shell will be using, you can use which command. For instance, to find out where the shell will get the ls command from, run:

$ which ls

Notice that, for security reasons, the current directory is not in the $PATH variable and that Linux does not look in the current directory to see whether a specific command is available from that directory. That is why you need to start a command that is in the current directory but nowhere in the $PATH by including ./ in front of it. Where the dot stands for the current directory. By running it as ./, you’ll tell bash to look for the command in the current directory. 

Note that

● You can get an overview of the current variables defined in your shell environment by using the env command.

When you want the system to execute a command, you rarely have to give the full path to that command. For example, we know that the ls command is in the /bin directory (you can check with which -a ls), yet we don't have to enter the command /bin/ls for the computer to list the content of the current directory.

The PATH environment variable takes care of this. This variable lists those directories in the system where executable files can be found, and thus saves the user a lot of typing and memorizing locations of commands.

So, the path naturally contains a lot of directories containing bin somewhere in their names, to check the type. 

$ echo $PATH  

These paths are subsequently searched for the required program. As soon as a match is found, the search is stopped, even if not every directory in the path has been searched. If you use programs in other directories more frequently, you can change your path to look in your directories, for example, to add your /home/Downloads directory:

$ export PATH=$PATH:/home/Downloads

Here Changes are not permanent! When using the export command in a shell, the changes are temporary and only valid for this session (until you log out). Opening new sessions, even while the current one is still running, will not result in a new path in the new session.

To make it permanent you need to add:

export PATH=$PATH:/path/to/dir to your home/sam/.profile file or home/sam/.bashrc file.

For example, to add home/Downloads to the PATH variable in the home/sam/.bashrc file. Here, I will use the nano editor to open the .bashrc file for editing $ nano .bashrc, and at the end of this file add:

export PATH=$PATH:/home/Downloads

Notice, I put $PATH to keep the old PATH as it is or it will be removed and you will only see the /home/Downloads path. Press Ctrl+X. Followed by pressing Y to confirm saving. Now, press Enter to confirm using the same name and exit.

Depending on what you're doing, you also may want to symlink to binaries:

$ cd /usr/bin

$ ln -s /path/to/binary binary-name

This will not automatically update your path for the remainder of the session. To do this, you should run:

$ source ~/.bashrc

or

$ source ~/.profile

Notice, sometimes you will need to log out and log in again to see the effect.

Important

● In case you want the updates to be working for all users or the root user, you will do the same steps but on the /etc/profile file or the /etc/bashrc file.

1.4 Environment Configuration Files

Generally, environment variables can be in three categories:

1\. Local Environment Variables

These environment variables last only for the current session and are not in the configuration files. For example, you can use the export command on the terminal to set a temporary environment variable. That variable will be accessible on that terminal and vanish when you close it.

$ export NAME="sam"

$ echo $NAME

2\. User Environment Variables

These variables are defined for a particular user and are loaded every time this user logs in using a local terminal session or a remote login session. These variables are typically set in and loaded from the following configuration files: .bashrc, and .bash_profile files present in the user’s home directory.

3\. System-wide Environment Variables

These are the environment variables available system-wide for all the users on that system. These variables are present in system-wide configuration files present in the following directories and files: /etc/environment, /etc/profile, /etc/profile.d/, /etc/bashrc. These variables are loaded every time the system starts and a user logs in, either locally or remotely.

The /etc/profile file

is a system-wide environment and startup program, for login setup. The file content is executed as a shell script only when a login shell is created.

To add a variable in the /etc/profile file, open it using your preferred text editor:

# vim /etc/profile

Press I to enter the insert mode, then type the following:

$ export NAME="sam"

Press ESC to switch to the command mode, then type "wq" to save and exit.

To read and execute the  /etc/profile file content in the current shell, run:

# source /etc/profile

To verify, run:

# echo $NAME

Note that

● Creating a shell script (a file with extension .sh) in /etc/profile.d directory instead of editing the /etc/profile file is recommended.

The /etc/environment file

is a system-wide configuration file used to set environment variables and is not a shell script.

Note that if you run a non-interactive command with OpenSSH, you will see variables from the /etc/environment but not from the /etc/profile.

The /etc/bashrc file

is used for system-wide functions and aliases and is executed for both interactive (A shell you use to type commands) and non-interactive shells (A shell running a script).

1.5 Using /etc/motd and /etc/issue

Bash offers an option to include messages in the /etc/motd and the /etc/issue files.

Messages in /etc/motd display after a user has successfully logged in to a shell.

Note that

● Users in a graphical environment do not see its contents after a graphical login.

● Using /etc/motd can be a convenient way for system administrators to inform users.

Another way to send information to users is by using /etc/issue. The contents of this file are displayed before the user logs in. This provides an excellent means of specifying specific log in instructions to users who are not logged in yet using a text user interface. For example, nano /etc/issue. Add some data. Press Ctrl+X. Press Y to confirm saving. And press Enter to confirm using the same name and exit. Now Press Ctrl+Alt+F3. You will see the data we have entered is appearing.

1.6 Using Special Key

Using Bash features, several special key combinations allow you to do things easier and faster with the GNU shell, Bash, which is the default on almost any Linux system, you are strongly suggested to make a habit out of using them, to get the most out of your Linux experience from the very beginning.

Popular Special keys Combinations:

● Press Ctrl+A to Move the cursor to the beginning of the command line.

● Press Ctrl+C to End a running program and return the prompt.

● Press Ctrl+D to Log out of the current shell session, it is equal to typing exit, and if you are using the user root it will switch to the regular user.

● Press Ctrl+E to Move the cursor to the end of the command line.

● Press Ctrl+H to Generate a backspace character.

● Press Ctrl+U Clear from the cursor to the beginning of the command line.

● Press Ctrl+k Clear from the cursor to the end of the command line.

● Press Ctrl+L to Clear this terminal.

● Press Ctrl+R to Search in the command history for a particular command.  

● Press ArrowLeft or ArrowRight to move the cursor one place to left or right on the command line so you can insert the characters anywhere other than just the beginning and the end.

● Press ArrowUp or ArrowDown to Browse history. Go to the line you want to repeat, edit details if necessary, and press Enter to save time.

● Press Shift+PageUp or Shift+PageDown to browse the terminal buffer (to see text that has "scrolled off" the screen). For example, cat /etc/passwd

● Press the Tab key for filename completion; when multiple choices are possible, the system will either signal with an audio or visual bell or do nothing.

● Pressing the Tab key twice will show you file or command completion possibilities.

Tab completion allows a user to quickly complete commands or file names once they have typed enough at the prompt to make it unique. 

ec<tab>

If the characters typed are not unique, pressing the Tab key twice displays all commands that begin with the characters already typed.

$ pas<Tab><Tab>

There are other several other command line-editing commands that you can find on the bash(1) man page.

1.7 Quick Start Commands

You Should Now That!

● Each string typed into the shell can have up to three parts: the command, options (that begin with a - or --), and arguments.      

● Each word typed into the shell is separated from the other with spaces.

● Commands are the names of programs that are installed on the system.  

● Each command has its options and arguments.

● The Enter key is pressed when a user is ready to execute a command.

● Each command is typed on a separate line and the output from each command displays before the shell displays a prompt. 

● If a user wants to type more than one command on a single line, a semicolon (;), can be used as a command separator. 

● A semicolon is a member of a class of characters called meta-characters that has special meanings for the bash shell.

Launch the terminal to have a look at the QuickStart commands!

The date command

is used to display the current date and time:

$ date

It can also be used by the super-user to set the system clock. An argument that begins with a plus sign (+) specifies a format string for the date command.

For example, we use the command date +%upper case R to show the time formatted hour:minutes.

$ date +%R

And we use the command date +%x to show the date formatted month/day/year.

$ date +%x

The pwd command

print working directory command is used for displaying the current working directory. 

$ pwd

The cd command

The change directory command is used for changing directories.

$ cd

Navigating to the home directory:

If you want to navigate to the home directory then type any of the following commands followed by Enter:

$ cd

OR

$ cd ~

Moving to the root directory:  

To move to the root directory, use:

$ cd /

Moving up one directory level:

For navigating up one directory level, use: 

$ cd ..

The passwd command

The passwd command changes a user's password. The original password for the account must be specified before a change will be allowed. By default, passwd is configured to require a strong password, consisting of lowercase letters, uppercase letters, numbers, and symbols, and is not based on a dictionary word.

Notice, the super-user can use the passwd command to change other users' passwords.

For example, to change my password as user sam:

$ passwd

As the root user, to change the user Sam password type the command: 

# passwd Sam

The file command

Linux does not require file name extensions to classify files by type. The file command scans the beginning of a file's contents and displays what type it is. The files to be classified are passed as arguments to the command. For example:

$ file /etc/passwd

$ file /bin/passwd

$ file /home

The head and tail commands

The head and tail commands display the beginning and end of a file respectively. By default, these commands display 10 lines, but they both have a -n option that allows a different number of lines to be specified. The file to display is passed as an argument to these commands. For example:

$ head /etc/passwd

And to show the last 3 lines in the passwd file you can use the command:

$ tail -n3 /etc/passwd

Or

$ tail -3 /etc/passwd

The wc command

The wc command counts lines, words, and characters in a file. It can take a -1, -w, or -c option to display only the lines, words, or characters, respectively. For example:

$ wc /etc/passwd

And to show the number of lines:

$ wc -1 /etc/passwd

And to show the number of characters in two files and the total of the characters in the two files:

$ wc -c /etc/group /etc/hosts

The cat command

The cat textfile command: throws the content of any text file on the screen. For example: 

$ cat /etc/hosts

The exit command

The exit command is used to leave this session. 

$ exit

The ls command

The ls command is used to list the specified directory content. For example, 

$ ls /etc

where the directory /etc is the argument to the ls command. This indicates that you want to see the content of that directory, instead of the default, which would be the content of the current directory, obtained by just typing ls followed by pressing Enter. Some commands require arguments, sometimes arguments are optional.

Note that

● You can find out whether a command takes options and arguments, and which ones are valid, by checking the online help for that command, which we will discuss later. 

The history command

displays a list of previously executed commands prefixed with a command number.

$ history

The exclamation point character "!"

The exclamation point character "!" is a metacharacter that is used to expand previous commands without having to retype them. ! number expands to the command matching the number specified. ! string expands to the most recent command that begins with the string specified. For example:

$ !ls

Or you can redo a command that was done before by specifying it’s number from the history after the exclamation mark, for example, to redo the fifth command in the history output, you can type:

$ !5

The Arrow Keys

can be used to navigate through previous command lines in the shell's history. Up Arrow edits the previous command in the history list. Down Arrow edits the next command in the history list. Use this key when the Up Arrow has been pressed too many times. The Left Arrow and Right Arrow move the cursor left and right in the current command line being edited.

Important

● Do not forget the space between cd and /. Otherwise, you will get an error.

● The root of the file system in Linux is denoted by '/'.

● In Windows, you use the backward slash "\" while in Linux systems, the forward slash is used "/".

1.8 Navigating through Multiple Directories

You can navigate through multiple directories at the same time by specifying their complete path. For example, if you want to move to the cpu directory under the /dev directory, you do not need to break this operation into two parts. Instead, you can type 'cd /dev/cpu' to reach the directory directly.

1.9 Relative and Absolute Paths

A path in computing is the address of a file or folder.

For Example:

/home/sam/Downloads

This is the path for the Downloads directory. 

There are two kinds of paths:

1) First Absolute Path:

Let's say you have to browse the images stored in the Pictures directory of the home folder 'sam'. The absolute file path of Pictures directory /home/sam/Pictures, to navigate to this directory, you can use the command cd /home/sam/Pictures. This is called the absolute path as you are specifying the full path to reach the file.

2) Second Relative Path:

The relative path comes in handy when you have to browse another subdirectory within a given directory. It saves you from the effort to type complete paths all the time.

Suppose you are currently in your Home directory cd /home/sam and you want to navigate to the Downloads directory. You don’t need to type the absolute path cd /home/sam/Downloads, instead, you can simply type cd Downloads and you would navigate to the Downloads directory as you are already present within the /home/sam directory.

This way you do not have to specify the complete path to reach a specific location within the same directory in the file system.

◆◆◆

II.II Use input-output redirection (>, >>, |, 2>, etc.)

Redirecting Output to a File or Program

Objectives

After completing this section, you should be able to:

● Describe the technical terms standard input, standard output, and standard error.

● Use redirection characters to control output to files.

● Use piping to control output to other programs.   

Standard Input, Standard Output, and Standard Error

You should know that!

● A process structure is constructed with numbered channels called (file descriptors) to manage open files.

● Processes connect to files to reach data content or devices these files represent.

● Processes are created with default connections for channels 0, 1, and 2, known as standard input, standard output, and standard error. 

● Processes use channels 3 and above to connect to other files.

![File Descriptors](image_rsrcZV0.jpg)

Redirecting Output to a File

Channel redirection replaces default channel destinations with file names representing either output files or devices. Using redirection process output and error messages can be captured as file contents sent to a device or discarded.

Redirecting stdout suppresses process output from appearing on the terminal. Redirecting only stdout does not suppress stderr error messages from displaying on the terminal. The special file /dev/null quietly discards channel output redirected to it.

◆◆◆

II.III Use grep and regular expressions to analyze text

The grep command

Suppose you want to search a particular information such as the postal code from a text file. You may manually skim the content yourself to trace the information. A better option is to use the grep command. It will scan the document for the desired information and present the result in the required format.

The grep command syntax:

$ grep [options] pattern [file]

Let's take an example:

To show the content of the file sample, use:

$ cat sample

To use the grep command to search for the string 'apple' in the file 'sample', use:

$ cat sample | grep apple

Options used with the grep command:

● The -v option shows all the lines that do not match the searched string. 

● The -c option displays only the count of matching lines. 

● The -n option shows the matching line and its number. 

● The -i option is used to match both (upper and lower) cases. 

● The -l option shows the name of the file with the string.

For example, you can use the '-i' option with grep to filter the string 'a' (case-insensitive) from all of the lines:

$ cat sample | grep - i a

Matching File Names Using Path Name Expansion

File globbing "path name expansion"

The Bash shell has a path name-matching capability historically called globbing. The Bash globbing feature, commonly called pattern matching or "wildcards" makes managing large numbers of files easier. Using meta-characters (Wildcards) that expand to match filenames and paths being searched commands are executed on a focused set of files simultaneously.

Pattern matching Globbing is a shell command-parsing operation that expands a wildcard pattern into a list of matching path names. Command-line meta-characters are replaced by the match list prior to command execution. Patterns, especially square-bracketed character classes, that do not return matches display the original pattern request as literal text. 

These are the common meta-characters and pattern classes. You can Take a look at them before starting the examples.

![Common Meta-Characters and Pattern Classes](image_rsrcZV1.jpg)

Let's create some files to help us demonstrate expansion.

$ mkdir glob; cd glob

$ touch apple bag cat dog egg fat goat hat ice jack

$ ls

Example of simple pattern matches using “*”:

An asterisk (*) – matches one or more occurrences of any character, including no character. For example:

$ ls a*

$ ls *a*

$ ls [ac]*

Example of simple pattern matches using “?”:

Question mark (?) – represents or matches a single occurrence of any character. For example:

$ ls ???

$ ls ????

Tilde expansion

The tilde character (~) - when followed by a slash delimiter, matches the current user home directory. When followed by a string of characters up to a slash, it will be interpreted as a username if one matches. If no username matches, then an actual tilde followed by the string of characters will be returned.

For example:

$ echo ~/glob

Here, the ~ sign represents the path to the current user home directory because it was followed by a slash delimiter.

$ echo ~sam

And here ~ represents the path to the current user home directory because it was followed by a string of characters that matched the username sam.

$ echo ~glob

But here, a tilde sign followed by the string glob will appear if no username matches.

$ echo ~+

This command expands to the value of the PWD variable, which holds the current working directory.

$ echo ~-

This command expands to the value of OLDPWD variable, which holds the previous working directory. If OLDPWD is unset, ~- is not expanded.

Brace expansion

is used to generate discretionary strings of characters. Braces contain a comma-separated list of strings or a sequence expression inside curly braces "{}". A sequence consists of a starting and ending item separated by two periods "..". The result includes the text preceding or following the brace definition. Brace expansions may be nested, one inside another. 

For example:

$ echo {sat, sun, mon}.log

$ echo file{1..4}.txt

$ echo file{a..d}.txt

$ echo file{a,b}{1,3}.txt

$ echo file{a{1,2},b}.txt

Command substitution

allows the output of a command to replace the command itself. Command substitution occurs when a command is enclosed with a beginning dollar sign and parenthesis $ (command), or with backticks `command`. The form with backticks is older and has two disadvantages, first, it can be easy to visually confuse backticks with single quote marks, and second backticks cannot be nested inside backticks. The $ (command) form can nest multiple command expansions inside each other.

For example:

$ echo today is `date +%A`.

Here I used %A for the full weekday name of the local area.

$ echo the time is $(date +%T).

And Here, I used the %T to show time as %H:%M:%S

Protecting arguments from expansion

Many characters have special meanings in the Bash shell. To ignore meta-character special meanings, single quoting and escaping are used to protect them from shell expansion. The backslash (\) is an escape character in Bash, protecting the single following character from special interpretation. To protect longer character strings, a single ( ' ) quote is used to enclose strings.

Use double quotation marks to suppress globbing and shell expansion, but still allow command and variable substitution. Variable substitution is a concept usually identical to command substitution but may use optional brace syntax.

For example:

$ echo the username is $USER.

$ echo ‘the username is $USER’.

Here using the single quote character prevented the $ character from interpretation “meaning that it prevented parameter expansion”.

$ echo the username is \$USER.

Using the \ escape character prevented the $ character from interpretation “meaning that it prevented parameter expansion”.

$ echo “the username is $USER”.

But here, using the double quotation allowed the $ character to interpret.

$ echo “~”

And here, using the double quote prevented pathname expansion “globbing”.

Use single quotation marks to interpret a text literally. Observe the difference, on both screen and keyboard, between the single quote ( ' ) and the command substitution backtick ( ` ). Besides suppressing globbing and shell expansion, quotations direct the shell to additionally suppress command and variable substitution. The question mark is a meta-character that also needed protection from expansion.

For example:

$ echo ‘$HOSTNAME?’

$ echo “$HOSTNAME?”

◆◆◆

II.IV Access remote systems using SSH

4.1 Accessing the Remote Command Line with SSH

Objective

After completing this section, you should be able to log into a remote system using ssh to run commands from a shell prompt.

What is Secure Shell (SSH)?

Secure Shell (SSH) is a cryptographic network protocol for operating network services securely over an unsecured network. SSH provides a secure channel over an unsecured network by using a client-server architecture. 

Note that

● SSH runs at TCP/IP port 22.

● Typical applications include remote command line, login, and remote command execution, but any network service can be secured with SSH. 

● Graphical applications can also be run securely over SSH from a remote location.

● SSH was designed as a replacement for unsecured remote shell protocols such as Telnet.

What is the OpenSSH secure shell (SSH)?

OpenSSH is an open-source implementation of the SSH protocol that is used in Linux systems. The OpenSSH Secure Shell (ssh) is used to securely run a shell on a remote system. If you have a user account on a remote Linux system providing SSH services, ssh is the command normally used to remotely log into that system. The ssh command can also be used to run an individual command on a remote system.

Secure Shell examples

Let’s take some examples of using the ssh command for remote login and remote execution:

The ssh command syntax used to log into a remote machine is very simple:

$ ssh user_name@hostname/IPaddress

As you see, the ssh command syntax consists of three different parts:

●    ssh command instructs the system to establish an encrypted secure connection with the host machine.

●    user_name represents the account that is being accessed on the host.

●    hostname refers to the machine being accessed. It can be a hostname or an IP address.

Let’s try accessing the previously created server as the user sam:

# ssh sam@rhel.server

If this is the first time you use ssh to connect to this remote server, you will see an alert message, at the end of it, and you will be asked to type yes to continue. Typing yes will add the server to your list of known hosts (~/.ssh/known_hosts) as seen in the message.

Note that

● Each server has a host key and the above question is related to verifying and saving the host key, so that the next time you connect to the server, it can verify that it is the same server.

● If a server's public key is changed because the key was lost due to hard drive failure, or replaced for other reasons, users will need to update their ~/.ssh/known_hosts files and remove the old entry to log in.

Type the user sam password on the server then press Enter. Once authentication has been accepted, you will be at the shell prompt for the remote machine. After logging into the server, commands will work as if they were written directly to it.

4.2 Executing remote commands on the server

Also, you can use the ssh command to remotely execute commands on the remote machine without logging into a shell prompt. The syntax for this is:

$ ssh hostname command

For example, to create a file test on the "rhel.server", type:

$ ssh centos.server touch test

Another example:

To show the hostname of the remote server on the local display without logging in the remote server type:

# ssh root@centos.server hostname

4.3 The w Command

The w command displays a list of users currently logged into the computer. This is especially useful to show which users are logged in using ssh from which remote locations, and what they are doing.

4.4 Configuring SSH Key-based Authentication

Objective

After completing this section, you should be able to set up SSH to allow secure logins without passwords by using a private authentication key file.

SSH key-based authentication

SSH keys provide an easy and secure way of logging into your server without a password by using public key authentication. It allows users to authenticate using a private-public key scheme. You will first need to generate two keys on your local system, a private key ~/.ssh/id_rsa and a public key ~/.ssh/id_rsa.pub, which are generated using the ssh-keygen command. The private key file is used as the authentication credential, you can consider it as a password, which must be kept secret and secure on your local system. The public key is copied to systems that you want to log into and is used to verify the private key. 

To generate the public and private keys type:

# ssh-keygen

Or you can specify the type of the generated key with the -t option.

# ssh-keygen -t rsa

Note that

● If the ssh-keygen command is invoked without any arguments, it will generate an RSA key.

The utility will prompt you to select a location for the keys that will be generated. By default, the keys will be stored in the .ssh directory within your user’s home directory. Press ENTER to accept the default.

Important

● It is best to stick with the default location to allow your SSH client to automatically find your SSH keys when attempting to authenticate. 

Note that

● If you had previously generated an SSH key pair, you may see a prompt to Overwrite. If you choose to overwrite the key on the disk, you will not be able to authenticate using the previous key anymore.

Next, you will be prompted to enter an optional passphrase which must be provided to access your private key. In the event the private key is stolen, it is very difficult for someone other than the issuer to use it when protected with a passphrase. This adds enough of a time buffer to make a new key pair and remove all references to the old keys before the private key can be used by an attacker who has cracked it. If you do not want to set a passphrase, you can press ENTER twice to bypass this prompt.

Note that
