● It is always wise to passphrase-protect the private key since the key allows access to other machines. However, this means the passphrase must be entered whenever the key is used, making the authentication process no longer passwordless. 

Once the SSH keys have been generated, they are stored by default in the .ssh/ directory of your home directory as follows:

●       ~/.ssh/id_rsa : identification (private) key with Permissions 600.

●       ~/.ssh/id_rsa.pub : public key with Permissions 644.

If the file permissions aren’t set to these values, you can change them using the chmod command.

Also, it is better to change the ~/.ssh directory permission to 700 using the chmod command.

$ chmod 700 ~/.ssh

Before key-based authentication can be used, the public key ~/.ssh/id_rsa.pub needs to be copied to the destination system in the ~/.ssh/authorized_keys file. This can be done using the command ssh-copy-id. The syntax is:

$ ssh-copy-id username@remote_host

Or you can specify the pasting location on a remote machine by using the command:

$ ssh-copy-id -i ~/.ssh/id_rsa.pub [root@rhel.server](mailto:root@rhel.server)

Type in the password and press ENTER. The utility will connect to the account on the remote host using the password you provided. It will then copy the contents of your ~/.ssh/id_rsa.pub key into the ~/.ssh/authorized_keys file in the remote system account.

Note that

● When copying the key to another system using ssh-copy-id, it copies the ~/.ssh/id_rsa.pub file by default.

● It is recommended to change the ~/.ssh/authorized_keys file permission to 600 using the chmod command.

After finishing the previous, try logging into the remote server using SSH keys:

$ ssh root@rhel.server

How to copy a public ley (~/.ssh/id_rsa.pub) to your server

Use the scp command to copy the id_rsa.pub (public key) from your local system to rhel.server remote server as authorized_keys file, this is known as, “installing the public key to server”:

$ scp ~/.ssh/id_rsa.pub sam@rhel.server:~/.ssh/authorized_keys

Another option is to use the ssh-copy-id command as follows from your local workstation:

$ ssh-copy-id sam@rhel.server

$ ssh-copy-id -i ~/.ssh/id_rsa.pub rhel.server

SSH key demonstration

● Use ssh-keygen to create a public-private key pair.

● Use ssh-copy-id to copy the public key to the correct location on a remote system. 

4.5 Customizing SSH Service Configuration

Objective

After completing this section, you should be able to customize sshd configuration to restrict direct logins as root or to disable password-based authentication.

Note that

● sshd is a server and ssh is a client.

SSH Configuration Files

There are two files used for the SSH configuration:

1\. /etc/ssh/ssh_config: The ssh client system-wide configuration file.  

2\. /etc/ssh/sshd_config: The sshd server system-wide configuration file and needs the root privileges.

The OpenSSH server configuration file

OpenSSH server configuration usually does not require modification, though additional security measures are available. Various aspects of the OpenSSH server can be modified in the configuration file /etc/ssh/sshd_config.

Let’s Take Some Examples!

Prohibiting the root user from logging in using SSHYour system security should prevent the root user from directly logging into the system with the ssh command. As the root username exists on every Linux system, a suspected attacker has to guess the password only. The other reason is the root user's unrestricted privileges. 

Important

● From a security standpoint, it is advisable to prohibit the root user from directly logging into the system with ssh.

● The sshd_config configuration file has a setting to enable or disable system login as user root, which is enabled by default.

Note that

● By disabling the previous option in the /etc/ssh/sshd_config configuration file, the root user will be unable to log into the system using the ssh command after the sshd service is restarted.

Prohibiting password authentication using SSH 

Only allowing key-based logins to the remote command line has various advantages:

● SSH keys are longer than an average password, which adds security.

● Less effort to initiate remote shell access after the initial setup.

There is an option in the /etc/ssh/sshd_config configuration file which turns on password authentication by default:

Note that

● To prevent password authentication, the PasswordAuthentication option has to be set to no and the sshd service needs to be restarted with systemctl.  

● Whenever you change the /etc/ssh/sshd_config file, the sshd service has to be restarted.

◆◆◆

II.V System Targets

A systemd target is a group of unit files. Some targets are isolatable, which means that they define the final state a system is starting in.

● emergency.target is a minimal target that mounts the least for troubleshooting.

● rescue.target is for troubleshooting but with more loaded or mounted units.

● multi-user.target is usually the normal target for the server.

● graphical.target is the normal target with graphical.

Note that

● When you use the command # systemctl disable xxxx you get the output:

Removed /etc/systemd/system/multi-user.target.wants/xxxx.service

● When you enable the service with the command # systemctl enable xxxx you get the output:

Created symbolic link /etc/systemd/system/multi-user.target.wants/xxxx.service → /usr/lib/systemd/system/xxxx.service

This means when we enable the service this symbolic link is created to make the service run in the multi-user.target.

● You can use the command # systemctl cat xxxx to find the [install] section which has the normal operational state of the server and the target where the service will run into.

● You can use the command # systemctl cat multi-user.target to read the configuration of the target.

● You can use the command # systemctl list-dependencies to show the dependency relations between targets and their units.

Setting the default systemd Target

● You can use the command # systemctl get-default to see the default systemd target.

● And you can use the command # systemctl set-default to set the default systemd target.

Booting into a specific target

● On the GRUB2 boot prompt, use systemctl.unit=xxxx.target to boot into a specific target.

● To change between targets on a running system, use systemctl isolate xxxx.target.

◆◆◆

II.VI Archive, Compress, Unpack and Uncompress Files Using tar, star, gzip, and bzip2

When backing data in a computer locally or to the cloud, you should always try to get the most data into the smallest space. This is achieved through data compression techniques and tools like tar, gzip, bzip2, and xz.

6.1 File Compression and Uncompression on Linux.

This is done using the following tools:

● tar

● gzip

● bzip2

● xz

1\. tar

The tar command is used to create archive files. A tar archive can consist of separate files, one or more directory hierarchies, or a mixture of both. Tar files have an extension .tar.

2\. gzip

The gzip program is used to compress one or more files. When executed it replaces the original file with a compressed version of the original. A file compressed with gzip has the extension .gz

3\. bzip2

The bzip2 command is similar to gzip but uses a different compression algorithm that archives a higher level of compression at the cost of compression speed. A file compressed with bzip2 has the extension .bz2

4\. xz

The xz is a general-purpose data compression tool with command line syntax similar to gzip and bzip2\. It compresses or decompresses each file according to the selected operation mode. A file compressed with xz has the extension .xz

6.2 Compress/Archive files using tar, gzip, bzip2, and xz

1\. The tar command

has four main operation utilities:

● c: Create a new archive

● x: Extract files from an archive

● t: List the contents of an archive

● v: Verbosely list files processed

Creating a New Archive

Using the ls command, let’s check the contents of the Downloads directory;

$ ls Downloads/

To create a new Archive file of the Downloads directory called Backup.tar, use the following command:

$ tar -cvf Backup.tar Downloads/

You can use the ls -lh command, to check the created archive above:

$ ls -lh      

You can use the -t option, to list the contents of the Backup.tar archive file:

$ tar -tvf Backup.tar        

2\. The gzip command

Using gzip command, let’s compress the Backup.tar file:

$ gzip Backup.tar

To check the compressed file above:

$ ls -lh

You will find that after compressing the Backup.tar file, you will get the output Backup.tar.gz with reduced file size.

You can use the -k option with gzip to Keep input files during compression:

$ gzip -k Backup.tar

You can verify that the input file Backup.tar is kept during compression:

$ ls -lh

You can use the -l option with gzip to list the following fields:

$ gzip -l Backup.tar.gz

● Compressed size: the size of the compressed file.

● Uncompressed size: the size of the uncompressed file.

● Ratio: compression ratio (0.0% if unknown).

● Uncompressed_name: name of the uncompressed file.

Note that

● You can use the -v option with gzip to display the name and percentage reduction for each file compressed: $ gzip -v Backup.tar

3\. The bzip2 command

Using the bzip2 command, we are going to compress the Backup.tar file.

$ bzip2 Backup.tar

To check the compressed file above:

$ ls -lh

After compressing the Backup.tar file, the output is Backup.tar.bz2 with reduced file size.

Note that

● You can use the -k option with bzip2 to Keep input files during compression: $ bzip2 -k Backup.tar

To check that the input file Backup.tar is kept during compression: $ ls -lh

Note that

● You can use the -v option with bzip2 to display the name and percentage reduction for each file compressed: $ bzip2 -v Backup.tar

4\. The xz command

Use the xz command, we are going to compress the Backup.tar file:

$ xz Backup.tar

To check the compressed file above:

$ ls -lh

After compressing Backup.tar file the output is Backup.tar.xz with reduced file size. You can use the -k option with xz to Keep the input files during compression:

$ xz -k Backup.tar

To check if the input file Backup.tar is kept during compression:

$ ls -lh

Note that

● You can use the -v option with xz to display the name and percentage reduction for each file compressed:

$ xz -v Backup.tar

6.3 Unpack/Uncompress files using tar, gunzip, bunzip2 and unxz

1\. The tar command

Now, to unpack the archived file Backup.tar use the tar command:

$ tar -xvf backup.tar   

Note that

● You can use the ls -lh command to list the contents of the current directory where you unpack the Backup.tar archive file: $ ls -lh

After unpacking the Backup.tar archive file, you will get your original directory Downloads.

2\. The gunzip command

To Uncompress a gzip file we use gunzip program or gzip with the -d option:

$ gunzip Backup.tar.gz

Or

$ gzip -d Backup.tar.gz

To check the uncompressed file, you can use the command:

$ ls -lh

After uncompressing the Backup.tar.gz file, you will get the Backup.tar file.

You can use the -k option with gunzip to Keep the input files during decompression:

$ gunzip -k Backup.tar.gz

To check if the input file Backup.tar.gz is kept during decompression:

$ ls -lh

Note that

● You can use the -v option with gunzip to display the name and percentage reduction for each file decompressed: $ gunzip -v Backup.tar.gz

3\. The bunzip2 command

To Uncompress a bzip2 file, you can use the bunzip2 program or bzip2 with -d option:

$ bunzip2 Backup.tar.bz2

OR

$ bzip2 -d Backup.tar.bz2

To check the uncompressed file, you can use the command:

$ ls -lh

After uncompressing Backup.tar.bz2 file, you will get the Backup.tar file.

Note that

● You can use the -k option with bunzip2 to Keep the input files during decompression:

$ bunzip2 -k Backup.tar.bz2

To check if the input file Backup.tar.bz2 is kept during decompression:

$ ls -lh

Note that

● You can use the -v option with bunzip2 to display the name and percentage reduction for each file decompressed:

$ bunzip2 -v Backup.tar.bz2

4\. The unxz command

To Uncompress an xz file, you can use the unxz program or xz with the -d option:

$ unxz Backup.tar.xz

OR

$ xz -d Backup.tar.xz

To check the uncompressed file:

$ ls -lh

After uncompressing Backup.tar.xz file, you will get the Backup.tar file.

Note that

● You can use the -k option with unxz to Keep the input files during decompression:

$ unxz -k Backup.tar.xz

To check if the input file Backup.tar.xz is kept during decompression:

$ ls -lh

Note that

● You can use the -v option with unxz to display the name and percentage reduction for each file decompressed:

$ unxz -v Backup.tar.xz

6.4 Building applications from tar, gzip, bzip2, and xz packages in Linux

For the tar.gz package file, open your terminal, then navigate to the directory where the package file is located. Then extract the package file using the following commands:

$ tar -xzvf package.tar.gz

$ gunzip package.tar.gz

You can extract the tar.bz2 package file using the following commands:

$ tar -xjvf package.tar.bz2

$ bunzip package.tar.bz2

You can extract the tar.xz package file using the following commands:

$ tar -xJvf package.tar.xz

$ unxz package.tar.xz

Once you have extracted the package file, navigate to that directory, then read the file INSTALL and/or README if exists to know if you need some dependencies.

Type the following commands to install the package

file:

$ ./configure

$ make

$ sudo make install

Reference

● man tar

● man gzip

● man bzip2

● man xz

◆◆◆

II.VII Create and edit text files

7.1 The touch Command

is a standard program for Linux operating systems, that is used to create, change and modify the timestamps of a file. Let’s take some examples of using the touch command:

To Create an Empty File test, you can use the command:

$ touch test

To verify:

$ ls -l

To change or update the last access and modification times of the file test, you can use the -a option. For example:

$ touch -a test

To verify:

$ ls -lu

Note that

● I used the -u option with the ls command to show the last access time instead of the last modification time.

● You can use the -a option with the touch command to set the current time and date on a file, if the file does not exist, this will create a new empty file.

To avoid creating a new file you can use the -c option. For example: 

$ touch -c text

To verify:

$ ls -l

Notice that this command will not create the file text if it does not exist.

If you like to change only the modification time of the file test, then you can use the -m option. For example:

$ touch -m test

To verify:

# ls -l

Notice that it will only update the last modification time (not the access time) of the test file.

Also, the touch command can create any number of files simultaneously. For example, to create three new empty files named file1, file2, and file3, you can use the command:

$ touch file1 file2 file3

To verify:

$ ls –l

7.2 Editing Text Files from the Shell Prompt

Objectives

After completing this section, you should be able to:

● Create new files and edit existing text files from the shell prompt.

● Navigate within an editor to effectively accomplish editing tasks.

● Use the Vim editor for basic editing tasks.

Editing files with VIM

A key design principle of Linux is that information is stored in text-based files. Text files include both files with rows such as configuration files in the /etc directory, and Extensible Markup Language (XML) files, which define data structure through text tags, seen in application configuration files in both /etc and /usr directories. The advantage of text files is that they can be moved or shared between systems without requiring conversion, and can be viewed and edited using any simple text editor.

Vim is an improved version of the vi editor distributed with Linux and UNIX systems. It is the most popular text editor in the Linux family. Highly configurable and efficient for practiced users, including features such as split screen editing, color formatting, and highlighting for editing text.

When first opened, Vim starts in command mode, used for navigation, cut and paste, and other text manipulation. Enter each of the other modes with single-character keystrokes to access specific editing functionality:

●        An ‘I’ keystroke enters insert mode, where all text typed becomes file content. Pressing Esc returns to command mode.

● A ‘V’ keystroke enters visual mode, where multiple characters may be selected for text manipulation. Use ‘V’ for multi-line and Ctrl+V for block selection. The same keystroke used to enter visual mode (V or Ctrl+V) is used to exit.

●        The : keystroke begins extended command mode for tasks like writing the file to save it. And quitting the Vim editor.

Overview of the Basic VIM Workflow

Vim has efficient, coordinated keystrokes for advanced editing tasks. Although considered useful with practice, Vim's capabilities can overwhelm new users. The basic Vim workflow presents the

minimum keystrokes every Vim user must learn to accomplish any editing task. 

Typical file editing session using only basic Vim keystrokes:

1\. To open a file with vim, type vim followed by the filename.

2\. After opening the file, you can use arrow keys to position the cursor.

3\. You can press the ‘I’ key to enter insert mode. 

4\. Now you can add new text to the file or edit the existing one.

5\. Press Esc to return to command mode.  

6\. You can press ‘U’ to undo mistaken edits on the current line.  

7\. Use arrow keys to position the cursor.  

8\. You can press ‘X’ to delete a selection of text.  

9\. To save or exit, choose one of the following to write or discard file edits: 

● Type ‘w’ to write (save) the file and remain in command mode for more editing.

● Type ‘wq’ to and write the file quit Vim.

● Type ‘q!’ to quit Vim, but discard all file changes since the last write.

Vimtutor

You can use the vimtutor to learn your first Vim commands. Which is a thirty-minute tutorial that teaches the most basic Vim functionality in eight easy exercises. While you can't learn everything about vim in just half an hour, the tutor is designed to describe enough of the commands that you will be able to easily use Vim as an all-purpose editor.

In Linux, if Vim has been properly installed, you can start this program from the shell or command line by entering the vimtutor command. This will make a copy of the tutor file so that you can edit it without the risk of damaging the original. 

There are a few translated versions of the tutor. To find out if yours is available, use the two-letter language code. For example, For French, this would be vimtutor fr (if installed on the system).

Editing Text Files with a Graphical Editor

Objectives

After completing this section, you should be able to:

● Edit text files with gedit.

● Copy text between graphical windows.

Editing files with gedit

The gedit application is a full-featured text editor for the GNOME desktop environment. Launch gedit by selecting Activities > Show Applications > Text Editor. Also, like other graphical applications, gedit can be started without navigating the menu. Press Alt+F2 to open the Enter a Command dialog box, then type gedit and press Enter.

GNOME Help includes a gedit help guide, which you can find by Opening gedit, then from the Text Editor menu select Help.

The gedit help guide includes these topics:

● Working with files.

● Configuring gedit.

● gedit Plugins.

● Printing with gedit.

To view the list of graphical applications:

Activities > Show Applications > Utilities > Help > Go > All Documents, then scroll down to select the gedit Text Editor hyperlink.

Basic gedit Keystrokes

You can perform many file management tasks using gedit's menu, for example:

● To create a new file in gedit, click the blank paper toolbar icon which will create a new document in a separate tab within the same window, or from the Text Editor menu select New or press (Ctrl+N) which will also create a new document but in a separate window.

● To save a file, you can click the save button, then type the new file name or confirm the current by clicking the save button. Or from this button select Save as or save all. Also, you can easily press (Ctrl+S) to save the file followed by clicking the save button again to confirm the name and the saving destination of the file.

● To open an existing file, click the Open toolbar button then click on the Other Documents button. The Open Files dialog window will display from which users can locate and select the file to open. Multiple files may be opened simultaneously, each with a unique tab under the menu bar. Tabs display a file name after being saved the first time.

Copying text between graphical windows

Text can be copied between documents, text windows, and command windows in the graphical environment. The selected text is duplicated using copy and paste or moved using cut and paste. Whether cut or copied, the text is held in memory for pasting into another location. 

To select text:

1\. Click and hold the left mouse button before the first character is desired.  

2\. Drag the mouse over and down until all required text is in a single highlighted selection, then release the left button. Do not click again with the left button, as that deselects the text.

To paste the selection

Multiple methods can accomplish the same result. In the first method:

1\. Click the right mouse button anywhere on the text area just selected.  

2\. From the resulting context menu, select either cut or copy.  

3\. Move the mouse to the window or document where the text is to be placed, click the left mouse button to position where the text should go, and click the right mouse button again, now choosing paste.

A shorter mouse technique is to:

1\. First, select the text.  

2\. Hover the mouse over the destination window and click the center mouse button, just once, to paste the text at the cursor.

Notice that, this method can only copy, not cut. The original text remains selected and can be deleted. As with other methods, the text remains in memory and can be repeatedly pasted.

The keyboard shortcut method can also be used in graphical applications:

1\. Select the text.  

2\. Use Ctrl+X to cut or Ctrl+C to copy the text.  

3\. Click the location where the text will be placed to place the cursor.

4\. Use Ctrl+V to paste.

Note that

● Ctrl+C and Ctrl+V will not copy and paste within a terminal window. And that Ctrl+C will terminate the currently running process within a terminal window. To copy and paste within a terminal window, you can use Ctrl+Shift+C and Ctrl+Shift+V.

◆◆◆

II.VIII Create, delete, copy, and move files and directories

Managing Files Using Command-Line Tools

Command-line file management

involves creating, deleting, copying, and moving files. Additionally, directories can be created, deleted, copied, and moved to help organize files logically. When working at the command line, file management requires awareness of the current working directory to choose either absolute or relative path syntax as the most efficient for the immediate task.

Creating Files

The touch command normally updates a file's timestamp to the current date and time without otherwise modifying it. This is useful for creating empty files, which can be used for practice, since "touching" a file name that does not exist causes the file to be created. For example:

$ pwd

$ ls -l

Assume you have a file named text, if you used the touch command on it: 

$ touch text

$ ls -l

You will notice that the text file timestamp is updated to the current date and time without otherwise modifying its content. To verify:

$ cat text

Another example for creating a new empty file “test”:

$ touch test

To verify:

$ ls -l

Also, you can use a text editor program like Vim or Nano to create a new file or modify the existing one. For example:

$ nano fruits

The new file will open, you can add some data then save and quit.

To verify:

$ ls -l

And to show the content of the file fruits, use the cat command:

$ cat fruits

Important

● It is recommended to avoid using spaces in file names since file names that include spaces frequently result in undesired command execution behavior.

Note that

● The ls command has multiple options for displaying attributes on files. The most common and useful are:

o  The -a option for displaying all files, including hidden files:

$ ls -a

o The -R option (recursive) to include the contents of all subdirectories:

$ cd

$ ls -R

o The -l option for long listing format:

$ ls -l

● By adding the -l option to the ls command, it can list all the files, directories, and their mode, the number of links, the owner of the file, file size, modified date and time, and filename.

Also, you can use ll instead of ls -l as it is aliased to ll. To verify:

$ alias ll

$ ll

● The two special directories at the top of the listing refer to the current directory with a dot ( . ) and the parent directory with a double dot ( . . ). These special directories exist in every directory on the system. Their usefulness will become apparent when file management commands are practiced. 

● File names beginning with a dot ( . ) indicate files hidden from normal view using ls and other commands. This is not a security feature. Hidden files keep necessary user configuration files from cluttering home directories. Many commands process hidden files only with specific command-line options, preventing one user's configuration from being accidentally copied to other directories or users.

● Protecting file contents from improper viewing requires the use of file permissions which I will discuss later.

The hidden directory ( . ) specifies the current directory on commands in which the current location is either the source or destination argument, avoiding the need to type out the directory's absolute path name. The other cd . . command uses the double dot hidden directory to move up one level to the parent directory, without needing to know the exact parent name. 

$ pwd

$ cd ..

$ pwd

Creating directories

The mkdir command creates one or more directories or subdirectories, generating errors if the file name already exists or when attempting to create a directory in a parent directory that doesn't exist. The -p parent option creates missing parent directories for the requested destination. Take care when using mkdir -p, since accidental spelling mistakes create unintended directories without generating error messages, for example, to create the 2019 directory:

$ mkdir 2019

To verify:

$ ls -l

And to create jan directory as a subdirectory for the 2020 directory:

$ mkdir 2020/jan

Here, the mkdir failed due to existing one of two reasons, first 2020 was misspelled, or 2020 does not exist as a location in which to create the jan subdirectory. If a -p were used, we would not have received an error message and we will have the two directories, 2020 and jan created. 

For example:

$ mkdir -p 2020/jan 

To verify:

$ ls -R

Here the -p parent option created the missing parent directory 2020.

Copying files

The cp command copies one or more files to become new, independent files. The syntax allows copying an existing file to a new file in the current or another directory or copying multiple files into another directory. In any destination, new file names must be unique. If the new file name is not unique, the copy command will overwrite the existing file.

For example:

$ cp text 2019

Here I copied the text file to the 2019 directory.

To check:

$ ll 2019

$ cat 2019/text

If we put some data in the original text file:

$ nano text

Then copied it again to the 2019 directory:

$ cp text 2019

The old text file will be replaced with the new one because they are using the same name.

To verify:

$ cat 2019/text

Note that

● When copying multiple files with one command, the last argument must be a directory.

Copying Directories with Contents

To protect users from accidentally overwriting directories with contents, multiple file cp commands ignore directories specified as a source. Copying non-empty directories, with contents, requires the - r recursive option.

For example:

$ cp fruits 2020/jan

$ ll 2020/jan

$ cp –r 2020/jan 2019

To verify:

$ ls –R 2019

Here we copied the jan directory into the 2019 directory.

Moving files

The mv command renames files in the same directory or relocates files to a new directory. File contents remain unchanged.

For example:

$ mv text test

To verify:

$ ll

Here, I used the mv command to rename the text file to the test file.

$ mv test Documents

To verify:

$ ll Documents

And here, I used the mv command to relocate the test file into the Documents directory.

Removing files and directories

The default syntax for the rm command deletes files, but not directories. Deleting a directory, and potentially many subdirectories and files below it requires the -r recursive option. 

Important

● There is no command-line undelete feature, nor a trash bin from which to restore.

For example:

To delete the file test in the Documents directory:

$ rm Documents/test

To verify:

$ ll Documents

And to delete the 2019 Directory and the files below it, you will need to use the -r recursive option or you will get an error:

$ rm 2019

$ rm –r 2019

To verify:

$ ll

After rm failed to delete the 2019 directory, the -r recursive option succeeded.

Note that

● Using the -i option will interactively prompt for each deletion. This is essentially the opposite of the -f option which will force the deletion without prompting the user.

● The rmdir command deletes directories only if empty.

Removed directories cannot be undeleted.

For example:

$ ls –lR 2020

$ rmdir 2020

Here, the rmdir command failed to delete the non-empty 2020 directory.

$ mkdir 2021

$ ls –lR 2021

$ rmdir 2021

And here, when deleting the empty 2021 directory the rmdir command succeeded.

◆◆◆

II.IX Create hard and soft links

What is a Link?

A link is a way of matching two or more file names to the same set of file data. There are two ways to achieve this, they behave similarly, but are not the same. 

Link types

1.    Hard link

A hard link associates two or more file names with the same inode. Each regular file is in principle a hard link. Hard links share the same data blocks on the hard disk, while they continue to behave as independent files.

There is an immediate disadvantage that is hard links can't span across partitions since they refer to inodes, and inode numbers are unique within a given partition.

2.    Soft link or Symbolic link (or for short symlink)

A soft link is a small file that is a pointer to another file. A symbolic link contains the path to the target file instead of a physical location on the hard disk. Since inodes are not used in this system, soft links can span across partitions.

Note that

● Removing the target file for a symbolic link makes the link useless.

● Symbolic links are always very small files, while Hard links have the same size as the original file.

Creating Symbolic Links

The command used to create links is ln. To create symlinks, you need to use the -s option.

The syntax for creating a symlink is: 

$ ln -s targetfile linkname

For Example, to create a symlink for the /usr/users/test/Downloads directory in the current directory with the name Downloads:

$ ln -s /usr/users/test/Downloads Downloads

To verify:

$ ls -l

In this example, I created a link in the current directory to a directory on another part of the system.

◆◆◆

II.X List, set, and change standard ugo/rwx permissions

Linux File System Permissions

Objective

After completing this section, you should be able to explain how the Linux file permissions model works.

Linux file system permissions

Access to files by users is controlled by file permissions. The Linux file permissions system is simple but flexible, which makes it easy to understand and apply to handle most permission cases easily.

Files have three categories of users to which permissions apply. The file is owned by a user, normally the one who created the file. The file is also owned by a single group, usually the primary group of the user who created the file, but this can be changed.

Different permissions can be set for the owning user, the owning group, and for all other users on the system that are not the user or a member of the owning group. 

Note that

● The most specific permissions apply. So, user permissions override Group permissions, which override other permissions.

Effects of permissions on files and directories:

● r (read) Contents of the file can be read. Contents of the directory (file names) can be listed.

● w (write) Contents of the file can be changed. Any file in the directory may be created or deleted.

● x (execute) Files can be executed as commands. Contents of the directory can be accessed (dependent on the permissions of the files in the directory).

Note that

● Users normally have both read and execute on read-only directories, so that they can list the directory and access its contents. 

● If a user only has read access on a directory, the names of the files in it can be listed, but no other information, including permissions or timestamps, are available, nor can they be accessed. 

● If a user only has execute access on a directory, he cannot list the names of the files in that directory, but if he already knows the name of a file that he has permission to read, then he can access the contents of that file by explicitly specifying the file name.

Viewing file/directory permissions and ownership

You can view file/directory permissions and ownership using the ls -l command.

For example:

$ ls -l test

$ ls -l /home

Note that

● You can add the -d option to the ls command to prevent the descent into the directory and see the expanded listing of the directory itself.

● Linux permissions only apply to the directory or file that they are set on and permissions on a directory are not inherited automatically by the subdirectories and files within it. However, permissions on a directory may effectively block access to its contents. 

Managing File System Permissions from the Command Line

Objective

After completing this section, you should be able to change the permissions and ownership of files using command-line tools.

Changing file/directory permissions

You can use the chmod command to change permissions from the command line. The chmod command takes permission instructions followed by a list of files or directories to change. The permission instruction can be issued either using the symbolic method or the numeric method.

1\. The Symbolic method keywords: 

● The Symbolic method keywords are (u, g, o, a) characters and are used (for the user, group, other, and all).

● The (+, -, = ) signs are used (for add, remove, and set exactly).

● The (r, w, x) characters are used (for read, write, and execute).

Note that

● With the symbolic method, it is not necessary to set a completely new group of permissions.

● With the symbolic method, you can use three symbols, + to add permissions to a set, - to remove permissions from a set, and = to replace the entire set for a group of permissions.

● The permissions are represented by a single letter: r for read, w for write, and x for execute.

2\. The Numeric method:

● In the Numeric method, each digit represents an access level: user, group, other.

● Each # represents the sum of permissions granted (r, w, and x) for each access level. Where # will = 7 if full permission is granted (rwx) as it will be the sum of 4 for r, 2 for w, and 1 for x.

● Using the Numeric method, permissions are represented by a three-digit octal number (or four, when setting advanced permissions).

● A single octal digit can represent the numbers from 0 to 7, exactly the number of possibilities for a three-bit number.

Convert between Symbolic and Numeric Representations of Permissions

In the three-digit octal (numeric) representation, each digit stands for one group of permissions, from left to right user, group, and other. In each of these groups, start with 0. If the read permission is present, add 4, add 2 if the write permission is present, and add 1 for execute permission.

Notice that, numeric permissions are often used by advanced administrators since they are shorter to type and pronounce, while still giving full control over all permissions.

If a file permissions are shown like this:

- rwx r-x --- 

For the user, rwx is calculated as 4+2+1=7. For the group, r-x is calculated as 4+0+1=5, and for other users, --- is represented with 0. Putting these three together, the numeric representation of those permissions is 750.

This calculation can also be performed in the opposite direction. 

If a file permissions are shown like this:

640

For the user permissions, 6 represents read (4) and write (2), which displays as rw- . For the group part, 4 only includes read (4) and displays as r--. The 0 for other provides no permissions (---) and the final set of symbolic permissions for this file is - rw- r-- ---.

Let’s take some examples:

● To remove read and write permission for group and other on test using the symbolic method:

$ chmod g-rw test

$ chmod o-rw test

● To add execute permission for everyone on the file test using the symbolic method:

$ chmod a+x test

● To set read, write, and execute permission for user, read and execute for the group, and no permission for other on the directory testdir using the numeric method:

$ chmod u=rwx test

$ chmod g=rx test

$ chmod o-rwx test

OR

$ chmod u=rwx,g=rx,o-rwx test

OR

$ chmod u=rwx,g+rx-w,o-rwx test

Note that

● The chmod commands support the -R option for recursively setting permissions on an entire directory tree.

● When using this option, be sure to use the uppercase X permissions instead of the lowercase x permission to indicate that execute permissions should only be set on directories, and not regular files. For example, the following command will recursively set read and write access on sampledir and all its children for their group owner, but will only apply to execute permissions to directories, not regular files: $ chmod -R RW sampledir

Changing file/directory user or group ownership

A newly created file is owned by the user who creates the file. By default, the new file has group ownership which is the primary group of the user creating the file. Since Red Hat Enterprise Linux uses user private groups, this group is often a group with only that user as a member. To grant access based on group membership, the owner or the group of a file may need to be changed.

File ownership can be changed with the chown command. For example, to grant ownership of the file test to sam, the following command could be used:

# chown sam test

The chown command can be used with the -R option to recursively change the ownership of an entire directory tree. This command will grant ownership of sampledir and all files and subdirectories within it to tom:

# chown -R tom sampledir

The chown command can also be used to change group ownership of a file by preceding the group name with a colon ( : ). For example, this command will change the group sampledir to developers:

# chown :developers sampledir

The chown command can also be used to change both owner and group at the same time by using the syntax owner:group. For example, to change the ownership of sampledir to test and the group to developers, use the command:

# chown test:developers sampledir

To verify:

# ls –l sampledir

Note that

● Only the root can change the ownership of a file. Group ownership, however, can be set by the root or the file's owner. 

● The root can grant ownership to any group, while non-root users can grant ownership only to groups they belong to.

● Instead of using the chown command, you can use the chgrp command to change the group ownership using the syntax: $ chgrp group filename

The chgrp command

# chgrp group file

This command works the same as changing ownership with the chown command, including the use of the -R option to affect entire directory trees, and in this case, the syntax will be:

# chgrp –R group dir

Managing Default Permissions and File Access

Objectives

After completing this section, you should be able to configure a directory in which newly created files are automatically writable by members of the group which owns the directory, also using special permissions and default umask settings.

Special Permissions

The setuid (or setgid) permission on an executable file means that the command will run as the user (or group) of the file, not as the user that ran the command. An example is the passwd command.

The sticky bit for a directory sets a special restriction on the deletion of files. Only the owner of the file (and root) can delete files within the directory. An example is the /tmp directory.

Lastly, setgid on a directory means that files created in the directory will inherit the group affiliation from the directory, rather than inheriting it from the creating user. This is commonly used on group collaborative directories to automatically change a file from the default private group to the shared group.

Effects of Special Permissions on Files and Directories

![Effects of Special Permissions on Files and Directories](image_rsrcZV2.jpg)

Setting Special Permissions

● Symbolically:

setuid = u+s; setgid = g+s; sticky = o+t

● Numerically:

It will be the fourth preceding digit:

setuid = 4; setgid = 2; sticky = 1

Let’s take some examples:

● To add the setgid bit on the directory sampledir:

$ chmod g+s sampledir

● To set the setgid bit, and read/write/execute for user and group on directory sampledir:

$ chmod g+srwx sampledir

Default File Permissions

The default permissions for files are set by the processes that create them. For example, text editors create files so they are readable and writeable, but not executable by everyone.

Additionally, binary executables are created executable by the compilers that create them. The mkdir command creates new directories with all permissions set-read, write, and execute.

Important

● Before the mask is applied, a directory has permissions 777 or (rwxrwxrwx), and a plain file has permissions 666 or (rw-rw-rw-).

Then umask value will be subtracted from these default permissions after the function has created the new file or directory. Thus, a directory will have permissions of 775 by default, a file 664, if the mask value is (0)002.

These permissions are not typically set when new files and directories are created. This is because some of the permissions are cleared by the umask of the shell process.

The umask command without arguments will display the current value of the shell’s umask:

$ umask

Every process on the system has a umask, which is an octal bitmask that is used to clear the permissions of new files and directories that are created by the process.

If a bit is set in the umask, then the corresponding permission is cleared in new files. Instead of adding the symbolic values to each other, as with chmod, for calculating the permission on a new file they need to be subtracted from the total possible access rights.  

For example, the previous umask, 0002, clears the write bit for other users. The leading zeros indicate the special, user, and group permissions are not cleared. A umask of 077 clears all the group and other permissions of newly created files.

You can use the umask command with a single numeric argument to change the umask of the current shell. The numeric argument should be an octal value corresponding to the new umask value. If it is less than 3 digits, leading zeros are assumed.

The system default umask values for Bash shell users are defined in the /etc/profile and /etc/bashrc files. You can override your system defaults as a user in your  .bash_profile and .bashrc files.

Important

● A directory gets more permissions by default, it always has the execute permission. If it wouldn't have that, it would not be accessible. You can try this out by chmodding a directory 644!

● If you log in to another group using the newgrp command, the mask remains unchanged. Thus, if it is set to 002, files and directories that you create while being in the new group will also be accessible to the other members of that group; you don't have to use chmod.

● The root user usually has stricter default file creation permissions.

◆◆◆

II.XI Locate, read, and use system documentation including man, info, and files in /usr/share/doc

Reading Documentation using man Command

Objectives

After completing this section, you should be able to locate documentation and research answers about commands.

Introducing the man command

The ability to efficiently search for topics and navigate man pages is a critical administration skill. The historical Linux Programmer's Manual, from which man pages originate, was large enough to be multiple printed books. Each contained information for specific types of files, which have become the sections you see when typing man man in the shell followed by Enter. Articles are referred to as topics, as pages no longer apply.

The content type of the man page sections

1\. User commands (both executable and shell programs)  

2\. System calls (kernel routines invoked from user space)  

3\. Library functions (provided by program libraries)  

4\. Special files (such as device files)  

5\. File formats (for many configuration files and structures)  

6\. Games (historical Section for amusing programs)  

7\. Conventions, standards, and miscellaneous (protocols, file systems)

8\. System administration and privileged commands (maintenance tasks)

9\. Linux kernel API (internal kernel calls)

Important

● To distinguish identical topic names in different sections, the man page references include the section number in parentheses after the topic. For example, passwd(l) describes the command to change passwords, while passwd(5) explains the /etc/passwd file format for storing local user accounts.

●  Popular system administration topics are in Section 1 (user commands), Section 5 (file formats), and Section 8 (administrative commands). The remaining sections are commonly for programmer reference or advanced administration.

Note that

● To read specific man pages, use man followed by the topic name. Topic contents display one screen at a time. Use arrow keys for single-line scrolling or the space bar for the next screen, and you can press Q any time to exit.

● The man command searches manual sections in a configured order, displaying popular sections first. For example, man passwd displays passwd section(l) by default.

● To display the man page topic from a specific section, include the section number argument, for example, man 5 passwd will display passwd section 5.

Identifying man pages by keyword

Let’s try the basic man navigation commands

To navigate the man page for a specified topic, you can use the following:

● Spacebar Scroll forward (down) one screen.

●  PageDown Scroll forward (down) one screen.

●  PageUp Scroll backward (up) one screen.

●  DownArrow Scroll forward (down) one line.

●  UpArrow Scroll back (up) one line.

● d Scroll forward (down) one half-screen.

● u Scroll backward (up) one half-screen.

●  /string Search forward (down) for a string in the man page. 

● n Repeat the previous search forward (down) on the man page. 

● N Repeat the previous search backward (up) on the man page.

● g Go to the start of the man page.

● G Go to the end of the man page.

● Q Exit man and return to the command shell prompt.

Let’s take some examples

To open the man page of the man command type:

$ man man

To open the man page of the passwd command type:

$ man passwd

Note that

● When performing searches inside man pages, string allows regular expression syntax. While simple text (such as passwd) works as expected, regular expressions use meta-characters (such as $, *, ., ") for more sophisticated pattern matching. For example, /o.t where the dot will be replaced with any character.

Searching for man pages by keyword

A keyword search of man pages is performed using the command man -k keyword, which searches the short manual page descriptions for keywords and displays a list of keyword-matching man page topics with section numbers.

For example:

$ man –k echo

Its result is similar to that of the apropos command:

$ apropos echo

Important

● Keyword searches rely on an index generated by the mandb command, which must be run as root. The command runs daily through cron.daily, or by anacrontab within an hour of a boot if out of date. If the man -k keyword command didn’t work with you, try the mandb command as root first.

● The man -K “uppercase” option performs a full-text page search, not just titles and descriptions like the -k “lowercase” option. For example: $ man –K echo. Note that, you can press Q followed by Enter to switch between result pages.

Notice that, a full-text search can use greater systems resources and take more time.

The info pages

In addition to the man pages, you can read the info pages about a command, using the info command. These usually contain more recent information and are somewhat easier to use. 

Notice that, the man pages for some commands refer to the info pages.

We will start by typing info info in a terminal window. Use the arrow keys to browse through the text and move the cursor on a line starting with an asterisk, containing the keyword about which you want info, then hit Enter. Use the P and N keys to go to the previous or next subject. The space bar will move you one page further, no matter whether this starts a new subject or an info page for another command. Also, you can use Q to quit. 

Reading Documentation using pinfo Command

Objective

After completing this lesson, you should be able to research answers using GNU info documentation.

Introducing GNU info

pinfo is an info file viewer or a program for viewing info files. The man pages have a formal format useful as a command reference, but less useful as general documentation. For such documents, The GNU Project developed a different online documentation system, known as GNU info. 

Info documents are an important resource on RHEL systems because many fundamental components and utilities, are either developed by the GNU Project or utilize the info document system.

You can specify which page you want to read by passing it as an info page argument. This argument contains the name of an info page. The program will then search for it in the current directory, /usr/share/info directory, and /usr/local/share/info. The search path can be adjusted by the INFOPATH environment variable or in the configuration files (.bash_profile or .bashrc).

You can use the pinfo command only to show the top of the info tree, which is a menu of major topics in the info topics directory.

Walking through pinfo

● Press the Right key or the Enter key to choose the highlighted topic.

● Press the Left key to go backward.

●  Press the d key to display the directory of topics.  

● Press PageDown or Space to Scroll forward (down) one screen.

● Press PageUp or b to Scroll backward (up) one screen.

● Press u to Display the parent node of a topic. 

● Press DownArrow to Scroll forward (down) to the next hyper link. 

● Press UpArrow to Scroll backward (up) to the previous hyper link.

●  Press / then type string to search for a pattern.

● Press the q key to exit.

Note that

● Some commands and utilities have both man pages and info documentation.

● Info documentation is structured as hyperlinked info nodes.

● Like man pages, info nodes are read from the command line, using either the info or pinfo commands.

You can show the documentation of the pwd command using man, info, and pinfo commands so you can compare between using anyone of them.

$ man pwd

$ info pwd

$ pinfo pwd

Reading Documentation in /usr/share/doc Directory

Objective

After completing this lesson, you should be able to research information using Red Hat Package Manager documentation.

Introducing package documentation

In addition to man and pinfo, developers may also choose to include documentation in their application's RPM distribution package.

The Red Hat Package Manager ‘RPM’ is a default open-source and most popular package management utility for Red Hat-based systems like RHEL, CentOS, and Fedora. This tool allows system administrators and users to install, uninstall, query, verify, and manage system software packages in Linux operating systems.

When the package is installed, files recognized as documentation are moved to /usr/share/doc/packagename. Software package builders may include anything deemed helpful to it, but not duplicating, man pages. 

GNU packages also use /usr/share/doc to supplement info nodes, most packages include files describing package distribution licensing, and some packages include extensive PDF or HTML-based documentation. 

Note that

● You can use a browser of choice, for example, firefox, to show the index of the doc directory by pointing it to file:/usr/share/doc.

The whatis command

is a short index of explanations for commands, as an example:

$ whatis ls

This displays short information about the ls command, and the first section in the collection of man pages that contains the appropriate page.

The --help option

Most GNU commands support the --help option, which gives a short explanation about how to use the command and a list of available options. Let’s try this option with the cat command:

$ cat --help

As you will see, it gives a short explanation about how to use the cat command and a list of its available options.

The Graphical help

Don't despair if you prefer a graphical user interface. The Gnome Help Browser is very user-friendly, and it provides painless access to man and info pages, where the system documentation and man pages are easily browsable with a plain interface.

You can start it by entering the command gnome-help info:info, or by typing yelp info:info in a terminal window in the foreground, and you will get a browsable info page about the info command. 

Similarly, yelp man:ls will present you with the man page for the ls command.

Note that

● Some commands don't have separate documentation because they are parts of another command, such as cd, exit, and logout. They are part of your shell program and are called shell built-in commands.

◆◆◆

Chapter III: Create simple shell script

Chapter III Contents

● III.I Conditionally execute code (use of: if, test, [], etc.)       

● III.II Use Looping constructs (for, etc.) to process file, command line input

● III.III Process script inputs ($1, $2, etc.)

● III.IV Processing output of shell commands within a script    

● III.V Processing shell command exit codes

◆◆◆

III.I Conditionally execute code (use of: if, test, [], etc.)

Conditional execution

Conditional execution means that you can choose to execute code only if certain conditions are met. Without this capability, all you would be able to do is execute one command after another.

Conditional execution syntax:

[ condition-to-test-for ]

Let’s take an example:

[ -e /etc/group ]

This tests if the /etc/group exists, and if it does this returns a true, command exit status of 0. If it doesn't exist, the command exits with the exit status of 1. The spaces around the symbols are required.

File test operators:

● -d FILE

True if the file is a directory

● -e FILE

True if the file exists

● -f FILE

True if the file exists and it's a regular file

● -r FILE

True if the file is readable by you

● -s FILE

True if the file exists and it's not empty

● -w FILE

True if the file is writable by you

● -x FILE

True if the file is executable by you

String test operators:

● -z STRING

True if the string is empty

● -n STRING

True if the string is not empty

● STRING1 = STRING2

True if the strings are equal

● STRING1 != STRING2

True if the strings are not equal

Arithmetic tests:

● arg1 -eq arg2

True if the arguments are equal

● arg1 -ne arg2

True if the arguments are not equal

● arg1 -lt arg2

True if the arg1 is less than arg2

● arg1 -le arg2

True if arg1 is less than or equal to arg2

● arg1 -gt arg2

True if arg1 is greater than arg2

● arg1 -ge arg2

True if arg1 is greater than or equal to arg2

&& and ||

It is possible to combine tests, and/or run a chain of multiple commands by using the && and || operators.

&& = AND - The command that follows && will be executed if and only the previous command succeeds (exits with 0 exit status).

|| = OR

The || operator performs a Logical OR, so when it only matters that one of the conditions is met, but not which one, you can use it.

IF/THEN

As with most programming languages, the shell has an if/then/else construct. The syntax uses square brackets to perform a test, and then and fi statements are required.

The syntax is:

if [ condition ]

then

statements for when the condition is true

fi

Note that

● It is quite common to use the semicolon to put the then on the same line as the if:

if [ condition ];then statements;fi

ELSE

The else statement is used to execute when the if statement evaluates to false. It may be that you want to run the command if possible, but if it can’t be done, then continue the execution of the script. the most common way to do so is to use the else statement.

ELIF

Use the elif statement is used to include multiple conditional expressions after the if condition or between the if and else statements. It is short for "else if".

Let’s take some examples

Example 1\. Check Shell Type

#!/bin/bash

SHELL="bash"

if [ "$SHELL" = "bash" ]

then

echo "This is a bash shell!"

fi

Example 2\. Check if a File is Readable

#!/bin/bash

# Check the possible causes of failure

ls -l /etc/shadow

if [ -r "/etc/shadow" ]; then

echo "This file is readable."

else

echo "This is not a readable file."

fi

Example 3\. OS Identify

#!/bin/bash

OS=`uname -s`

if [ "$OS" = "Windows_NT" ]; then

echo "This is MS/Windows"

elif [ "$OS" = "GNU" ]; then

echo "This is GNU"

elif [ "$OS" = "SunOS" ]; then

echo "This is Solaris"

elif [ "$OS" = "FreeBSD" ]; then

echo "This is FreeBSD"

elif [ "$OS" = "Linux" ]; then

echo "This is Linux"

else

echo "OS Unknown"

fi

Shell scripting

Small intro to shell programming

You can consider the shell as a full programming language with variables and functions, and also more advanced structures.

What is shell scripting?

Shell scripting is writing a series of commands for the shell to execute. It can be stored and executed anytime, which is great for automating tasks and reduces the effort required by the end user. 

Script commands are being executed by the shell, one by one, and everything you can type in the command line, you can also put in the script. Notice that, before running a script, you will need to set up permissions for execution with the command: 

$ chmod 755 script.sh

Then you can run the script, using the command:

$ ./script.sh

#!

#! (shebang) specifies the binary of the shell we want to execute the script, for example:

#!/bin/bash, or #!/bin/sh.

Let’s take an example:

1\. To show the sleep.sh file content, run:

$ cat sleep.sh

#!/bin/sh

sleep 90

2\. To modify the sleep.sh file permissions, run:

$ chmod 755 sleep.sh

3\. To run the sleep.sh file (script), run:

$ ./sleep.sh

4\. To show process info:

$ top

Note that

● If a script doesn't contain #!, commands are executed with the default shell.

● You can also use other binaries like python: #! /usr/bin/python

Variables

Variables are storage locations that have a name, and you can think of them as name-value pairs. The syntax used to create a variable is VARIABLE_NAME="Value". 

Important

● Variable names are case-sensitive and should be in uppercase. Also, make sure to not use spaces after and before = sign, when declaring a variable.

● Variables can be defined in the functions, but we cannot access them before a function is called.

● Valid variable names can consist of letters, numbers, and underscores, except that number cannot be the first char in the name.

● You can assign the output of the command to a variable, for example:

HOST_NAME=$(hostname)

$ echo "Hello ${HOST_NAME}"

Local variables

Local vars are created with local keywords, and only functions can have the local variables, so they can only be accessed within the function where they're declared.

◆◆◆

III.II Use Looping constructs (for, etc.) to process file, command line input

What is a loop?

A loop is a block of code that repeats (iterates) a list of commands as long as the loop's control condition is true.

for loops

Syntax:

for arg in [list]

During each pass through the loop, arg takes the value of each successive variable in the list.

Basic looping construct:

for arg in [list]

do

command(s)

done

Note that

● You can quote arguments in [list] to prevent possible word splitting.

● Each [list] element may contain multiple parameters.

● A variable may supply the [list] in a for loop.

● The argument list may contain wild cards.

● If do is on the same line as for, there needs to be a semicolon after the list, for example: for arg in [list]; do

For example, to iterate through an array of values until the list is exhausted:

#!/bin/bash

for i in 1 2 3 4 5

do

echo "This is iteration number $i"

done

while

The while construct tests for a condition at the top of the loop, and it keeps iterating as long as that condition is true (returns an exit state of 0). In contrast to the for loop, the while loop finds use in situations where the number of loop iterations is not known in advance.

Basic while construct:

while [condition]

do

command(s)

done

OR

while [ condition ]; do commands; done

Note that

● If do is on the same line as while there needs to be a semicolon after the condition test.

● A while loop may have multiple conditions. Only the final condition determines when the loop terminates.

For example, to read a file with a while loop by iterating over it line by line:

#!/bin/bash

file=test.txt

while read -r line;

do

echo $line

done < "$file"

until

The until construct tests for a condition at the top of the loop, and continues iterating as long as that condition is false, you can consider it as the opposite of the while loop.

Basic until construct:

until [condition-is-true]

do

command(s)

done

Note that

● If do is on the same line as while, there needs to be a semicolon after the condition test, for example: until [condition-is-true]; do

For example, to use until loop to display numbers 0 through 4:

#!/bin/bash

x=0

until [ ! $x -lt 5 ]

do

echo $x

x=`expr $x + 1`

done

◆◆◆

II.III Process script inputs

How to use inputs in your shell scripts

Passing arguments to a script is useful because it means that you can use a script repeatedly without modification. This section will cover how to present and use arguments from the command line.

Shell scripts can access some variables from the environment:

●        $0 - The name of the script

●        $1 - The first argument sent to the script

●        $2 - The second argument sent to the script

●        $3 - The third argument ... and so on

●        $# - The number of arguments provided

●        $@ - A list of all arguments provided

Important

● The default separator between arguments is the Space character.

● If the values are enclosed in double quotes the script interprets it as one argument.

Handle many arguments

The $@ variable contains a list of all arguments the script receives. You can use a loop to handle all the variables.

For example, to display any arguments you pass to a script:

#!/bin/bash

echo "Values of all the arguments: $@"

◆◆◆

II.IV Processing output of shell commands within a script

How to redirect shell command output

Shell scripts provide a powerful feature, the ability to redirect the output from commands and scripts and send it to files, devices, or even as input to other commands or scripts.

Types of output

Commands and scripts in a shell can generate two basic types of output:

1\. STDOUT: The normal output from a command/script (file descriptor 1)  

2\. STDERR: The error output from a command/script (file descriptor 2)

Note that

● By default, STDOUT and STDERR are sent to your terminal's screen.

● By default, STDIN reads input from the keyboard (file descriptor 0).

● A file descriptor is a unique identifier for a file or other I/O resource.

How to redirect shell output

There are multiple ways to redirect output from shell scripts and commands:

1\. Redirect STDOUT

To redirect the standard output to a file, use the following syntax:

$ command > filename

OR

$ command 1> filename

To append the standard output to a file, use the following syntax:

$ command >> filename

Note that

● If you omit the file descriptor, STDOUT is used by default.

2\. Redirect STDERR

To redirect stderr (standard error) to a file, use the following syntax:

$ command 2> filename

To suppress the error messages from being displayed on the screen, redirect stderr to /dev/null. The syntax is:

$ command 2> /dev/null

Important

● To append data to an existing file, use the append parameter >>. Otherwise, it will overwrite the existing file data.

3\. Redirect STDOUT and STDERR

Another common situation is to send both STDOUT and STDERR to the same file:

To redirect both stderr and stdout (standard output) to a file, use the following syntax:

$ command &> filename

OR

$ command > filename 2>&1

In this example, all output (normal and error) is sent to the same file.

The 2>&1 construction means "send the STDERR to the same place you are sending the STDOUT."

To append both stderr and stdout (standard output) to a file, use the following syntax:

$ command &>> filename

To redirect stdout to the file output and then redirect stderr to stdout to the file errors, use the following syntax:

$ command > output 2>errors

To redirect stdout to the file output and stderr to the file error, use the following syntax:

$ command 2>error 1>output

4\. Constructing pipelines

Redirection controls channel output to or from files while piping sends channel output to another process.

Examples of process piping redirection

Piping a long output to less command

For example:

$ ls -l /usr/bin | less

Note that

● The -l option is used for long listing format.

● The less command allows for back-and-forth movement in the file. Also, it is not necessary to read the entire input file before starting, so with large input files, it starts faster than text editors.

Using the tee command for Piping 

The tee command displays or redirects the intermediate result which is usually blocked by pipes. For example, to list information about the current directory files on the terminal while simultaneously being stored in a file, use:

$ ls -l | tee /tmp/output

◆◆◆

II.V Processing shell command exit codes

What is an exit code in the Linux shell?

The exit code is the code returned to a parent process by an executable. The standard exit code is 0 for success and any number from 1 to 255 for anything else.

Note that

● If exit codes are not set the exit code will be the exit code of the last run command.

How to get the exit code of a command?

To get the exit code for the last run command, use: 

$ echo $?

If it was successful without any errors, the exit code will be 0. If not, the exit code will be 1.

What is an exit code in Linux Shell?

Exit code (return code) is the code returned from a command or script to a parent process after execution. The standard exit code is 0 for success and any number from 1 to 255 for anything else.

Note that

● You can benefit from exit codes to adapt scripts if a process or command succeeds or fails.

● If no exit codes are set, the exit code will be the exit code for the last run command.

Customize the return code

How to use exit codes in scripts?

To use exit codes in scripts, you can use an if statement to see if an operation was successful.

For example:

#!/bin/bash

cat /etc/passwd

if [ $? -eq 0 ]

then

echo "You have root permission!"

exit 0

else

echo "Permission denied!" >&2

exit 1

fi

Notice that

● If the command runs successfully, the exit code will be 0, and "You have root permission!" will be printed on the terminal.

How to set an exit code?

To set an exit code in a script use exit followed by the number you want to return.

For example:

1\. Create a test.sh file:

$ vim test.sh

2\. Add the following lines:

#!/bin/bash

exit 5

3\. Save and quit

4\. To verify, use the following command:

$ sh test.sh

$ echo $?

The result should be 5.

Test the return code with a shell script

If you need to test the exit (return) code of a command you called in your shell script, you need to check the $? variable value immediately after executing the command, by assigning it to another variable, then showing that variable value.

Note that

● You can use the errno command to list all the standard error codes: $ man errno

◆◆◆
