◆◆◆

IV.I Boot, reboot, and shut down a system normally

How to boot, shut down, and suspend your system from the Linux command line

Important

● Sometimes, restarting the system is a key component of troubleshooting.

Boot the System

Simplified overview of the entire Linux boot and startup process:

1\. You power up the system.   

2\. The firmware (BIOS or UEFI) does minimal hardware initialization and hands over control to the boot loader.  

3\. The boot loader starts, and the Master Boot Record (MBR) or GUID Partition Table (GPT) loads.  

4\. The operating system selection menu appears.  

5\. The boot loader loads the selected kernel.  

6\. The kernel loads an initial RAM disk that loads the system drives and mount the root filesystem.  

7\. Once the kernel is set up, it launches systemd as PID 1.  

8\. systemd mount the host’s file systems and start services.  

9\. The default.target file loads.  

10\. The user is prompted to authenticate.  

11\. The system launches to the default.target (providing either a command-line interface or graphical environment).

Reboot the System

Restarting the computer helps solve many problems and may be necessary to finish some configurations. However, restarting is considered downtime on the server and should be avoided if possible.

Important

● Try using the systemctl restart service_name to restart the services instead of restarting the whole system. However, sometimes a restart is required.

You can restart the system using either of these commands:

# systemctl reboot

OR

# reboot

Shutdown the System

To shut down the system, you can either use the systemctl utility, or call the shutdown command directly.

$ systemctl poweroff

OR

$ shutdown

Note that

● The shutdown command schedules a system shutdown after 1 minute, and you can use the "shutdown -c" command to cancel. While the poweroff command directly turns off the system.

● The shutdown command has two options: --halt and --poweroff. The --halt option stops the operating system while the --poweroff option shuts down the system.

Shutting down the system using the shutdown command

The advantage of using the shutdown command is:

●        The support for time argument

●        The option to cancel the shutdown

One of the main benefits of the shutdown command is that you are able to define a shutdown delay to give users time to save their work and log off the system. You can schedule a time that suits your needs by using the format hh:mm (24-hour time designations). Let’s take some examples:

To shut down the system and power off the machine at a certain time, you can use the command:

# shutdown --poweroff hh:mm

Where hh:mm is the time in 24 hour clock format.

To shut down and halt the system after a delay without powering off the machine, you can use the command:

# shutdown --halt +m

Where +m is the delay time in minutes.

To halt the system at 10pm, type:

# shutdown --halt 22:00

To halt the system after a five-minute delay, type:

# shutdown --halt +5

You can attach a message to all users by entering it after setting the time. For example, if you want all users to know that the system will shutdown in 10 minutes:

# shutdown --halt +10 “Attention! The system will be closed within ten minutes.”

Also, you can cancel a pending shutdown using the command:

# shutdown -c

Shutting down the system using the systemctl command

To shut down the system and power off the machine, use the command:

# systemctl poweroff

To shut down and halt the system without powering off the machine, use the command:

# systemctl halt

Suspend the system

To suspend the system, you can run the following command:

# systemctl suspend

Suspending the system saves the system state in RAM and turns off most of the hardware in the device. When the device restarts, the system restores its state from the RAM without having to boot again. 

Note that

● Restoring the system from suspended mode is much faster than restoring the system from hibernation.

Hibernating the system

To hibernate the system, you can run the following command:

# systemctl hibernate

This command saves the system state to the hard disk and turns off the machine. When the machine restarts, the system restores its state from the saved data without having to boot again.

◆◆◆

IV.II Boot systems into different targets manually

Working with systemd targets

The target units file ends with .target, is a representation of systemd targets. Its sole purpose is to group other systemd units through a series of dependencies. Each .target unit can start other .target unit and a set of required services.

Systemd Targets Description

![Systemd Targets Description](image_rsrcZV3.jpg)

Viewing the default target

The default target unit is represented by the file /etc/systemd/system/default.target.

To specify which target unit is used by default:

$ systemctl get-default

To specify the default target using a symbolic link:

$ ls -l /usr/lib/systemd/system/default.target

To display the active units only, use the command:

$ systemctl list-units

To list all loaded units regardless of their state:

$ systemctl list-units --type target --all

To list all target units currently loaded:

$ systemctl list-units --type target

Changing the default target

You can use the following procedure to change the default target using the systemctl command:

1\. To specify the default target unit:

# systemctl get-default

2\. To set a default target unit:

# systemctl set-default multi-user.target

3\. Remove the file default.target:

# rm /etc/systemd/system/default.target

4\. To make the file /etc/systemd/system/default.target a symlink to /usr/lib/systemd/system/name.target, where name is the name of the target module you want to use: 

# ln -s /usr/lib/systemd/system/multi-user.target /etc/systemd/system/default.target

5\. Reboot the system:

# reboot

Changing the default target using a symbolic link

You can use the following procedure to change the default target by creating a symbolic link to the target.

Procedure

1\. To show the default target unit:

# ls /usr/lib/systemd/system/default.target -l

2\. To create a symbolic link:

# ln -sf /usr/lib/systemd/system/graphical.target /etc/systemd/system/default.target

3\. To reboot the system:

# reboot

4\. To verify the newly created default.target:

$ systemctl get-default

Changing the target unit using systemctl 

You can use the following procedure to change the default target unit using systemctl.

Procedure

1\. To switch to a different target unit in the current session:

# systemctl isolate multi-user.target

2\. To verify:

$ systemctl get-default

multi-user.target

Note that

● Using the command # systemctl isolate xxxx.target starts the xxxx target unit and all dependent units, and stops all others.

● You can replace xxxx with the name of the target unit you want to use by default.

Booting to rescue mode

Rescue mode provides a single-user environment and allows you to repair your system in situations where the system can't boot. In rescue mode, the system tries to mount all the local file systems and start some important system services, but it does not activate the network interfaces or allow more users to log into the system at the same time.

To switch to rescue mode in the current session, use:

# systemctl rescue

OR

# systemctl isolate rescue.target

To switch to rescue mode in the current session and prevent systemd from sending an informational message to all users currently logged in the system, use:

# systemctl --no-wall rescue

Booting to emergency mode

Emergency mode provides the minimum possible environment that allows you to repair your system even in situations where the system cannot enter rescue mode. In emergency mode, the system mounts the root file system as read-only, does not attempt to mount any other local file systems, does not activate network interfaces, and only starts some basic services.

To switch to emergency mode, use:

# systemctl emergency

OR

# systemctl isolate emergency.target

To switch to emergency mode in the current session and prevent systemd from sending an informational message to all users currently logged in the system, use:

# systemctl --no-wall emergency

Note that

● If you have disabled the graphical target unit and cannot see the login prompt, you can switch to a different TTY console using the CTRT+ALT+F1 to F6 key combination.

◆◆◆

IV.III Interrupt the boot process to gain access to a system

How to interrupt the Linux boot process

When you see the grand unified bootloader (GRUB) screen, you will see the default option highlighted (the latest kernel installed on the machine), then press E to interrupt the boot process. This shows the boot parameters assigned to the default menu selection.

Procedure (RHEL 8)

1\. Restart the system.

2\. Catch the grub screen.

3\. Stop on your kernel line and press “E” to enter the editing mode.

4\. Find the line that starts with Linux and at the end of that line replace “rhgb quiet” with “rd.break”

5\. Press Ctrl+X to enter the emergency mode.

6\. # mount –o remount,rw /sysroot

7\. # chroot /sysroot

8\. # passwd

9\. # touch /.autorelabel

10\. Press “Ctrl+D twice”.

Procedure (RHEL 9)

1\. To reboot the system:

# reboot

2\. On the GRUB 2 boot screen, use the down arrow key on your keyboard to select the rescue mode.

3\. Press the E key to interrupt the boot process.

4\. Go to the end of the line that starts with linux by pressing Ctrl+E.

5\. Add rd.break to the end of the line that starts with linux.

6\. Press Ctrl+X to start the system with the changed parameters.

7\. Press the Enter key to enter maintenance mode and the sh-5.1# prompt will appear.

8\. To remount the file system as writable, use:

# mount -o remount,rw /sysroot

9\. To enter the chroot environment, use:

# chroot /sysroot

10\. To reset the root password, use:

# passwd

11\. To enable the SELinux relabeling process on the following system boot, use:

# touch /.autorelabel

12\. To exit the chroot environment and the maintenance mode press Ctrl+D twice.

Note that

● Interrupting the boot process is useful for troubleshooting and maintenance.

● GRUB screen is a text menu that allows you to select the kernel version you want to boot into.

● By default, GRUB assumes you want to boot to the latest kernel installed on the machine.

Important

● If you use full disk encryption when installing your operating system, no one can access your drive without your passphrase, even from a pre-boot environment.

● You can use the fsck tools to check your system for problems.

● File system check tools cannot repair hardware problems.

● A file system must be fully readable and writeable if the repair is to work successfully.

◆◆◆

IV.IV Identify CPU/memory intensive processes and kill processes

What is a process?

An instance of a running program is called a process. Every time you run a shell command, a program is run and a process is created for it. 

Note that

● Processes are also frequently referred to as tasks.

● Several processes may be associated with the same program.

The lifecycle of a process

A new process (referred to as a child) is normally created when an existing process (referred to as a parent) makes an exact copy of itself in memory (using the fork() ). The child process will have the same environment as its parent, but only the process ID number is different. Every new process is assigned a unique process ID (PID) for tracking and security. The process ID (PID) and the parent's process ID (PPID) are elements of the new process environment. 

Note that

●  Parent processes – are processes that create other processes during run-time.

● Child processes – are processes created by other processes during run-time.

Any process may create a child process. All processes are descendants of the first system process, which is systemd(1) on a Red Hat Enterprise Linux system. 

The systemd process is the mother (or parent) of all processes on the system, it’s the first program that is executed when the Linux system boots up; it manages all other processes on the system. It is started by the kernel itself, so in principle, it does not have a parent process.

The systemd process always has a process ID of 1\. It functions as an adoptive parent for all orphaned processes. You can use the pidof command to find the ID of a process. The syntax is:

$ pidof [options] program

The Fork and exec functions

Through the fork routine, a child process inherits security identities, previous and current file descriptors, port and resource privileges, environment variables, and program code. A child process may then exec its program code. Normally, a parent process sleeps while the child process runs, setting a request (wait ) to be signaled when the child completes. Upon exit, the child process has already closed or discarded its resources and environment; the remainder is referred to as a zombie. The parent, signaled awake when the child exited, cleans the remaining structure, then continues with its program code execution.

Process states

In a multitasking operating system, each CPU or CPU core can be working on one process at a single point in time. As a process runs, its immediate requirements for CPU time and resource allocation change. Processes are assigned a state, which changes as circumstances require. Process states define what the process is doing and what it is expected to do in the near time. The performance of the system depends on a major number of process states.

From birth (spawn) to death (kill/terminate or exit), the process has a life cycle going through several states. Some processes exist in the process table even after they are killed/died, those processes are called zombie processes.

Broadly process states are:

● Running or Runnable

● Sleeping or waiting

● Stopped

● Zombie

Let's dive into each state!

Process state: Running

Kernel-defined state name TASK_RUNNING

The healthiest state of all. It indicates a process is active and serving its requests. The process is properly getting system resources (especially CPU) to perform its operations. The Running process is a process that is being served by the CPU currently. It can be identified by state flag R in ps or top commands output as you will see later in this section in listing processes.

Process state: Runnable

The process state Runnable is when a process has got all the system resources to perform its operation except the CPU. This means the process is ready to go once the CPU is free. Runnable processes are also flagged with the state flag R.

Process state: Sleeping

The Sleeping process is the one that waits for resources to run. Since it’s on a waiting stand, it gives up CPU and goes to sleep mode. Once its required resource is free, it gets placed in the scheduler queue for the CPU to execute.

There are three types of sleep modes: Interruptible, Uninterruptible, and Killable

● Interruptible sleep mode

Kernel-defined state name TASK_ INTERRUPTIBLE

In this mode, the process is waiting for some conditions: hardware request, system resource access, or signal. When an event or signal satisfies the condition, the process returns to Running. These processes are shown with state S in ps or top output.

● Uninterruptible sleep mode

Kernel-defined state name TASK_ UNINTERRUPTIBLE

Process in this sleep mode gets its timeout value before going to sleep. Once the timeout sets off, it awakes. Or it awakes when waited-upon resources become available for it. It can be identified by state D in outputs.

● Killable sleep mode

Kernel-defined state name TASK_ KILLABLE

This sleep mode is identical to the uninterruptible D state but modified to allow the waiting task to respond to a signal to be killed (exited completely). This process flag is K but utilities frequently display Killable processes as D state.

Process state: Stopped

The process state Stopped has two Kernel-defined states and both have the same T flag (state):

1\. TASK_ STOPPED

TASK_STOPPED: The process has been Stopped (suspended), usually by being signaled by a user or another process. The process can be continued (resumed) by another signal to return to Running.

2\. TASK_TRACED

TASK_TRACED: A process that is being debugged is also temporarily Stopped and shares the same T state flag.

Process state: Zombie

The process state Zombie has two Kernel-defined states:

1\. EXIT_ZOMBIE - Z state flag

EXIT_ZOMBIE: A child process signals its parent as it exits. All resources except for the process identity (PlD) are released. It can be identified by state Z in outputs.

2\. EXIT_DEAD – X state flag

EXIT_DEAD: After the parent receives the exit signal from the child process, the parent cleans up (reaps) the remaining child process x structure and removes the process slot in the process table, the process is now released completely. This state will never be observed in process-listing utilities.

Note that

● The process can stay in zombie mode if its parent died before it releases the child process’s slot in the process table. It can be identified with Z in outputs.

The complete life cycle of a process can be circled as:

1\. Spawn  

2\. Waiting  

3\. Runnable  

4\. Running  

5\. Stopped  

6\. Zombie  

7\. Removed from the process table

Listing processes

How to View Active Processes in Linux?

There are several Linux tools for viewing and listing running processes on the system, the two traditional and well-known are ps and top commands:

Let’s try using the ps command:

$ ps

The ps “Process Status” command is used for listing current processes. The command can provide detailed process information. It reads the process information from the virtual files in the /proc file system. The ps command provides numerous options for manipulating the output according to your needs.

Note that

● By default, ps with no options selects all processes with the same effective user ID ( EUID) as the current user and associated with the same terminal where ps was invoked.

Adding the aux option to the ps command will provide a full listing of the current processes:

$ ps aux | less

● The a option tells ps to list the processes of all users on the system rather than just those of the current user.

● The u option tells ps to provide detailed information about each process.

● The x option adds to the list of processes that have no controlling terminal, such as daemons that are started during booting.

Note that

● As the number of processes can be quite long and occupy more than a single screen, here, I piped the output of the ps aux command to the less command, which lets it be viewed one screenful at a time. The output can be advanced one screen forward by pressing the SPACE bar and moved one screen backward by pressing the b key.

● Among the information that ps aux provides about each process is the effective user name of the process owner (USER), the process ID (PID), the percentage of CPU used by the process, the percentage of memory used by the process, the virtual memory size in kilobytes (VSZ), the non-swapped physical memory size in kilobytes (RSS), the location of process STDOUT, known as the controlling terminal, STAT (the process state code), the starting time of the process, the length of time the process has been active and the command that initiated the process.

The ps command output can display in a tree format to view parent/child relationships using the pstree command:

$ pstree

The addition of the -p option will also show the PIDs:

$ pstree -p

Monitoring Process Activity

Let’s understand two important phrases in a Linux system:

1\. System load/CPU Load  

2\. Load average

1\. System load/CPU Load

The system load is a measurement of the computational work the system is performing. This measurement is displayed as a number. A completely idle computer has a load average of 0\. Each running process either using or waiting for CPU resources adds 1 to the load average. So, if your system has a load of 4, then four processes are either using or waiting for the CPU.

2\. Load average

The load average is useful to understand how your system is performing. If it’s overloaded, you may need to deal with a process that’s wasting resources, provide more hardware resources, or move some of the workloads to another system.

The easiest, most standardized way to see your load average is to run the uptime command in a terminal. This command shows your machine’s load average as well as how long it’s been powered on.

Understanding the Load Average Output

Here’s an example load average readout:

load average: 1.02, 0.70, 3.09

From left to right, these numbers show you the average load over the last one minute, the last five minutes, and the last fifteen minutes:

●Load average over the last 1 minute: 1.02

●Load average over the last 5 minutes: 0.70

●Load average over the last 15 minutes: 3.09

What Do the Numbers Mean, Exactly?

Let’s use the above numbers to understand what the load average means. Assuming you’re using a single-CPU system, the numbers tell us that:

● Over the last 1 minute: The computer was overloaded by 2% on average. On average, .02 processes were waiting for the CPU. (1.02)

● Over the last 5 minutes: The CPU idled for 30% of the time. (0.70)

● Over the last 15 minutes: The computer was overloaded by 209% on average. On average, 2.09 processes were waiting for the CPU. (3.09)

You probably have a system with multiple CPUs or a multi-core CPU. The load average numbers work a bit differently on such a system. For example, if you have a load average of 2:

● On a single-CPU system, this means your system was overloaded by 100 percent — the entire period, one process was using the CPU while one other process was waiting. 

● On a system with two CPUs, this would be complete usage, two different processes were using two different CPUs the entire time. 

● On a system with four CPUs, this would be half usage, two processes were using two CPUs, while two CPUs were sitting idle.

Note that

● If you divided the displayed load average values by the number of logical CPUs in the system:

● A value below 1, will indicate satisfactory resource utilization and minimal wait times.

● A value above 1, will indicate resource saturation and some amount of service waiting times.

Monitoring CPU Utilization on Red Hat Enterprise Linux

CPU utilization can be done graphically using GNOME System Monitor, ps, sar, or top commands in a terminal shell. 

Using the GNOME System Monitor Tool

The Processes tab of the System Monitor tool allows you to view, search for, change the priority of, and kill processes from the graphical user interface. 

To install the tool, issue the following command as root:

# yum install gnome-system-monitor

To start the System Monitor tool, either select Applications > System Tools > System Monitor from the panel or type gnome-system-monitor at a shell prompt. Then click the Processes tab to view the list of running processes.

For each listed process, the System Monitor tool displays its name (Process Name), current status (Status), percentage of the CPU usage (% CPU), nice value (Nice), process ID (ID), memory usage (Memory), the channel the process is waiting in (Waiting Channel), and additional details about the session (Session). 

To sort the information by a specific column in ascending order, click the name of that column. Click the name of the column again to toggle the sort between ascending and descending order. By default, the System Monitor tool displays a list of processes that are owned by the current user. 

Selecting various options from the View menu allows you to:

● View only active processes.

● View all processes.

● View your processes.

● View process dependencies.

● Refresh the list of processes.

● View a memory map of a selected process.

● View the files opened by a selected process.

Additionally, various options in the Edit menu allow you to:

● Stop a process,

● Continue running a stopped process,

● End a process,

● Kill a process,

● Change the priority of a selected process, and

● Edit the System Monitor preferences, such as the refresh interval for the list of processes, or what information to show.

Note that

● You can show any process properties by selecting it and clicking the show Process Properties button.

● You can also end a process by selecting it from the list and clicking the End Process button.

Let’s try running ps, sar, and top commands and taking a look at their output!

The top command

The top command is the best resource monitoring tool that can provide you with an in-depth representation of CPU utilization, to use it run:

$ top

The sar command

To get a full report of server activity, run:

$ sar -A

The ps command

To get details about processes, run:

$ ps -edf

The top command

The top command provides a screen-oriented means of displaying processes running on your system. With top, the default is to display processes based on how much CPU time they are currently consuming. However, you can sort by other columns as well. After you identify a misbehaving process, you can also use the top utility to kill (completely end) or renice (reprioritize) that process.

If you want to be able to kill or renice processes, you need to run top as the root user. If you just want to display processes, and possibly kill or change your processes, you can do that as a regular user. 

General information about your system appears at the top of the top output, followed by information about each running process. At the top, you can see how long the system has been up, how many users are currently logged in to the system, and how much demand there has been on the system for the past 1, 5, and 10 minutes.

Other general information includes how many processes (tasks) are currently running, how much CPU is being used, and how much RAM and swap are available and being used.

Note that

● By default, all this information is redisplayed every 5 seconds.

Actions you can do with the top command include:

● Press h to see help options, and then press any key to return to the top display.

● Press M to sort by memory usage instead of CPU, and then press P to return to sorting by CPU.

● Press the number 1 to toggle showing CPU usage of all your CPUs, if you have more than one CPU on your system.

● Press R to reverse sort your output.

● Press u and enter a username to display processes only for a particular user.

● A common practice is to use the top utility to find processes that are consuming too much memory or processing power and then act on those processes in some way. A process consuming too much CPU can be reniced to give it less priority to the processors. A process consuming too much memory can be killed. 

With top running, here’s how to renice or kill a process:

● Renicing a process: Note the process ID of the process you want to renice and press r. When the PID to renice: a message appears, type the process ID of the process you want to renice. When prompted to Renice PID to value: type in a number from -19 to 20. (See “Setting processorpriority with nice and renice” later in this chapter for information on the meanings of different renice values.)

● Killing a process: Note the process ID of the process you want to kill and press k. Type 15 to terminate cleanly or 9 to just kill the process outright. 

Killing Processes

Objectives

After completing this section, you should be able to:

● Use commands to kill and communicate with processes.

● Define the characteristics of a daemon process.

● End user sessions and processes.

What are signals in Linux?

Signals are software interrupts sent to a program to indicate that an event has occurred. Events that generate a signal can be an error, an external event (e.g., an expired timer), or by explicit request (e.g., a signal-sending command).

Process control using signals 

The fundamental signals used by system administrators for process management. 

![Process control using signals](image_rsrcZV4.jpg)

Note that

● You can refer to signals by either their short (e.g., HUP), or proper name (e.g., SIGHUP), or by number (e.g., -1). 

● Each signal has a default action, usually one of the following:

o Term: The default action is to terminate the process.

o Core: Cause a program to save a memory image (core dump), then terminate.

o Stop: Cause a program to stop executing (suspend) and wait to continue (resume).

o Cont: The default action is to continue the process if it is currently stopped.

● For a complete list and description of the signals, you can refer to the manual for signal(7) running:

$ man 7 signal

The kill Command

sends a signal to the process via the process identifier (PID) using the [kill pid] syntax. You can use the kill command to send any signal to a process, not just the process termination signals, by specifying the signal number after the kill command using the [kill -signal pid] syntax. 

Note that

● If you don't specify a signal, the kill command will send a TERM [15] signal by default.

● You can run the kill command with the -l option to see signals on the system with their corresponding numbers: $ kill -l

For example:

$ sleep 100 &

$ ps

$ kill pid

To verify:

$ ps

Or you can provide the signal number using the command:

$ sleep 100 &

$ kill -15 pid

To verify:

$ ps

Also, you can use the killall command to send a signal to one or more processes matching selection criteria, such as a command name, processes owned by a specific user, or all system-wide processes.

Let’s take some examples:

$ sleep 100 &

Now to kill the sleep command using the killall command:

$ killall sleep

To verify:

$ ps

Another example is to use the killall command to kill multiple commands at the same time:

$ yelp &

$ sleep 100 &

$ killall yelp sleep

To verify:

$ ps

The pkill command

The pkill command can signal multiple processes of a running program based on certain criteria. It uses advanced selection criteria, which can include combinations of full or partial command names, UID, GID, parent process, or Terminal.

To show more details, you can refer to the manual for the pkill command running:

$ pkill --help

Logging users out administratively 

The w Command

The w command displays the users currently registered in the system and their activities.

$ w

You can use the TTY and FROM columns to locate the user. All users have a control terminal listed as pts/N (PTS stands for pseudo terminal slave). You can use the -f option to show the remote hostname field. Remote users display their connection system name in the FROM column when using the -f option.

$ w -f

You can discover how long a user has been on the system by viewing the session login time [LOGIN@].

For each session, the CPU resources consumed by current jobs, including background tasks and children, are in the JCPU column. And the current consumption of the foreground process CPU is in the PCPU column.

Note that

● Users may be forced out of the system due to security breaches, resource overload, or other administrative needs.

● Users should terminate unnecessary applications, close unused command shells, and exit login sessions on demand.

● When users cannot be contacted or have unresponsive sessions, erratic resource consumption, or improper access to the system, the system administrator may end their sessions using signals.

● Since the initial process in a login session is designed to handle session termination requests and ignore unintended keyboard flags, killing all user processes and login shells requires the use of the SIGKILL signal.

The pgrep Command

The pgrep command searches for processes that are currently running on the system, based on complete or partial process name, username, or other specified attributes.

To list all the processes owned by the user test using the pgrep command, use the following command:

# pgrep -l -u test

Here, I used the -l option to list the process id and process name, and -u to match by effective ID.

To kill all the processes owned by the user test, run:

# pkill -u test

To verify:

# pgrep -l -u test

Notice that, using this command the session leader, which is the bash login shell handled session termination requests, but all other session processes are terminated.

To kill all of a user's processes including the login shell using the SIGKILL signal, run:

# pkill -SIGKILL -u test

To verify:

# pgrep -l -u test

Note that

● You can determine the controlling terminal for the session using the tty command: $ tty

To kill the user test terminal, run:

# pkill -t pts/N

You can use the pstree -p command to view a process tree for the system or a single user. Then use the parent process's ID to kill all children they have created. For example:

# pstree -p test

Now to kill these processes using the parent Process id:

# pkill -P ppid

Note that, the -P option matches only the child processes of the given parent.

To verify:

# pgrep -l -u test

And if you want to kill these processes using the SIGKILL signal, run:

# pkill -SIGKILL -P ppid

To verify:

# pgrep -l -u test

Notice that, here the parent bash login shell survives because the signal is directed only at its child processes.

◆◆◆

IV.V Adjust process scheduling

The Nice Number

The priority or importance of a job is indicated by its nice number. A program with a high nice number is friendly to other programs, other users, and the system; It is not an important job. The lower the nice number, the more important a job is and the more resources it will take without sharing.

Making a task nicer by increasing its nice number is only useful for processes that use a lot of CPU time (e.g., compilers). Processes that always use a lot of I/O time are automatically given higher priority by the system (which means a lower nice number). For example, keyboard input always gets the highest priority in the system.

Defining the priority of a program is done with the nice command. Also, you can use the renice command to change the niceness of a running command. 

To start a process with a niceness value other than the default value, use the following syntax:

$ nice -n nice_value process name

If there is some process already running on the system then you can Renice its value using the syntax:

$ renice nice_value -p pid

To change Niceness, you can use the top command to determine the PID (process id) and its Nice value. Then use the renice command to change the value. Or you can directly renice the process with top running.

A common practice is to use the top utility to find processes that are consuming too much memory or processing power and then act on those processes in some way:

● A process consuming too much CPU can be reniced to give it less priority to the processors.

● A process consuming too much memory can be killed.

With top running, here’s how to renice a process:

1\. Note the Process ID (PID) of the process you want to renice.

2\. Press R.

3\. When the PID to renice message appears, type the process ID of the process you want to renice. 

4\. When prompted to Renice PID to value, type in a number from -19 to 20. 

Note that

● The default nice value of all the processes is 0, but if you start the command with nice it'll be 10.

● NI – is the nice value, which is a user-space concept.

● PR or PRI – is the process’s actual priority, as seen by the Linux kernel.

How to Calculate PR or PRI Values?

● Total number of priorities = 140

● Real-time priority range (PR or PRI): 0 to 99

● Userspace priority range: 100 to 139

● Nice value range (NI): -20 to 19

PR = 20 + NI

PR = 20 + (-20 to + 19)

PR = 0 to 39 which is the same as 100 to 139

Setting the process priority using the chrt utility

The chrt utility checks and modifies scheduler policies and priorities. It can start new processes with the desired properties, or change the properties of a running process.

To check the attributes of a particular process, run:

# chrt -p pid

OR

# chrt --pid pid

To set the priority of a process to SCHED_FIFO, run:

# chrt -f -p priority_value pid

Note that, the -f or --fifo option sets the schedule to SCHED_FIFO.

To set the priority of a process to SCHED_OTHER, run:

# chrt -o -p priority_value pid

Note that, the -o or --other option sets the schedule to SCHED_OTHER.

To start a new application with a given policy and priority, run:

# chrt -f priority_value app_name

◆◆◆

IV.VI Manage tuning profiles

How to manage tuning profiles in Linux?

You can use the tuned utility to monitor and optimize your Linux system for specific workloads. tuned is a Linux feature that uses profiles to monitor and improve system performance under certain workloads. A profile is a set of rules that define certain system parameters such as disk settings, kernel parameters, network optimization settings, and many other aspects of the system. Using tuned is a way to put optimization settings together in a single file and apply them to the system. tuned comes with some default profiles that you can apply statically or dynamically.

Applying tuned profile statically

To apply a profile statically, you can select a profile based on the workload. The system uses this selection until you manually switch to a different profile. Static profiles are enabled by default on Red Hat Enterprise Linux (RHEL).

Applying tuned profile dynamically

Dynamic option changes profiles based on live activity data from monitored system components, ensuring optimum performance.

Install tuned

By default, tuned is installed on RHEL systems. If not installed, you can use the following commands to install, start and enable tuned: 

# dnf install -y tuned

# systemctl enable --now tuned

Note that

● You can install additional tuned profiles for workloads that are not in the default profiles.

● tuned comes with a frontend for interacting with the tuneD daemon called tuned-adm.

To search for tuned profiles in your repository, run: 

# dnf search tuned-profiles

To install a tuned profile, run:

# dnf install -y tuned-profiles-name

List Tuned profiles

To list all available tuned profiles on your system, run:

# tuned-adm list

Basic tuned profiles

![Basic tuned profiles](image_rsrcZV5.jpg)

To display the currently active profile, run:

$ tuned-adm active

To show tuned recommendations for the most suitable profile for your system, run:

$ tuned-adm recommend

To activate a profile, run:

$ tuned-adm profile profile_name

To show the current active tuned profile on your system, run:

$ tuned-adm active

To verify that the tuned profile is active and applied, run:

$ tuned-adm verify

Note that

● The tuned log file is /var/log/tuned/tuned.log.

◆◆◆

IV.VII Locate and interpret system log files and journals

How to find and interpret system log files on Linux?

Log files and journals are important to a system administrator for troubleshooting, as they contain events and messages generated by the kernel, applications, and users that log into the system.

Use rsyslog

When systemd became the default service manager with Red Hat Enterprise Linux, it introduced a new logging system called systemd-journald. Which is the logging mechanism on RHEL 8 and 9 while keeping rsyslog for backward compatibility.

Note that

● The rsyslog service log files are in the /var/log directory.

To view boot and other kernel messages, run:

$ cat /var/log/messages

To view log files as new log file entries are added, run:

$ tail -f /var/log/messages

To view users and their activities, run:

$ tail -f /var/log/secure

Use systemd-journald

systemd-journald saves the events and messages in a binary format. To show saved events and messages, you can query the journal with the journalctl command.

To show all event messages, run:

$ journalctl

To view the last x event messages, run:

$ journalctl -n x

To show new journal entries as they are added to the journal, run:

$ journalctl -f

To show the kernel message log from the last boot:

$ journalctl -k

To query all messages related to a particular user, find the user's ID (UID) and use that to perform the query. 

To query all messages related to a user with the user's ID (UID) 1111,

$ journalctl _UID=1000

Note that

● To get the user id (UID), you can run: $ id username

● The journalctl command has several choices that can make querying the journal easier. You can run the $ journalctl --help command to list the available options.

Manage log forwarding

systemd-journald does not have a mechanism to forward logs to external systems and monitoring applications. But you can configure the ForwardToSyslog parameter in the /etc/systemd/journald.conf file to enable entries in the journal to be forwarded to syslog.

When enabled, syslog captures the entries as they come through systemd-journald and forwards them accordingly.

◆◆◆

IV.VIII How to configure your system to preserve system logs after a reboot

Edit your systemd-journald configuration to store journal entries for as long as you need them.

Log entries for the systemd-journald service, do not persist across reboots. However, there is a workaround process that you can do to keep log entries even after a reboot. In this process we will create a storage location (directory), then we will edit the journald configuration to direct messages to that location.

Procedure

1\. Create a storage directory:

# mkdir /var/log/journal

2\. Edit the journald.conf file to set the Storage parameter to persistent (By default, it is set to auto):

# vim /etc/systemd/journald.conf

Storage=persistent

3\. Restart systemd-journald service:

# systemctl restart systemd-journald

4\. Reboot the server to confirm the persistence of entries 

# reboot

5\. To verify, you can list the /var/log/journal content:

$ ls /var/log/journal

◆◆◆

IV.IX Linux network services: How to start, stop, and check their status

Objective

In this section, you will learn how to use the systemd command to manage your network services.

What is a service?

A service is a process that runs on a computer, generally in the background. For instance, a network service runs at the application layer to provide some form of service over a network. 

Red Hat Enterprise Linux (RHEL) provides an environment where these network services can run, and it provides the systemd utility to help you manage these services.

The systemd process is the first process started on the system with PID 1, and it is responsible for starting the rest of the system. With systemd, you can start, stop, and check the status of the services running on your server.

Note that, you will use the systemctl command to interact with systemd.

What is NetworkManager?

NetworkManager is a system network service that discovers and configures network interfaces connected to the system to maintain an active network connection.

Managing Network Services

To list all loaded services on a server, run:

# systemctl list-units --type=service

To start the NetworkManager service, run:

# systemctl start NetworkManager

To stop the NetworkManager service, run:

# systemctl stop NetworkManager

To reload the NetworkManager service, run:

# systemctl reload NetworkManager

To show the status of the NetworkManager service, run:

# systemctl status NetworkManager

To verify whether the NetworkManager service is active, run: 

$ systemctl is-active NetworkManager

To configure the NetworkManager service to start automatically with the system, run:

# systemctl enable sshd

To start and enable the NetworkManager service at the same time, add the --now option:

# systemctl enable NetworkManager --now

To verify whether the NetworkManager service is enabled, run:

$ systemctl is-enabled NetworkManager

To disable the NetworkManager service, run:

# systemctl disable NetworkManager

Note that

● When a service is enabled, it starts automatically at boot. When it's disabled, it doesn't start automatically at boot.

◆◆◆

IV.X Securely transfer files between systems

What are SCP and SFTP?

In Red Hat Enterprise Linux (RHEL), SFTP (Secure File Transfer Protocol) and SCP (Secure Copy) are handy commands for securely transferring files between systems. They rely on Secure Shell (SSH) to transfer files.

Note that

● Using secure file copy commands like scp and sftp is an important part of network hardening.

Using SCP and SFTP to Securely Transfer Files

How to copy files using SCP?

To transfer a file test using the scp command to destination server1:/home/sam as  user sam, run:

$ scp test sam@server1:/home/sam/

This example copies the file test on the local server to /home/sam/ on the remote server server1.

If the SSH server uses a different port, say 2140, then run:

$ scp -P 2140 test sam@server1:/home/sam/

To copy a directory named backup, run:

$ scp -r backup sam@server1:/home/sam/

Important

● The user you are connecting with must have permission to do the operation you want.

● If the file you are copying already exists at the destination, SCP replaces or overwrites the content.

How to copy files using SFTP?

When you initiate an SFTP connection, it connects to its destination and enters interactive mode on the remote server. You can then transfer the files using commands like get, put, cd and rmdir.

To create an SFTP connection to server1 with user sam, run:

$ sftp sam@server1

This command will provide you with a command prompt similar to the following:

sftp>

If SSH is running on an alternate port, run:

$ sftp -oPort=2140 sam@server1

To transfer the file /etc/test file to /etc directory on the remote server server1, use the following procedure:

$ sftp sam@server1

sftp> cd /etc

sftp> put /etc/test

To download a file named /home/test from the remote server server1 to the local system, use the following procedure:

$ sftp sam@server1

sftp> cd /home

sftp> get test

To upload the directory backup, run:

sftp> put -r backup 

To download the directory backup, run:

sftp> get -r backup

Note that

● SFTP uses SSH port 22.

● For additional options, use the sftp --help command or consult the manual pages by typing the command:

$ man sftp.

◆◆◆

Chapter V: Configure local storage

Chapter V Contents

● V.I List, create, and delete partitions on MBR and GPT disks

● V.II Create and remove physical volumes

● V.III Assign physical volumes to volume groups

● V.IV Create and delete logical volumes

● V.V Configure systems to mount file systems at boot by universally unique ID (UUID) or label

● V.VI Add new partitions and logical volumes, and swap to a system non-destructively

◆◆◆

V.I List, create, and delete partitions on MBR and GPT disks

How to list, create and delete partitions on MBR and GPT disks?

Disk partitions are the basis of storing our data on disks. To be able to handle partitions, I will add an empty disk to our test RHEL 9 system, and create, list, and delete a new partition on it. First, we will use the classic MBR (Master Boot Record) setup, then we’ll do the same on GPT (GUID Partitioning Table) setup. 

GPT vs MBR

GPT is a more advanced partitioning technique that allows large partitions, whereas MBR is limited to 2 TB of disk space per partition.

How to create a new MBR partition using fdisk?

Important

● It is recommended to create a backup before modifying partitions if your disk contains valuable data.

Here, the primary disk that contains the operating system is /dev/sda which is 20 GB in size, while the newly added raw disk is /dev/sdb which is 10 GB in size.

To list partitions using the fdisk command, run:

# fdisk -l

In the output, you will see the main disk /dev/sda with two partitions, /dev/sda1 and /dev/sda2 on it, and the new empty disk /dev/sdb with no partitions, as well as the logical volumes the system contains.

Creating a new partition with fdisk on RHEL 9

Procedure

1\. To create a new partition on the empty disk using the fdisk command, run:

# fdisk /dev/sdb

2\. Type n followed by pressing Enter to create a new partition.

3\. Type p followed by pressing Enter to create a primary partition, or you can press Enter directly as p is the default option.

4\. Enter the number of partitions you want, here I will create a single partition that will cover the disk

5\. Press Enter to select the first available sector to start with. 

6\. Press Enter to select the last sector to end with.

Note that, you can specify an exact size. For example, if we would like a partition of 2 GB in size, in the last sector you could type: +2G

7\. Type w to write the changes to the disk.

Note that, the changes are in-memory until we write them to the disk. 

8\. To notify the system regarding the changes, run:

# partprobe /dev/sdb

9\. To verify:

# fdisk -l /dev/sdb

You will see that the new disk now contains a new /dev/sdb1 partition that is ready to be used.

Deleting a partition with fdisk on RHEL 9

Procedure

1\. To specify the device you will work on, run:

# fdisk /dev/sdb

2\. Type d to delete the partition.

3\. Type w to write the changes to the disk.

4\. To notify the system regarding the changes, run:

# partprobe /dev/sdb

5\. To verify:

# fdisk -l /dev/sdb

How to create a GPT partition using the gdisk utility?

Procedure

1\. To create a new partition on the empty disk using the gdisk command, run:

# gdisk /dev/sdb  

2\. Type n followed by pressing Enter to create a new partition.

3\. Enter the number of partitions you want, here I will create a single partition that will cover the disk, or you can press Enter directly as the default selection is 1.

4\. Press Enter to select the first available sector to start with. 

5\. Press Enter to select the last sector to end with.

Note that, you can specify an exact size. For example, if we would like a partition of 2 GB in size, in the last sector you could type: +2G

6\. Type w to write the changes to the disk.

Note that, the changes are in-memory until we write them to the disk. 

7\. Press Y to confirm.

8\. To notify the system regarding the changes, run:

# partprobe /dev/sdb

9\. To verify, you can use either of these commands:

# gdisk -l /dev/sdb

OR

# fdisk -l /dev/sdb

You will see that the new disk now contains a new /dev/sdb1 partition that is ready to be used.

Deleting a partition with the gdisk utility on RHEL 9

Procedure

1\. To specify the device you will work on, run:

# gdisk /dev/sdb

2\. Type d to delete the partition.

3\. Type w to write the changes to the disk.

4\. Type Y to confirm.

5\. To notify the system regarding the changes, run:0

# partprobe /dev/sdb

6\. To verify, you can use either of these commands:

# gdisk -l /dev/sdb

OR

# fdisk -l /dev/sdb

◆◆◆

V.II Create and remove physical volumes

What is LVM?

LVM stands for Logical Volume Manager. It is a form of storage virtualization that provides system administrators with a more flexible approach to managing disk storage space than traditional partitioning by creating a layer of abstraction over physical storage.

LVM is based on three main concepts:

1\. Physical volume (pv): a partition or whole disk.

2\. Volume group (vg): a collection of physical volumes (PVs).

3\. Logical volume (lv): lv is part of vg that represents a mountable storage device. You can create a file system on the lv.

Note that

● A pv can only belong to one vg.

●  An lv can only belong to one vg.

● You can increase the lv size if you have space available in the linked vg file.

● If you don't have any space available on vg, you can add a new disk as pv, add it to vg, then you can increase your lv size.

● Creating or expanding logical volumes does not require any downtime.

To list information about all available block devices:

# lsblk -a

Note that

● The lsblk command lists the size of the partition while df lists the size of the filesystem.

Physical Volume Management

To create a physical volume (/dev/sdb), run:

# pvcreate /dev/sdb

To remove a physical volume (/dev/sdc) not belonging to any volume group, run:

# pvremove /dev/sdc

To get a list of the physical volumes, run:

# pvs

◆◆◆

V.III Assign physical volumes to volume groups

Volume Group Management

To create a volume group vg, using a physical volume /dev/sdb, run:

# vgcreate vg /dev/sdb

To create a volume group vg, using a physical volume /dev/sdb with a physical extent size of 8MB, run:

# vgcreate -s 8m vg /dev/sdb

To add a physical volume /dev/sdc to an existing volume group vg, run:

# vgextend vg /dev/sdc

To remove a physical volume /dev/sdc from an existing volume group vg, run:

# vgreduce vg /dev/sdc

To remove an existing volume group vg, run:

# vgremove vg

To get the list of the volume groups, run:

# vgs

◆◆◆

V.IV Create and delete logical volumes

Logical Volume Management

To create a logical volume lv with a size of 2GB in the vg volume group, run:

# lvcreate --size 2G --name lv vg

To remove a logical volume lv in the vg volume group, run:

# lvremove /dev/vg/lv_vol

To get the list of the logical volumes, run:

# lvs

Logical Volume Resizing

To resize logical volume lv to 4 GB in the volume group vg and resizes the file system automatically, run:

# lvresize -L 4G -r /dev/vg/lv

Note that

● The lvresize command should only be used when the volume is unmounted.

● The -r option resizes ext2, ext3, and ext4 in both directions, only the xfs file system supports growth.

Logical Volume Extend

To extend logical volume /dev/vg/lv by 1GB, run:

# lvextend --size +1G -r /dev/vg/lv

Note that

● The -r option is used to automatically resize the underlying filesystem together with the logical volume without any need to unmount it.

To reduce the size of a logical volume by 1GB, you need to do the following steps::

1\. Unmount the file system /dev/vg/lv:

# umount /dev/vg/lv

2\. Reduce the logical volume /dev/vg/lv and the associated file system, run:

# lvreduce --size -1G -r /dev/vg/lv

3\. Mount the file system /dev/vg/lv to the /mnt directory, run:

# mount /dev/vg/lv /mnt

◆◆◆

V.V Configure systems to mount file systems at boot by universally unique ID (UUID) or label

Mounting file systems

You can mount file systems on your system to access data on them.

On Linux, file systems on different partitions and removable devices can be attached to a mount point in the directory tree and then detached again. 

Note that

● While a file system is mounted on a directory, you can't access the original content of that directory.

● Linux does not prevent you from mounting a file system to a directory with a file system already attached to it.

To mount a device, you can identify it with any of the following:

● Universally unique identifier (UUID)

● Volume label (LABEL)

● Full path to a non-persistent block device (ex., /dev/sdb1)

The /etc/fstab file

The file systems table (fstab) file is designed to create a rule where certain file systems are detected and then mounted automatically in the order the user wants each time the system boots. The table itself is a 6-column structure, where each column defines a specific parameter and must be set up in the correct order. 

The columns of the table are as follows from left to right:

1\. Device: The given name or UUID of the mounted device.

2\. Mount Point: The directory where the device is/will be mounted.

3\. File System Type: Shows the type of filesystem in use.

4\. Options: lists active mount options. If using multiple options, they must be separated by commas.

5\. Backup Operation: This is a binary system where 1 = dump utility backup of a partition. 0 = no backup. Note that, this is an outdated backup method and should NOT be used. 

6\. File System Check Order: Here we can see three possible outcomes. 0 means that fsck will not check the file system. Numbers higher than this represent the check order. The root file system should be set to 1 and the other partitions should be set to 2.

To list the UUID and Label attributes, run:

$ lsblk --fs storage-device

Mounting a file system with the mount command

procedure

1\. Make sure that no file system is already mounted on your chosen mount point:

$ findmnt mount-point

2\. To attach a certain file system, run:

# mount device mount-point

For example, to mount a local XFS file system identified by UUID on /mnt:

# mount UUID= afa5d5e3-9050-48c3-acc1-bb30095f3dc4 /mnt

Note that

● If mount cannot automatically recognize the file system type, you can specify it using the --types option: # mount --types type device mount-point

Moving a mount point

The procedure for changing a mounted file system mount point to a different mount point (directory):

1\. To change the directory in which a file system is mounted:

# mount --move old-directory new-directory

For example, to move the file system mounted in the /mnt/ directory to the /media mount point, run:

# mount --move /mnt /media

2\. To verify that the file system has been moved, run:

$ findmnt

$ ls old-directory

$ ls new-directory

Unmounting a file system with umount

The procedure for unmounting a file system using the umount command:

1\. You can unmount the file system using either of the following commands:

● By mount point:

# umount mount-point

● By device:

# umount device

2\. If the command fails with an error “target is busy”, this means that a process is using the file system resources.

3\. If the file system is in use, you can use the fuser command to show the processes accessing it:

$ fuser -v mount-point

Note that, The -v option is used to get verbose output.

4\. To terminate (kill) the processes using the file system: 

$ fuser -k mount-point

Note that, The -k option is used to kill the process.

5\. To try unmounting it again, run:

# umount mount-point

OR

# umount device

Common mount options

![Common mount options](image_rsrcZV6.jpg)

Syntax:

# mount --options option1,option2,option3 device mount-point

Mount with UUID

Procedure

1\. To get the list of disks UUID, run:

# blkid

2\. To define the disk or partition that you want to mount and append it to the /etc/fstab file, run:

# blkid | grep lv >> /etc/fstab

3\. Then, edit the /etc/fstab file and change the newly added line with the correct parameters. For example:

UUID=" afa5d5e3-9050-48c3-acc1-bb30095f3dc4 " /mnt ext4 defaults 1 2

4\. To mount the disk or partition, run:

# mount /mnt

OR

# mount -a

Mount with label

Procedure

1\. To assign a label to a disk or partition /dev/sdb1, run:

# e2label /dev/sdb1 home

OR

# tune2fs -L home /dev/sdb1

2\. To get the label associated with a disk or a partition /dev/sdb1, run:

# e2label /dev/sdb1

3\. To mount an ext4 formatted partition /dev/sdb1, edit the /etc/fstab file and paste the following line:

LABEL=home /mnt ext4 defaults 1 2

4\. To mount the disk or partition, run:

# mount /mnt

OR

# mount -a

◆◆◆

V.VI Add new partitions and logical volumes, and swap to a system non-destructively

To add swap space to a system, you can use either of these ways:

● A logical volume inside a volume group.

● A new partition.

Add Swap Space using a Logical Volume 

Procedure

1\. Create a logical volume lv_swap with a size of 2G in the vg volume group:

# lvcreate --size 2G --name lv_swap vg

2\. Create the swap space on the logical volume:

# mkswap /dev/vg/lv_swap

3\. Activate the swap logical volume on the system:

# swapon /dev/vg/lv_swap

4\. You can use either of these commands to check the result:

# swapon -s

OR

# cat /proc/swaps

5\. Add the following line to the /etc/fstab file:

/dev/mapper/vg-lv_swap swap swap defaults 0 0

Removing the Swap Logical Volume

Procedure

1\. Remove the line previously created in the /etc/fstab file.

2\. Run the following commands:

# swapoff /dev/vg/lv_swap

# lvremove /dev/vg/lv_swap

Add Swap Space using a New Partition

Procedure

1\. Create a new partition /dev/sdb:

# fdisk /dev/sdb

2\. At the fdisk prompt, type ‘c‘, then ‘u‘ and finally ‘p‘ to print the partition table.

3\. Type ‘n‘ to create a new partition, type the partition number, the first sector, and the partition size.

4\. To give the swap type to this partition press ‘t‘, then the partition number, then ‘82‘.

5\. Type ‘w‘ to write the partition table on the disk.

6\. To make the kernel reread the partition table (assuming that the swap partition is /dev/sdb1):

# partprobe /dev/sdb1

7\. Create the swap space on the swap partition:

# mkswap /dev/sdb1

8\. Activate the swap partition on the system:

# swapon /dev/sdb1

9\. You can use either of these commands to check the result:

# swapon -s

OR

# cat /proc/swaps

10\. Add the following line to the /etc/fstab file:

/dev/sdb1 swap swap defaults 0 0

Removing the Swap Partition

Procedure

1\. Remove the line previously created in the /etc/fstab file.

2\. Run the following commands:

# swapoff /dev/sdb1

# lvremove /dev/sdb1

◆◆◆
