◆◆◆

VII.I Schedule tasks using at and cron

Scheduling Tasks

Automate your Linux system tasks with cron

Cron allows system administrators to schedule and automate tasks with ease and precision.

The crontab file has the following syntax:

minute(0-59) hour(0-23) day_of_month(1-31) month_of_year(1-12) day_of_week(0-7) command_to_run(or path to script)

Note that

● A cron daemon (crond) is a system-managed executable that runs in memory and from which users can schedule tasks. 

● Any user can schedule cron jobs or tasks on the system.

● The task runs under the user account from which it was created.

● In day_of_week both 0 and 7 are Sundays.

Prerequisites for Cron Jobs

Use the following procedure to install the cronie package:

1\. To install the cronie package, run:

# dnf install cronie

2\. To enable and start the crond service, run:

# systemctl enable crond.service --now

3\. To verify that the crond service is started and enabled, run:

# systemctl status crond.service

Listing Configured Cron Tasks

To show your configured cron tasks, run:

$ crontab -l

To show the scheduled tasks of a particular user, use the following syntax as root:

# crontab -u username -l

Creating and Editing Cron Tasks

To create or edit cron tasks, you can use any of the following methods:

First method

By editing your crontab file (it will be opened in a vim editor), using the command:

$ crontab -e

Second method

Using the System-Wide Cron Schedule.

● /etc/cron.d

● /etc/crontab

● /etc/cron.daily

● /etc/cron.hourly

● /etc/cron.monthly

● /etc/cron.weekly

Third method

If you have sudo privileges, you can edit another user’s crontab with the following command:

$ sudo crontab -u username -e

Note that

● The syntax of system-wide crontab files is slightly different than user crontabs. It contains an additional mandatory user field that specifies which user will run the cron job.

* * * * * <username> command(s)

Remove Crontab Entry

Using crontab with the -r option will remove complete scheduled tasks without confirmation:

# crontab -r

You can add the -i option to prompt for confirmation before removing the tasks:

# crontab -i -r

Scheduling a Job to Run at a Specific Time using at

To schedule a one-time task (job), to run once at a specific time, use the at utility. The jobs are then executed by the atd service.

Prerequisites for At Jobs

To start using the at utility, you need to do the following first:

1\. To install the at package, run:

# dnf install at

2\. To enable and start the atd service, run:

# systemctl enable atd.service --now

3\. To verify that the atd service is started and enabled, run:

# systemctl status atd.service

Scheduling an At Job

Procedure to schedule an at job:

1\. Log in as the desired user and run:

# at time

For example, to execute the job at 14:00, run:

# at 14:00

2\. At the displayed at> prompt, enter the command to execute then press Enter:

For example:

at> sh /usr/local/bin/backup.sh

3\. Press Ctrl+D on an empty line to finish specifying the job.

Note that

● If the specified time has passed, the job is executed at the same time the next day.

Viewing Pending Jobs

To view the list of pending jobs, run:

# atq

Deleting a Scheduled Job

Procedure to delete a scheduled job:

1\. List pending jobs:

# atq

2\. Specify the number of the job you want to delete, for example, "25".

3\. Delete the scheduled job by running the command:

# atrm 25

◆◆◆

VII.II Start and stop services and configure services to start automatically at boot

Objective

In this section, you will learn how to enable, disable, start, stop, restart, and show the status of a service.

What is systemd?

Systemd is a system and service manager for Linux operating systems that introduces the concept of systemd units.

Managing Services with systemd

Prerequisites

You must have root access to the system.

Procedure

To enable a service at boot time, use:

# systemctl enable service_name

For example, to enable the httpd service (which should be installed first):

# systemctl enable httpd

To Start a service, use:

# systemctl start service_name

For example, to start the httpd service (which should be installed first):

# systemctl start httpd

To enable and start a service in a single command, use:

# systemctl enable --now service_name

For example, to enable and start the httpd service in a single command (which should be installed first):

# systemctl enable --now httpd

To disable a service, use:

# systemctl disable service_name

For example, to disable the httpd service:

# systemctl disable httpd

To stop a service, use:

# systemctl stop service_name

For example, to stop the httpd service:

# systemctl stop httpd

To restart a service, use:

# systemctl restart service_name

For example, to restart the httpd service:

# systemctl restart httpd

To show the status of a service, use:

# systemctl status service_name

For example, to show the status of the httpd service:

# systemctl status httpd

Note that

● You cannot enable a service that has been previously masked. You have to unmask it first by using the command: # systemctl unmask service_name

Listing Services

To list all systemd units(.service, .target, .device, .mount, ....), use:

# systemctl list-unit-files

To show the status of all services, use:

# systemctl list-units --type service --all

Note that

● You can remove the --all option to display the active units only.

To list all enabled services, use:

# systemctl list-unit-files --state=enabled

To list all disabled services, use:

# systemctl list-unit-files --state=disabled

Masking and UnMasking a Service

You can mask any service unit to prevent it from being started manually or by another service. 

Prerequisites

● You must have root access to the system.

To mask a service, use:

# systemctl mask name.service

To unmask a service, use:

# systemctl unmask name.service

Note that

● Masking a service replaces the /etc/systemd/system/name.service file with a symbolic link to /dev/null.

◆◆◆

VII.III Configure systems to boot into a specific target automatically

Working with systemd Targets

systemd targets are represented by target units, which end with the .target file extension. systemd targets are used to group other systemd units through a chain of dependencies.

To list the currently loaded target units, run:

# systemctl list-units --type target

To change the current target, run:

# systemctl isolate name.target

To view the default target unit, run:

$ systemctl get-default

To list all loaded units regardless of their state:

$ systemctl list-units --type target --all

To list all currently loaded target units:

$ systemctl list-units --type target

Configuring the system to use a different target unit by default

To configure the system to use a different target unit by default, use:

# systemctl set-default unit.target

For example:

To configure a system to boot into multi-user level without a graphical interface, use:

# systemctl set-default multi-user.target

To configure a system to boot into a multi-user level with the graphical interface, use:

# systemctl set-default graphical.target

To check the current configuration, use:

# systemctl get-default

◆◆◆

VII.IV Configure time service clients

Configure chrony as an NTP client or server in Linux

In Red Hat Enterprise Linux (RHEL), the chronyd daemon provided by the chrony package is the default NTP client. And it can be configured as a server to provide accurate time to computer systems in your internal network.

The Network Time Protocol (NTP) protocol is a cross-platform protocol designed to synchronize the clocks of computers over a network. It commonly synchronizes a computer to Internet time servers or other sources. Also, it can be used as a time source/server for client systems.

Chrony works both as an NTP server and as an NTP client, which is used to synchronize the system clock with NTP servers and can be used to synchronize the system clock with a reference clock (e.g a GPS receiver). It is also used to synchronize the system clock with a manual time input, and as an NTPv4 server or peer to provide a time service to other computers in the network.

Install and configure the NTP server and client using the chrony package

Test environment:

NTP Server - RHEL: 192.168.69.10

NTP Client - RHEL: 192.168.69.15

Install Chrony in RHEL

1\. To install the chrony suite, use:

# dnf install chrony

Note that

● The chrony suite consists of chronyd, and chronyc

● chronyc is a command line interface program that can be used to monitor the performance of chronyd and to change various operating parameters while it is running.

2\. To start the chronyd service and enable it to auto start at system boot, use:

# systemctl start chronyd

# systemctl enable chronyd

OR

# systemctl enable chronyd --now

3\. To verify the running status of the chronyd service, use:

# systemctl status chronyd

Configure the NTP Server Using Chrony

Procedure to set up your RHEL NTP server as a master NTP time server:

1\. Open the /etc/chrony.conf configuration file using a text-based editor:

# vim /etc/chrony.conf

2\. Then look for the allow configuration directive and uncomment it and set its value to the network or subnet address from which the clients are allowed to connect:

allow 192.168.69.0/24

3\. Save and quit, by typing ":wq" in vim command mode followed by Enter.

4\. Restart the chronyd service to apply the recent changes:

# systemctl restart chronyd

5\. Open access to the NTP service in firewalld configuration to allow incoming NTP requests from clients:

# firewall-cmd --permanent --add-service=ntp

# firewall-cmd --reload

Configure the NTP Client Using Chrony

Procedure to set configure chrony as a direct NTP client in your RHEL server:

1\. To install the chrony package, use:

# dnf install chrony

2\. To start the chronyd service and enable it to auto start at system boot, use:

# systemctl start chronyd

# systemctl enable chronyd

OR

# systemctl enable chronyd --now

3\. To verify the running status of the chronyd service, use:

# systemctl status chronyd

4\. To configure your system as a direct client of the NTP server. Open the /etc/chrony.conf configuration file with a text-based editor:

# vim /etc/chrony.conf

5\. Comment out the default NTP servers.

6\. Specify the NTP servers your client-server should ask for the current time. You can specify the servers using the server or pool directive:

server 192.168.69.10

Note that

● You can add the iburst option that allows chronyd to make the first update of the clock sooner. For example, server 192.168.69.10 iburst

● The server directive allows you to specify a single NTP server as a time source.

● The pool directive allows you to specify a pool of NTP servers.

7\. Save and quit, by typing ":wq" in vim command mode followed by Enter.

8\. Restart the chronyd service for the recent changes to take effect:

# systemctl restart chronyd

9\. Run the following command to show the current time sources:

# chronyc sources

10\. You can run the following command on the server to display information about NTP clients accessing the NTP server:

# chronyc clients

Note that

● To set the hardware clock, use: # hwclock

● To set the system clock, use: # timedatectl  OR  # date

● To select the current timezone, use: # tzselect

◆◆◆

VII.V Install and update software packages from Red Hat Network, a remote repository, or from the local file system

Work with Package Module Streams

Software management tools in RHEL

The software management tools in RHEL are DNF and YUM.

What is YUM?

YUM stands for YellowDog Updater Modified. It is a package manager used for installing, updating, or removing software packages in the Red Hat Enterprise Linux system. While installing, updating, or removing software packages YUM performs dependency resolution.

What is DNF?

DNF stands for Dandified YUM and is an improved version of the YUM package manager. It offers more features while installing, updating, or removing software packages in a RedHat-based Linux system.

Note that

● The new version of YUM Tool is YUM v4. It is based on DNF technology.

Uses of YUM & DNF

1\. Searching for software packages with YUM or DNF

To search for a package, use:

# yum search term

OR

# dnf search term

Replace the term with a term related to the package.

2\. Listing installed and available packages with YUM or DNF

To list information on all installed and available packages, use:

# yum list --all

OR

# dnf list --all

To list all packages installed on your system, use:

# yum list --installed

OR

# dnf list --installed

To list all packages in all enabled repositories that are available to install, use:

# yum list --available

OR

# dnf list --available

3\. Listing enabled and disabled repositories with YUM or DNF

To list all enabled repositories on your system, use:

# yum repolist

OR

# dnf repolist

To list all disabled repositories on your system, use:

# yum repolist --disabled

OR

# dnf repolist --disabled

To list both enabled and disabled repositories, use:

# yum repolist --all

OR

# dnf repolist --all

To list additional information about the repositories, use:

# yum repoinfo

OR

# dnf repoinfo

Note that

● You can filter the results by passing the ID or name of repositories as arguments or by appending global expressions. 

4\. Displaying package information with YUM or DNF

To display information about one or more packages, use:

# yum info package-name

Or

# dnf info package-name

Replace the package-name with the name of the package.

Note that

● You can filter the results by appending global expressions as arguments. See Specifying global expressions in yum input for more details.

5\. Listing installed package groups with YUM or DNF

A package is a group of packages that serve a common purpose (system tools, audio, and video). Installing a package group pulls up a group of dependent packages, which greatly saves time.

Procedure

To view the number of installed and available groups, use:

# yum group summary

OR

# dnf group summary

To list all installed and available groups, use:

# yum group list

OR

# dnf group list

Note that

● You can filter the results by appending command line options for the yum group list command (--hidden, --available).

6\. Installing software packages with YUM or DNF

To install a package and all the package dependencies, use:

# yum install package-name

OR

# dnf install package-name

Replace the package-name with the name of the package.

To install multiple packages and their dependencies simultaneously, use:

# yum install package-name-1 package-name-2

OR

# dnf install package-name-1 package-name-2

Replace package-name-1 and package-name-2 with the names of the packages.

To install a previously-downloaded package from a local directory, use:

# yum install /path/

OR

# dnf install /path/

Replace /path/ with the path to the package.

7\. Installing a package group with YUM or DNF

To install a package group by a group name, use:

# yum group install group-name Or # dnf group install group-name 

OR

# yum install @group-name Or # dnf install @group-name

Replace group-name with the full name of the group or environmental group.

To install a package group by the groupID, use:

# yum group install groupID

OR

# dnf group install groupID

Replace groupID with the ID of the group.

8\. Updating software packages with YUM or DNF

To see which packages installed on your system have available updates, use:

# yum check-update

OR

# dnf check-update

The output returns the list of packages and their dependencies that have an update available.

8.1 Updating a single package with YUM or DNF

To update a package, use:

# yum update package-name

OR

# dnf update package-name

Replace package-name with the name of the package.

Important

● When applying updates to the kernel, yum always installs a new kernel regardless of whether you are using the yum update or yum install command.

8.2 Updating a package group with YUM or DNF

To update a package group, use:

# yum group update group-name

OR

# dnf group update group-name

Replace group-name with the name of the package group.

8.3 Updating all packages and their dependencies with YUM or DNF

To update all packages and their dependencies, use:

# yum update

OR

# dnf update

8.4 Updating security-related packages with YUM or DNF

To upgrade to the latest available packages that have security errata, use:

# yum update --security

OR

# dnf update --security

To upgrade to the last security errata packages, use:

# yum update-minimal --security

OR

# dnf update-minimal --security

9\. Uninstalling software packages with YUM or DNF

9.1 Removing packages

To remove a particular package and all dependent packages, use:

# yum remove package-name

OR

# dnf remove package-name

Replace package-name with the name of the package.

To remove multiple packages and their dependencies simultaneously, use:

# yum remove package-name-1 package-name-2

OR

# dnf remove package-name-1 package-name-2

Replace package-name-1 and package-name-2 with the names of the packages.

Note that

● yum is not able to remove a package without removing depending packages.

9.2 Removing a package group with YUM or DNF

To remove a package group by the group name, use:

# yum group remove group-name OR # dnf group remove group-name 

OR

# yum remove @group-name OR # dnf remove @group-name

Replace group-name with the full name of the group.

To remove a package group by the groupID, use:

# yum group remove groupID

OR

# dnf group remove groupID

Replace groupID with the ID of the group.

Managing software repositories

The configuration information for yum and related utilities are stored in the /etc/yum.conf file. This file contains one or more [repository] sections, which allow you to set repository-specific options.

Note that

● It is recommended to define individual repositories in new or existing .repo files in the /etc/yum.repos.d/ directory.

● The values you define in individual [repository] sections of the /etc/yum.conf file override values set in the [main] section.

Setting YUM repository options

The /etc/yum.conf configuration file contains the [repository] sections, where the repository is a unique repository ID. The [repository] section allows you to define individual yum repositories.

Adding a YUM repository

To define a new repository, you can:

● Add a [repository] section to the /etc/yum.conf file.

● Add a [repository] section to a .repo file in the /etc/yum.repos.d/ directory.

To add a repository to your system and enable it, use the following command:

# yum-config-manager --add-repo repository_URL

Replace repository_url with the URL pointing to the repository.

Warning

● Obtaining and installing software packages from unverified or untrusted sources other than Red Hat certificate-based Content Delivery Network (CDN) constitutes a potential security risk, and could lead to security, stability, compatibility, and maintainability issues.

Enabling a YUM repository

Once you added a YUM repository to your system, enable it to ensure installation and updates.

To enable a repository, use the following command:

# yum-config-manager --enable repositoryID

Replace repositoryID with the unique repository ID.

Disabling a YUM repository

Disable a specific YUM repository to prevent particular packages from installation or updated.

To disable a yum repository, use the following command:

# yum-config-manager --disable repositoryID

Replace repositoryID with the unique repository ID.

RPM (Redhat Package Manager)

To install a package:

# rpm -i Package_name

Note that

● Using RPM is not recommended, and you should use DNF instead, as RPM doesn’t consider dependencies.

● RPM is helpful to perform queries.

Performing Queries using RPM

To get the package where the file is coming from, use:

# rpm -qf filename

To show package files, use:

# rpm -ql packagename

To show package configuration files, use:

# rpm -qc packagename

To show packages in a file that is not installed, use:

# rpm -qp --scripts package-file.rpm

Setting up a Local Repository

This local repository will make you able to install packages from the RHEL installation disk ISO image.

Procedure

Creating an ISO image from RHEL DVD

● To create an ISO image from DVD, run: 

# dd if=/dev/sr0 of=/rhel.iso bs=1M 

Here, the input file is the optical device, the output file will be the ISO image file, and Block Size will be 1 Mega.

Creating a local repo using the RHEL ISO image

Procedure

1\. Create a directory /repo: 

# mkdir /repo  

2\. Edit the fstab file to add a mount point :  

/rhel.iso /repo iso9660 defaults 0 0  

3\. Reload the changed unit files: 

# systemctl daemon-reload  

4\. Mount the iso imager,: 

# mount -a  

5\. Copy the media.repo file to the /etc/yum.repos.d/ directory with a new name "rhel.repo":

# cp /opt/media.repo /etc/yum.repos.d/rhel.repo  

6\. Change the rhel.repo file permissions to 644:

# chmod 644 /etc/yum.repos.d/rhel.repo   

7\. Edit the file in your favorite text editor:

# vim etc/yum.repos.d/rhel.repo  

8\. Replace the data in the file with the following data:

[InstallMedia-BaseOS]

name=RHEL - BaseOS

metadata_expire=-1

gpgcheck=0

enabled=1

baseurl=file:///opt/BaseOS/

[InstallMedia-AppStream]

name=RHEL - AppStream

metadata_expire=-1

gpgcheck=0

enabled=1

baseurl=file:///opt/AppStream/

9\. Type ":wq" in vim command mode followed by Enter to save and quit.   

10\. To clean dnf and the subscription-manager cache, use:

# dnf clean all  

# subscription-manager clean   

11\. To list available repositories for the system, use:

# dnf repolist

◆◆◆

VII.VI Work with package module streams

RHEL Application Streams

RHEL 8 came with a new feature called Application Streams (AppStreams), where multiple versions of packages are provided, with a known support period. These modules can be thought of as packages that represent an application, a set of tools, or a runtime language.

Each of these modules can have different streams, which represent different versions of the software, giving the user the option to use whichever version suits their needs. Each module will also contain installation profiles, which help define a specific use case, and will identify which packages are installed on the system.

Components made available as Application Streams can be packaged as:

● RPM packages

● Modules

● Software Collections

Note that

● Application Streams are delivered through the AppStream repository.

● Each Application Stream component has a given life cycle, either the same as the RHEL release or shorter.

● Application Streams are available for RHEL 8 and 9.

● New versions of most Application Streams will become available as needed during the Red Hat Enterprise Linux 9 life cycle. 

● Lifecycles will align to the upstream support life or product needs as required.

● Multiple versions of the same Application Stream may be available at the same time with overlapping support durations.

Rolling Streams

● Rolling Application Streams include tools and applications that move very fast and are updated frequently.

● Rolling Application Streams are fully supported for the full life of the RHEL major release.

Dependent Application Streams

● Only dependent application Streams are supported and used by depending Application Streams and are not independent.

Releases and Support

● Red Hat will support Application Streams for the specified lifecycle, after which customers are encouraged to either upgrade to a later release or continue to self-support without official Red Hat support.

Guide to Managing Package Module Streams in RHEL

Modularity and AppStreams

Modularity and AppStreams provide users with access to newer versions of software while being supported for a longer period.

Note that

● Modularity allows a single repository to host multiple versions of an application’s package and its dependencies.

● RHEL 8 & 9 content is distributed through two main software repositories, BaseOS and AppStream.

BaseOS

● The BaseOS repository provides the core operating system content for RHEL as RPM packages.

● The BaseOS components have a similar lifecycle to that of previous RHEL versions.

AppStream

● The Application Stream repository provides content with different life cycles as traditional packages and modules.

● Application Stream contains the necessary parts of the system, as well as a wide variety of applications that were previously available as part of Red Hat's suite of software and other products.

● The Application Stream repository contains two types of content, Modules, and traditional RPM packages.

Module

● A module describes a set of RPM packages that belong together.

● Modules can contain several streams to make multiple versions of applications available for installation.

● Enabling a module stream gives the system access to the RPM packages within that module stream.

Module Streams

● Each module can have one or more module streams, which hold different versions of the content.

● Each of the streams receives updates independently. Think of the module stream as a virtual repository in the Application Stream's physical repository. 

● For each module, only one of its streams can be enabled and provide its packages.

Module Proﬁles

● Each module can have one or more proﬁles.

● A proﬁle is a list of certain packages to be installed together for a particular use case such as for a server, client, development, minimal install, or other. 

● Installing a particular module proﬁle simply installs a particular set of packages from the module stream. Note that, you can subsequently install or uninstall packages normally. 

● If you do not specify a proﬁle, the module will install its default proﬁle.

Managing Modules Using YUM or DNF

● To handle modular content, yum and dnf modules commands have been added. Other than that, yum and dnf works with modules as with regular packages.

Listing Modules

To display a list of available modules, use the yum module list:

$ yum module list  

OR

$ dnf module list  

Let's take an example to show all the options:

# yum install @module: version/profile

● @module - defines which module we are going to use.

● :version - specifies which stream we are going to use for the specified module.

● /profile - tells us which profile to use. If no profile is specified, the default one is used. If no default is defined, then the module is only enabled but no packages are installed.

Note that

● You can use the hint at the end of the output to help determine which streams and proﬁles are enabled, disabled, installed, and which are the default settings.

To list the module streams for the postgresql module and retrieve their status:

$ yum module list postgresql  

To list the packages installed by the default module stream:

$ yum module info postgresql  

To display information about packages installed by each of the module's proﬁles, you can use the --profile option. For example:

$ yum module info --profile perl:5.24

Enabling Module Streams and Installing Modules

Module streams must be enabled to install their module. To simplify this process, when a module is installed it enables its module stream if necessary. Module streams can be enabled manually using yum module enable and providing the name of the module stream.

Note that,  only one module stream may be enabled for a given module. Enabling an additional module stream will disable the original module stream.

To install the postgresql module, using the default stream and proﬁles:

$ sudo yum module install -y postgresql  

Note that

● The same results could have been accomplished by running: $ yum install @postgresql

● This command will install the default, PostgreSQL 10 module.

● The @ notation informs yum that the argument is a module name instead of a package name.

To verify the status of the module stream and the installed proﬁle:

$ yum module list perl 

Removing Modules and Disabling Module Streams

Removing a module removes all of the packages installed by proﬁles of the currently enabled module stream and any further packages and modules that depend on these. 

Packages installed from this module stream not listed in any of its proﬁles remain installed on the system and can be removed manually.

Note that

● Switching the stream enabled for a module is equivalent to resetting the current stream and enabling the new stream. It does not automatically change any installed packages. You have to do that manually. 

● Directly installing a module stream that is different than the one that is currently installed is not recommended, because upgrade scripts might run during the installation that would break things with the original module stream. That could lead to data loss or other conﬁguration issues. 

To remove an installed module, for example (postgresql), use:

$ sudo yum module remove -y postgresql

After the module is removed, the module stream is still enabled. To verify the module stream is still enabled, use:

$ yum module list postgresql 

To disable a module, for example (postgresql), use:

$ sudo yum module disable postgresql

Switching Module Streams

Switching module streams generally requires upgrading or downgrading the content to a different version.

To ensure a clean switch, you should remove the modules provided by the module stream ﬁrst. That will remove all the packages installed by the proﬁles of the module, and any modules and packages that those packages have dependencies on.

To enable a different module stream and install the module, for example ( perl:5.24):

$ sudo yum module install perl:5.24

The new module stream will be enabled and the current stream disabled. 

◆◆◆

VII.VII Modify the system bootloader

What is a bootloader?

The boot loader is a small program that loads the Linux kernel in memory and executes it.

Why use a bootloader?

It is needed to run the startup process, initialize the hardware, and pass control to the kernel, which initializes the operating system.

What is GRUB2?

GRUB2 is the latest version of GNU GRUB, the GRand Unified Bootloader. Starting RHEL7 GRUB2 is the bootloader. The GRUB2 configuration file is /boot/grub2/grub.cfg.

The GRUB2 Configuration File /boot/grub2/grub.cfg

The GRUBv2 configuration is read on boot from /boot/grub2/grub.cfg. This file is not to be edited by hand but is modified based on the contents of the /etc/default/grub file and the files found inside the /etc/grub.d directory.

You can use the grub2-mkconfig command as root to generate grub.cfg file. This command uses the template scripts in the /etc/grub.d directory and menu-configuration settings taken from the /etc/default/grub file when generating the grub.cfg file.

Note that

● The /etc/grub2.cfg file is a symbolic link to the /boot/grub2/grub.cfg file.

The /etc/default/grub File

GRUB2 menu-configuration settings are taken from /etc/default/grub when generating the grub.cfg file.

To show the contents of the /etc/default/grub file, use:

# cat /etc/default/grub

Example of the grub file output:

GRUB_TIMEOUT=5

GRUB_DISTRIBUTOR="$(sed 's, release .*$,,g' /etc/system-release)"

GRUB_DEFAULT=saved

GRUB_DISABLE_SUBMENU=true

GRUB_TERMINAL_OUTPUT="console"

GRUB_CMDLINE_LINUX="resume=/dev/mapper/rhel-swap rd.lvm.lv=rhel/root rd.lvm.lv=rhel/swap rhgb quiet"

GRUB_DISABLE_RECOVERY="true"

GRUB_ENABLE_BLSCFG=true

Note that

● If you change any of these parameters, you must run the grub2-mkconfig command as root to re-generate the /boot/grub2/grub.cfg file:

# grub2-mkconfig -o /boot/grub2/grub.cfg

OR

# grub2-mkconfig -o /etc/grub2.cfg

Values are described as follows:

● GRUB_TIMEOUT: The time in seconds after the menu is displayed to boot the default entry unless a key is pressed. The default is 5\. Set to 0 to boot immediately without displaying the menu, or to -1 to wait until the user makes a selection.

● GRUB_DISTRIBUTOR: Set by distributors of GRUB and is used to generate more informative menu entry titles. The example evaluates to CentOS Linux Server.

● GRUB_DEFAULT: The default menu entry to boot. Requires an integer value that indicates which OS or kernel entry in the GRUB initial screen should be selected to boot by default. A value of 0 boots the first menuentry. A value of 1 boots the second menuentry. A value of saved instructs GRUB2 to load the last successfully loaded operating system. 

● GRUB_DISABLE_SUBMENU: By default, the grub2-mkconfig command generates a top-level menu entry for the kernel with the highest version number and puts all other found kernels or alternative menu entries for recovery mode in a submenu. Setting GRUB_DISABLE_SUBMENU=true disables this.

● GRUB_TERMINAL_OUTPUT: The terminal output device. When specifying multiple devices, separate the valid terminal output names with spaces.

● GRUB_CMDLINE_LINUX: Kernel boot parameters. More information on kernel boot parameters is provided in the next slide.

● GRUB_DISABLE_RECOVERY: By default, two menu entries are generated for each Linux kernel: one default entry and one for recovery mode. Setting GRUB_DISABLE_RECOVERY=“true” disables this.

Updating Grub2 bootloader on an RHEL 

Procedure

1\. Open the /etc/default/grub in your favorite text editor:

$ sudo vim /etc/default/grub

2\. Switch to editing mode, then modify the file as required.

3\. Save and quit.

4\. Run the following command as root:

$ sudo grub2-mkconfig -o /boot/grub2/grub.cfg

5\. Reboot the system:

$ sudo reboot

The grubby command  

grubby is a command line tool for updating and displaying information about the configuration files for the grub2 boot loader. It is primarily designed to be used from scripts that install new kernels and need to find information about the current boot environment. Also, you can use grubby for changing the default boot entry, and for adding/removing arguments from a GRUB menu entry.

Let's take some examples of how to use grubby:

To list the default Kernel, use:

$ sudo grubby --default-kernel

To show the index number of the default kernel, use:

$ sudo grubby --default-index

To show the default kernel title, use:

$ sudo grubby --default-title

To show the list of all installed Kernels, use:

$ sudo grubby --info=ALL | grep ^kernel

To show all installed kernels’ indexes, use:

$ sudo grubby --info=ALL | grep -E "^kernel|^index"

To persistently change the default kernel, use:

$ sudo grubby --set-default /boot/new_kernel

Reboot the system to apply the changes:

$ sudo reboot

To persistently change the default kernel, using the kernel index, use:

$ sudo grubby --set-default-index=kernel_index

Reboot the system to apply the changes:

$ sudo reboot

To remove a certain kernel entry, use:

$ sudo grubby --remove-kernel=kernel_name

OR

$ sudo grubby --remove-kernel=kernel_index

Important

● Removing an incorrect kernel entry may cause the system to fail to boot.

Changing kernel command-line parameters for all boot entries

To add a parameter to kernel command-line parameters for all boot entries, use:

#  grubby --update-kernel=ALL --args="<NEW_PARAMETER>"

To apply the configured kernel options to the new kernels, use:

# grub2-mkconfig -o /etc/grub2.cfg

To remove a parameter from kernel command-line parameters for all boot entries, use:

#  grubby --update-kernel=ALL --remove-args="<PARAMETER_TO_REMOVE>"

To apply the configured kernel options to the new kernels, use:

# grub2-mkconfig -o /etc/grub2.cfg

To add a parameter to kernel command-line parameters for a single boot entry, use:

#  grubby --update-kernel=/boot/vmlinuz-$(uname -r) --args="<NEW_PARAMETER>"

To apply the configured kernel options to the new kernels, use:

# grub2-mkconfig -o /etc/grub2.cfg

To remove a parameter from kernel command-line parameters for a single boot entry, use:

#  grubby --update-kernel=/boot/vmlinuz-$(uname -r) --remove-args="<PARAMETER_TO_REMOVE>"

To apply the configured kernel options to the new kernels, use:

# grub2-mkconfig -o /etc/grub2.cfg

Changing kernel command-line parameters temporarily at boot time (only during a single boot process)

Procedure

1\. Select the kernel you want to start when the GRUB2 boot menu appears and press the E key to edit the kernel parameters.  

2\. Find the kernel command line that starts with linux.  

3\. Move the cursor to the end of the line.  

4\. Edit the kernel parameters as required.   

5\. For example, to enable the system messages, remove the rhgb quiet parameters.  

6\. Press Ctrl+X to boot with the selected kernel and the modified command line parameters.

Note that, you can press:

● Ctrl+A to jump to the start of the kernel command line.

● Ctrl+E to jump to the end of the kernel command line.

Important

● You can press the Esc key to leave the command line editing and it will drop all the user-made changes.

Updating the kernel

Procedure

1\. To update the kernel, use:

# yum update kernel

OR

# dnf update kernel

2\. Reboot the system for the changes to take effect:

# reboot

Installing the kernel

Procedure

1\. To install a specific kernel version, use:

# yum install kernel-{version}

OR

# dnf install kernel-{version}

2\. Reboot the system for the changes to take effect:

# reboot

Note that

● To list installed kernel packages, you can use:

# dnf list installed "kernel-*"

◆◆◆

Chapter VIII: Manage basic networking

Chapter VIII Contents

● VIII.I Configure IPv4 and IPv6 addresses

● VIII.II Configure hostname resolution

● VIII.III Configure network services to start automatically at boot

● VIII.IV Restrict network access using firewall-cmd/firewall

◆◆◆

VIII.I Configure IPv4 and IPv6 addresses

How to change interface settings?

Previously, NetworkManager stored new network configurations to /etc/sysconfig/network-scripts/ in the ifcfg format. Starting with RHEL 9.0, RHEL stores new network configurations at /etc/NetworkManager/system-connections/ in a key file format. The connections for which the configurations are stored to /etc/sysconfig/network-scripts/ in the old format still work uninterrupted. Modifications in existing profiles continue updating the older files. 

Previously, NetworkManager stored new network configurations to /etc/sysconfig/network-scripts/ in ifcfg format. Starting with RHEL 9.0, RHEL stores new network configurations in /etc/NetworkManager/system-connections/ in a key file format. Connections for which configurations are stored in /etc/sysconfig/network-scripts/ still work without interruption. 

Note that

● While the  ifcfg- and key file formats do not have the same syntax, they mostly represent similar data about the interface configuration.

● Modifications to existing profiles continue to update older files.

● You can keep using  ifcfg files in  /etc/sysconfig/network-scripts, but you need to be aware that, NetworkManager will prioritize key files.

Changing interface settings

Whether you decide to continue using ifcfg-rh files (although this is not recommended) or use key files, you can change interface settings (such as IP addresses) in one of the following two ways with administrator privileges:

1\. Tool based

You can use tools like nm-connection-editor (GUI), nmcli (CLI), nmtui (GUI), gnome-control-center (GUI), cockpit (GUI), and nmstate (CLI).

Note that

● nmstate is not installed as a part of the default installation. To install nmstate package, you can use the following command: $ sudo dnf install nmstate -y

2\. File based

You can also use a text editor to edit the key file in /etc/NetworkManager/system-connections or the ifcfg-rh file in /etc/sysconfig/network-scripts, and then notify NetworkManager of the changes.

Important

● It is highly recommended to set a static IP address on your Linux system, especially on servers. The main benefit of a static IP address is that it persists across reboots.

Configure IP Networking with nmcli Command

With the nmcli (network manager command-line interface) utility, you can show, create, edit, enable, and disable network interfaces or connections. It is especially useful for servers and systems without a GUI (graphical user interface).

The nmcli syntax

$ sudo nmcli [OPTIONS] OBJECT {COMMAND | help }

The OBJECT can be:

● Device or network interface.

● NetworkManager’s connection.

● NetworkManager’s stats.

Showing Interfaces, Connections, and Devices using the nmcli utility

To display detailed information about all the network interfaces, use:

$ nmcli

To get a summary of the network interfaces, use:

$ nmcli device status 

OR

$ nmcli dev status

To list all the active connections and interfaces on your system, use:

$ nmcli connection show

OR

$ nmcli con show

OR

$ nmcli connection show --active

Note that

● You can specify any field (DEVICE, TYPE, CONNECTION, CONN-UUID, STATE, IP4-CONNECTIVITY, and IP6-CONNECTIVITY) you want to be shown on the terminal. For example, to display the CONNECTION and STATE, use: $ nmcli -f CONNECTION, STATE device

● You can use the -p (pretty) option, to show the output in a human-readable format. For example:

$ nmcli -p device

Configuring a static IP using the nmcli utility

Assuming we have a static IP address with the following values:

● IP address: 192.168.69.10/24

● Default gateway: 192.168.69.1

● Preferred DNS: 8.8.8.8, 8.8.4.4

● The current IP address: 192.168.68.15

Before you assign the static IP, check the current IP address of your system using:

$ ip addr show

To set the static IP address with the connection name or profile called static-ip, IPv4 address 192.168.69.10/24, and default gateway 192.168.69.1 on the network interface enp0s2, use:

$ sudo nmcli con add type ethernet con-name "static-ip" ifname enp0s2 ipv4.addresses 192.168.69.10/24 gw4 192.168.69.1

To disable DHCP ip and configure static ip, use:

$ sudo nmcli con add type ethernet con-name "static-ip" ifname enp0s2 ipv4.method manual ipv4.addresses 192.168.69.10/24 gw4 192.168.69.1

To configure the DNS server, use:

$ sudo nmcli con mod static-ip ipv4.dns "8.8.8.8 8.8.4.4"

To activate the connection, use:

$ sudo nmcli con up static-ip ifname enp0s2

To confirm that the IP address was successfully added, use:

$ ip addr sh

OR

$ nmcli con sh static-ip

OR

$ nmcli dev sh enp0s2

To disable or deactivate a connection, use:

$ sudo nmcli con down id "static-ip" ifname enp0s2

OR

$ sudo nmcli con down id "static-ip"

To enable a connection, use:

$ sudo nmcli con up id "static-ip" ifname enp0s2

OR

$ sudo nmcli con up id "static-ip"

How to Configure IPv6 Network on RHEL?

IPv6 was designed to overcome the limitations of IPv4\. It aims to solve IPv4 address exhaustion by utilizing a much wider network address space. An IPv6 address is a 128-bit alphanumeric value that identifies an endpoint device in an IPv6 network consisting of 8 colons separated groups of 4 hex digits each.

An example of an IPv6 address is: 

2001:0db8:85a3:0000:0000:8a2e:0370:7334

Note that

● IPv6 is usually enabled by default on RHEL 8 & 9.

To check if IPv6 is enabled on your system, you can use either of these methods:

First method

$ sudo sysctl -a | grep ipv6.*disable

OR

$ cat /sys/module/ipv6/parameters/disable

The value 0 indicates that IPv6 is active on your node. A value of 1 shows that IPv6 is disabled. 

Second method

On RHEL 8

By viewing your network interface at the /etc/network-scripts/ directory. For example to view the network configuration of the interface enps02:

$ cat /etc/sysconfig/network-scripts/ifcfg-enps02

Then look for the following IPv6 options:

IPV6INIT=yes - Enable IPv6 on this interface.         

IPV6_AUTOCONF=yes - This enables the IPv6 auto-configuration for the interface. 

IPV6_DEFROUTE=yes - This interface is the default route for IPv6 traffic. 

IPV6_FAILURE_FATAL=no - This interface is not disabled if configuration fails.

On RHEL 9

By viewing your network interface at the /etc/NetworkManager/system-connections/ directory. For example to view the network configuration of the interface enps02:

$ cat /etc/NetworkManager/system-connections/enps02.nmconnection

Then look for the following IPv6 options:

[ipv6]

method=auto OR manual

Temporarily Enable or Disable IPv6 Address

To temporarily enable IPv6, use:

$ sudo sysctl -w net.ipv6.conf.all.disable_ipv6=0

Restart the NetworkManager to apply the changes:

$ sudo systemctl restart NetworkManager

To verify:

$ ip -6 a

To temporarily disable IPv6, use:

$ sudo sysctl -w net.ipv6.conf.all.disable_ipv6=1

Restart the NetworkManager to apply the changes:

$ sudo systemctl restart NetworkManager

To verify:

$ ip -6 a

Permanently Enable or Disable IPv6 Address

To permanently disable IPv6, you can use any of the following methods:

First method

1\. Open the GRUB /etc/default/grub file in the editing mode:

$ sudo vim /etc/default/grub

2\. In the line, GRUB_CMDLINE_LINUX, append the argument ipv6.disable=1 at the end of the line. 

3\. Reboot the system to apply the changes:

$ sudo reboot

Second method

Using the nmtui tool:

1\. To install NetworkManager-tui:

# yum install NetworkManager-tui

2\. To start the nmtui tool:

$ nmtui

3\. To navigate, use the arrow keys or press Tab to step forwards and press Shift+Tab to step back through the options. Press Enter to select an option. The Space bar toggles the status of a check box.

Note that

● To apply changes after a modified connection that is already active requires a reactivation of the connection.

Third method

Using the nmcli tool:

Disabling IPv6 on a connection using nmcli

Procedure

1\. To display the list of network connections, use:

# nmcli connection show

2\. To set the ipv6.method parameter of the connection "test" to disabled, use:

# nmcli connection modify test ipv6.method "disabled"

3\. To restart the network connection, use:

# nmcli connection up test

4\. To verify:

# ip address show device

OR

# cat /proc/sys/net/ipv6/conf/test/disable_ipv6

Here, the output should be 1, which means that IPv6 is disabled for the device.

Enabling IPv6 on a connection using nmcli

Procedure

1\. To display the list of network connections, use:

# nmcli connection show

2\. To set the ipv6.method parameter of the connection "test" to enabled, use:

# nmcli connection modify test ipv6.method "enabled"

3\. To restart the network connection, use:

# nmcli connection up test

4\. To verify:

# ip address show device

OR

# cat /proc/sys/net/ipv6/conf/test/disable_ipv6

Here, the output should be 0, which means that IPv6 is enabled for the device.

The ip Command

The ip tool can be used to manage all aspects of IP networking on runtime. It is the replacement of the ifconfig tool.

To get the depth information on your network interfaces, use:

# ip addr show

To assign a temporary IP Address to a specific interface "eth3", use:

# ip addr add 192.168.50.5 dev eth3

To remove an assigned IP address from a specific interface "eth3", use:

# ip addr del 192.168.1.15/24 dev eth3

To activate the eth2 network interface, use:

# ip link set eth2 up

To deactivate the eth2 network interface, use:

# ip link set eth2 down

To check the routing table information on your system, use:

# ip route show

To add a static route on the eth1 network interface, use:

# ip route add 192.168.69.0/24 via 192.168.5.1 dev eth1

To remove the assigned static route, use:

# ip route del 192.168.69.0/24

To add a temporary default gateway, use:

# ip route add default via 192.168.5.1

Note that

● We use a static route when the traffic must not pass through the default gateway.

● DNS configuration is in the /etc/resolv.conf file.

◆◆◆

VIII.II Configure hostname resolution

What is a hostname?

The hostname is the basic information needed by the system administrator to track the system. It is especially important when working on a remotely managed system.

Viewing Your Hostname

To view your current hostname, use:

$ hostname

OR

$ hostnamectl

Setting a Hostname using hostnamectl

To set a new hostname, use:

$ sudo hostnamectl set-hostname new-hostname

To verify:

$ hostname

OR

$ hostnamectl

Setting a Hostname using nmtui

Procedure

1\. Start the nmtui tool:

$ sudo nmtui

2\. Select:

Set system hostname

3\. Enter the new system hostname.

4\. Restart the systemd-hostnamed service to apply the changes: $ sudo systemctl restart systemd-hostnamed

Setting a Hostname Remotely

To set a hostname on a remote system, use:

# hostnamectl set-hostname -H [username]@hostname

Note that

● The hostnamectl  tool uses SSH to connect to the remote system.

● The -H option is used to execute the operation remotely by specifying a hostname, or a username and a hostname separated by an "@" sign, to connect to it.

◆◆◆

VIII.III Configure network services to start automatically at boot

How to Configure Network Services to Auto Start on Boot?

Configuring essential network services to automatically start on boot (by enabling the services) saves you the hassle of starting them manually after a reboot. 

Note that

● Important network services include SSH, NTP, chronyd, and httpd.

Enabling (to start automatically on boot) and Disabling Services

To configure a service to start automatically on boot, use:

# systemctl enable [service]

To verify:

# systemctl status [service]

For example, to enable the httpd service, use:

# systemctl enable httpd

To verify:

# systemctl status httpd

To view all the enabled services, use:

$ sudo systemctl list-unit-files --state=enabled

To prevent a service from starting at boot (disable), use:

# systemctl disable [service] 

To verify:

# systemctl status [service]

For example, to disable the httpd service, use:

# systemctl disable httpd 

To verify:

# systemctl status httpd 

To view all the disabled services, use:

$ sudo systemctl list-unit-files --state=disabled

◆◆◆

VIII.IV Restrict network access using firewall-cmd/firewall

How to Restrict Network Access Using FirewallD?

As a system administrator, you can control allowing or restricting network access to some services or IP addresses using the firewalld service. 

Note that

● The firewalld service uses the firewall-cmd utility to configure the firewall.

Enable and Start the firewalld Service

To enable the firewalld service, use:

$ sudo systemctl enable firewalld

To start the firewalld service, use:

$ sudo systemctl start firewalld

OR you can enable and start the firewalld service in one line, using:

$ sudo systemctl enable firewalld --now

To verify the status of the firewalld service, use:

$ sudo systemctl status firewalld

Configuring Rules using Firewalld

Adding a TCP/UDP Port

To add the TCP port (22), use:

$ sudo firewall-cmd --add-port=22/tcp --permanent

To add the UDP port (23), use:

$ sudo firewall-cmd --add-port=23/udp --permanent

To verify:

$ sudo firewall-cmd --list-all

Note that

● You have to specify the protocol type (TCP or UDP) after the port number.

● We use the --permanent flag to ensure that the rules persist after a reboot.

Blocking a TCP/UDP Port

To block the TCP port (22), use:

$ sudo firewall-cmd --remove-port=22/tcp --permanent

To block the UDP port (23), use:

$ sudo firewall-cmd --remove-port=23/udp --permanent

To verify:

$ sudo firewall-cmd --list-all

Allowing a Service

To allow a service, for example, ssh, use:

$ sudo firewall-cmd --add-service=ssh

To verify:

$ sudo firewall-cmd --list-all

Blocking a Service

To block a service, for example, http, use:

$ sudo firewall-cmd --remove-service=http

To verify:

$ sudo firewall-cmd --list-all

Whitelisting an IP address

To allow a single IP address across the firewall, use:

$ sudo firewall-cmd --add-source=192.168.1.15 --permanent

To allow an entire subnet, use:

$ sudo firewall-cmd --add-source=192.168.1.0/24 --permanent 

Removing a Whitelisted IP address

To remove a whitelisted IP, use:

$ sudo firewall-cmd --remove-source=192.168.1.15 --permanent

To remove an entire subnet, use:

$ sudo firewall-cmd --remove-source=192.168.1.0/24 --permanent

Blocking an IP address

To block an IP address, use:

$ sudo firewall-cmd --add-rich-rule="rule family='ipv4' source address='192.168.1.15' reject" --permanent 

To block an entire subnet, use:

$ sudo firewall-cmd --add-rich-rule="rule family='ipv4' source address='192.168.1.0/24' reject" --permanent

Applying the New Firewall Rules

For the new firewall rules to be applied immediately, use:

$ sudo firewall-cmd --reload

Viewing the Firewall Rules

To view all the firewalld rules, use:

$ sudo firewall-cmd --list-all

Important

● Always ensure that you reload the firewall for the new rules to take effect.

◆◆◆

Chapter IX: Manage users and groups

Chapter IX Contents

● IX.I Create, delete, and modify local user accounts

● IX.II Change passwords and adjust password aging for local user accounts

● IX.III Create, delete, and modify local groups and group memberships

● IX.IV Configure superuser access

◆◆◆

IX.I Create, delete, and modify local user accounts

What is a multi-user system? 

Linux is a multi-user system. An operating system is considered a "multi-user" if it allows multiple people to use a computer and does not affect each other's "items" such as files and preferences.

To show information about the currently logged-in user, run:

$ id

To show information about a specified user, you can use the syntax:

$ id username

To view the user associated with a file or directory, use:

$ ls -l

By default, systems use the /etc/passwd file to store information about local users. 

To show the content of the /etc/passwd file, use:

# cat /etc/passwd

The format of /etc/passwd follows (seven colon-separated fields):

1\. Username: is a mapping of a UID to a name.  

2\. Password: is where passwords were kept in an encrypted format. Now, they are stored in the /etc/shadow file.   

3\. UID: is the user ID.  

4\. GID: is the user's primary group ID number.   

5\. GECOS: is usually used to record general information about the account or the user such as the user's real name and phone number.  

6\. /home/dir: is the path to the user's home directory.  

7\. Shell: is the path to the shell used.

Managing Local User Accounts 

Manage users via command line tools

When managing users via the command line, the following commands are used: useradd, usermod, userdel, or passwd. Affected files include /etc/passwd, which stores user account information, and /etc/shadow, which stores secure user account information.

Create a Local User Account

You can use the useradd utility to create a new user and add him to the system using the syntax:

# useradd [option(s)] username

Note that

● To display the basic options that can be used with the useradd command to override the defaults, use:

$ useradd --help

● In most cases, the same options can be used with the usermod command to modify an existing user:

$ usermod --help

The /etc/login.defs file

Some of the default settings, such as the default password aging rules and the range of valid UID numbers, are read from the /etc/login.defs file.

To view the /etc/login.defs, use:

$ less /etc/login.defs

Note that

● The values in this file are used only when creating new users. Any change to this file will not affect any existing users.

Procedure

1\. To create a standard user account with its UID, automatically create a home directory (/home/username/) where default user settings will be stored, and set the default shell to /bin/bash, run the following command at a shell prompt as root:

# useradd username

2\. To unlock the account to make it accessible, you need to set the new user password by using the command:

# passwd username

To view created user line in the /etc/shadow file, use:

# grep username /etc/shadow

Note that

● Any UID below 1000 is reserved for system accounts, services, and other special accounts.

● Regular users' UIDs and GIDs are above 1000.

● The root account has the overwhelming privilege of having UID = 0 and GID = 0.

● A User Private Group with the same name as the created user is automatically created.

● The GID of the User Private Group is equal to the UID.

Some common useradd command options include: 

The -c option

When creating a local user, you can use the -c option followed by a string to add a comment (e.g., the user's full name) to the user by using the syntax:

# useradd -c "string" username

The -d option

When creating a local user, you can use the -d option to set a different home directory for the user than the default (/home/username/) by using the syntax:

# useradd -d home_directory

The -M option

When creating a local user, you can use the -M option if you do not want to create the home directory for the user by using the syntax:

# useradd -M username

The -m and -k options

When creating a local user, you can use the -m and -k options to copy the content of a directory to the created user's home directory. 

# useradd -m -k directory username

The -e option

When creating a local user, you can use the -e option if you need to create an account for a certain time and disable it on a certain date by using the syntax:

# useradd -e YYYY-MM-DD username

The -s option

When creating a local user, you can use the -s option if you want to change the user's default login shell (e.g., ksh, csh, tsh) by using the syntax:

# useradd -s login_shell username

Also, you can use the -s option to create a local user account without login access by using the syntax:

# useradd -s /sbin/nologin username

Note that

● A user without a login shell cannot log into the system and therefore cannot run any command interactively on the system.

● Make sure there is a nologin shell in the /etc/shells file.

The -u and -g option

You can use the -u and -g options to create a local user account with a given UID and GID by using the syntax:

# useradd -u UID -g GID username

The -G option

You can use the -G option to create a local user account with a secondary group by using the syntax:

# useradd -G secondary_group_name user_name

Modify a Local User Account

The usermod command

You can use the usermod command to modify any attributes of an already-created user account via the command line.

Note that

● Only the superuser (root) is allowed to execute the usermod command.

● To display the basic options that can be used with the usermod command to modify existing account attributes, use: $ usermod --help

Some common usermod command options include: 

The -l option

You can use the -l option to change the name of a local user account by using the syntax:

# usermod -l new_username old_username

The -G option

You can use the -G option to add the user to supplementary groups by using the syntax:

# usermod -G supplementary_group username

The -a option

You can use the -a option with the -G option to append the user to the supplemental groups mentioned without removing the user from other groups by using the syntax:

# usermod -aG supplementary_group username

The -e option

You can use the -e option for setting the user account expiry date by using the syntax:

# usermod -e YYYY-MM-DD username

To confirm the new expiry date, use the syntax:

# chage -l username

The -L option

You can use the -L option to lock an account by using the syntax:

# usermod -L username 

Delete a Local User Account

The userdel command

You can use the userdel command to delete a user account on the system by using the syntax:

# userdel username

Some common userdel command options include: 

The -r option

You can use the -r option to delete a local user account and remove his home directory and the user’s mail spool by using the syntax:

# userdel -r username

Note that

● The associated group is automatically removed.

The -f option

You can use the -f option to force the deletion of the account by using the syntax:

# userdel -f username

Important user and group account configuration files include:

● /etc/default/useradd: Used to specify the default settings for the useradd command.

● /etc/logins.defs: Shadow password suite configuration.

● /etc/skel: The skeleton of the local user home directory.

● /etc/passwd: User account information.

● /etc/shadow: Secure account information.

● /etc/group: Group account information.

● /etc/gshadow: Secure group account information.

◆◆◆

IX.II Change passwords and adjust password aging for local user accounts

Forcing Linux system password changes with the chage command

The chage command

Since password aging requires users to change their password periodically, you can use the chage command to change the user's password expiration information. The syntax is :

# chage [options] username

Some common chage command options include: 

The -d option

You can use the -d option to force a password update on the next login by using the syntax:

# chage -d 0 username

The -l option

You can use the -l option to list a username's current settings by using the syntax:

# chage -l username

The -E option

You can use the -E option to specify the account expiry date by using the syntax:

# chage -E YYYY-MM-DD 

The -m option

You can use the -m option to specify the minimum number of days between password changes by using the syntax:

# chage -m min_number_of_days username

To verify, use:

# chage -l username

The -M option

You can use the -M option to specify the maximum number of days between password changes by using the syntax:

# chage -M max_number_of_days username

To verify, use:

# chage -l username

Also, you can set the minimum and the maximum number of days in one line by using the syntax:

# chage -m min_number_of_days -M max_number_of_days username

To verify, use:

# chage -l username

The /etc/shadow file format

Password expiration information is stored in the /etc/shadow file. The format of the /etc/shadow file follows (nine colon-separated fields):

$ less /etc/shadow

1\. Name: is the login name and must be a valid account name on the system.   

2\. Password: is the encrypted password. A password field that starts with an exclamation mark means that the password is locked.   

3\. Lastchange: is the date of the last password change, represented as the number of days since 1970.01.01.   

4\. Minage: is the minimum number of days before a password may be changed, where 0 means "no minimum age requirement."   

5\. Maxage: is the maximum number of days before a password must be changed.   

6\. Warning: is the warning period that a password is about to expire. Represented in days, where 0 means "no warning given."   

7\. Inactive: is the number of days an account remains active after a password has expired. A user may still log into the system and change the password during this period. After the specified number of days, the account is locked, becoming inactive.   

8\. Expire: is the account expiration date, represented as the number of days since 1970.01.01.  

9\. Blank: is a blank field that is reserved for future use. 

The /etc/login.defs file

The /etc/login.defs file provides default user account settings. Default values include:

● Location of user mailboxes.

● Password aging controls (PASS_MAX_DAYS, PASS_MIN_DAYS, PASS_MIN_LEN, PASS_WARN_AGE).

● Values for automatic UID selection (UID_MIN, UID_MAX).

● Values for automatic GID selection (GID_MIN, GID_MAX).

● User home directory creation options.

● Umask value.

● The encryption method used to encrypt passwords.

To view the /etc/login.defs file, use:

# cat /etc/login.defs

In RHEL 9, you can set the password minimum length using the following procedure:

1\. Open the /etc/security/pwquality.conf file in the edit mode:

# vim /etc/security/pwquality.conf

2\. Set the minlen value to the required length.

3\. Save and quit.

Note that  

● In RHEL 9, the /etc/login.defs file  [PASS_MIN_LEN, PASS_MAX_LEN, PASS_CHANGE_TRIES, PASS_ALWAYS_WARN] are not supported.

● In RHEL 9, you can use the /etc/security/pwquality.conf  file to set the password minimum length using the minlen  variable.

● The /etc/security/pwquality.conf file provides a way to configure default password quality requirements for system passwords. This file is read by the libpwquality library and utilities that use this library to check and generate passwords.

Restricting access

You can use the usermod command to "lock" an account with the - L option.

The syntax is:

# usermod -L username

To lock and expire an account with a single usermod command. The date must be given as the number of days since January first, 1970:

# usermod -L -e number_of_days username

Or, you can set the date of expiration:

# usermod -L -e YYYY-MM-DD username

◆◆◆

IX.III Create, delete, and modify local groups and group memberships

What is a group in Linux? 

Linux groups are a mechanism for managing a group of users of a computer system. Like users, groups have a name and a number which is a GID. Local groups are specified in the /etc/group file.

To show the groups on your system, use:

$ cat /etc/group

User's Primary Group

Every user has one primary group. For local users, the primary group is a newly created group with the same name as the user and is defined by the GID number which is the same number as the UID, and the user is the only member of this User Private Group (UPG). Normally, the primary group owns new files created by the user. 

Supplementary groups

A user may not be a member of any Supplementary Group and may be a member of one or more Supplementary Groups. Supplementary group membership is used to help ensure that users have access permissions to files and other resources on the system. 

Note that

● The users that are supplementary members of local groups are listed in the last field of the group's entry in the /etc/group file. 

Managing Local Group Accounts 

Managing supplementary groups

A group must exist before a user can be added to that group. 

The groupadd command

Using the groupadd groupname command without options uses the next available GID from the range specified in the /etc/login.defs file:

# groupadd groupname

The -r option

You can use the -r option to create a system group using a GID from the range of valid system GID numbers listed in the /etc/login.defs file by using the syntax:

# groupadd -r groupname

The -g option

You can use the -g option to create a group with a given GID by using the syntax:

# groupadd -g GID groupname

The groupmod command

The groupmod command is used to change a group name to a GID mapping. 

The -n option

You can use the -n option to specify a new name by using the syntax:

# groupmod -n new_groupname old_groupname 

The -g option

You can use the -g option to specify a new GID by using the syntax:

# groupmod -g GID groupname

The usermod command

The membership of a group is controlled with the usermod command.

The -g option

You can use the -g option to change the user's primary group by using the syntax:

# usermod -g groupname username

The -aG options

You can use the -aG options to add a user to a supplementary group by using the syntax:

# usermod - aG groupname username

Note that

● If you didn't use the -a (append) option, you will be removed from all other supplementary groups.

The groupdel command

You can use the groupdel command to remove a group. 

To remove a group by using the syntax: 

# groupdel groupname

Note that

● The group may not be removed if it is the primary group of any existing user.

The gpasswd command

The gpasswd command is used to administer /etc/group, and /etc/gshadow. Every group can have administrators, members, and a password. The syntax is:

# gpasswd [option] groupname

The gpasswd command is called by a group administrator with a group name only to prompt for the new password of the group:

# gpasswd groupname

Note that

● If a password is set, members can still use the newgrp command without a password, and non-members must provide the password.

Some common gpasswd command options include:

The -A option

System administrators can use the -A option to define group administrators by using the syntax:

# gpasswd -A admin_name groupname

The -M option

System administrators can use the -M option to define group members by using the syntax:

# gpasswd -M member_name groupname

Note that

● Except for the -A and -M options, the options cannot be combined:

# gpasswd -A admin_name -M member_name groupname

The -a option

You can use the -a option to add a user to the named group by using the syntax:

# gpasswd -a username groupname

The -d option

You can use the -d option to remove the user from the named group by using the syntax:

# gpasswd -d username groupname

The -r option

You can use the -r option to remove the password from the named group by using the syntax: 

# gpasswd -r groupname

Note that

● Only group members will be allowed to use newgrp to join the named group.

The -R option

You can use the -R option to Restrict access to the named group by using the syntax:

# gpasswd -R groupname

Note that

● Only group members will be allowed to use newgrp to join the named group.

The groups command

The groups command displays group memberships for each username, or, for the current user if no username is specified.

The syntax is:

# groups [option] [username]

The newgrp command

The newgrp command is used to change the current group ID during the login session. If the optional [-] flag is given, the user's environment will be re-initialized as if the user was logged in, otherwise, the current environment, including the current working directory, will remain unchanged. The syntax is:

$ newgrp [-] [groupname]

To verify, use:

$ id

Note that

● You can only change your real group name to a group you are a member of.

◆◆◆

IX.IV Configure superuser access

Gaining Superuser Access

The root user has the power to override normal privileges on the file system and is used to manage and administer the system.

Important

● The root user has unlimited power to damage the system by removing files and directories, user accounts, adding backdoors, and others.

● It is recommended to Log in as a regular user and escalate privileges to root only when needed.

Switching users with the su command 

The su command allows the user to switch to a different user account. If no username is specified, you will be switched to the root account.

To switch to another user account, use the syntax:

$ su username OR # su username

OR

$ su - username OR # su - username

To switch to the root account, use the syntax:

$ su

OR

$ su -

To run a command as root without switching to the root account, use the syntax:

$ su -c ‘command’ 

OR

$ sudo command

Note that

● The main difference between su and su -  is that using su -  initializes the shell environment as if it was a clean login as the user specified while using su only starts the shell as that user with the current environment settings.

● When running the su username  command as root "#", there is no need to enter the account password.

Running commands as root with the sudo command

The sudo command allows the user to be allowed to run the command as root, or as another user, depending on the settings in the /etc/sudoers file.

To view the /etc/sudoers file, run:

# cat /etc/sudoers

The easiest way to give a user sudo access, without modifying the  /etc/sudoers file is to use the usermod command to add the user to the wheel group by using the syntax:

# usermod -aG wheel username

OR

# usermod -aG wheel user_id

To verify that the user is in the group wheel, use:

$ id username

What enables all users in the group wheel to run any command with sudo is that the default /etc/sudoers file contains the following line:

%wheel ALL=(ALL) ALL

Note that

● sudo requires users to enter their password for authentication, not the root password.

● Commands executed using sudo are logged by default to the  /var/log/secure  file.

● When using sudo, every 5 minutes, you need to type the password once.

Using sudo without a password

You can also configure  sudo to not require a password to verify your identity (not recommended).

To do this, you need to edit the /etc/sudoers file as root.

Procedure

1\. Run visudo as root to edit the /etc/sudoers  file:

# visudo /etc/sudoers

Note that

● The advantage of using visudo is that it will validate the changes to the file.

2\. The default /etc/sudoers file contains two lines for the group wheel. Uncomment the NOPASSWD line and comment out the wheel line without NOPASSWD. After making the changes, it should look like this:

# %wheel ALL=(ALL) ALL

%wheel ALL=(ALL) NOPASSWD: ALL

3\. Save and quit.

Enable sudo during system installation

During the installation of the RHEL system, you can enable sudo for the user that you created during the installation. By checking the "Make this user an administrator" checkbox on the user creation screen where you enter the username and password. This will make this user a member of the wheel group during installation.

Reset the root password on RHEL

In case you forgot the root password on RHEL 8 or 9, you can use the following procedure to reset the root password to access the system.

Reset the root password on RHEL 8

Procedure

1\. Restart the system.

2\. Catch the grub screen.

3\. Stop on your kernel line and press “E” to enter the editing mode.

4\. Find the line that starts with Linux and at the end of that line replace 

“rhgb quiet” with “rd.break”

5\. Press Ctrl+X to enter the emergency mode.

6\. To remount the file system as writable, use:

# mount –o remount,rw /sysroot

7\. To Enter the chroot environment, use:

# chroot /sysroot

8\. To reset the root password, use:

# passwd

9\. To enable the SELinux relabeling process on the following system boot, use:

# touch /.autorelabel

10\. To exit the chroot environment and the maintenance mode press Ctrl+D twice.

Reset the root password on RHEL 9

Procedure

You can use either of the following methods:

First method

1\. Reboot the system.

2\. On the GRUB 2 boot screen, use the down arrow key on your keyboard to select the rescue mode.

3\. Press the E key to interrupt the boot process.

4\. Go to the end of the line that starts with linux by pressing Ctrl+E.

5\. Add rd.break to the end of the line that starts with linux.

6\. Press Ctrl+X to start the system with the changed parameters.

7\. Press the Enter key to enter maintenance mode and the sh-5.1# prompt will appear.

8. To remount the file system as writable, use:

# mount -o remount,rw /sysroot

9\. To Enter the chroot environment, use:

# chroot /sysroot

10\. To reset the root password, use:

# passwd

11\. To enable the SELinux relabeling process on the following system boot, use:

# touch /.autorelabel

12\. To exit the chroot environment and the maintenance mode press Ctrl+D twice.

Second method

1\. Reboot the system

2\. On the GRUB 2 boot screen, use the down arrow key on your keyboard to select the rescue mode.

3\. Press the E key to interrupt the boot process.

4\. Go to the end of the line that starts with linux by pressing Ctrl+E.

5\. Add init=/bin/bash to the end of the line starting with linux, and change ro to rw.

6\. Press Ctrl+X to start the system with the changed parameters.

7\. To reset the root password, use:

# passwd

8\. To enable the SELinux relabeling process on the following system boot, use:

# touch /.autorelabel

9\. To execute the /sbin/init program to coordinate the rest of the boot process and configure the environment, use:

# exec /sbin/init

Disallowing Root Access

As an administrator, you have different methods to choose from to disallow root login. Let's take a look at some of them.

1\. Changing the root shell

As a system administrator, you can prevent users from logging in directly as root by setting the root account's shell to /sbin/nologin in the /etc/passwd file.

Warning

● Programs that do not require a shell are not prevented from accessing the root account (e.g., sudo, FTP clients, Email clients).

2\. Disabling root access using any console device (tty)

As an administrator, you can disable root logins in the console by editing the /etc/securetty file. This file lists all devices that the root user is allowed to log into. If the file does not exist at all, the root user can log in through any communication device on the system. This is dangerous because the user can log into their machine as root using Telnet, which transmits the password in plain text over the network.

To prevent the root user from logging in, remove the contents of this file using the following command as root:

# echo > /etc/securetty

Warning

● A blank /etc/securetty file does not prevent the following programs from accessing the root account: [su, sudo, ssh, scp, sftp]

3\. Disabling root SSH logins

To prevent root logins via SSH, use the following procedure:

1\. Open the /etc/ssh/sshd_config file in edit mode:

# vim /etc/ssh/sshd_config

2\. Change the following line:

#PermitRootLogin yes

to be as follows:

PermitRootLogin no

3\. Save and quit.

4\. Restart the sshd service:

# systemctl restart sshd

Warning

● Preventing root access using the OpenSSH suite of tools does not prevent the following programs from accessing the root account: [ssh, scp, sftp]

◆◆◆

Chapter X Contents

● X.II Create and use file access control lists

● X.III Configure key-based authentication for SSH

● X.IV Set enforcing and permissive modes for SELinux
